\title{\color{navy}\bfseries Lexicographic Orders of Well-Orderings\\
of the Surreal Numbers\\[5pt]
\large Set approximations, the singular birthday transition, set-length words,\\
class orders, and higher-order structure}
\author{Research report merged from four independent manuscripts\\
prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov (sources 08, 09, 11 and 12 of batch 80)}
\date{Sources dated 2 October 2026 (source 11: 3 October 2026, UTC); merged 2 October 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{empty}
\begin{abstract}

% ---- source 11, lines 82-113
The phrase \emph{all well-orders of the surreal numbers} has several
inequivalent meanings. Well-orders of a fixed birthday fragment form a set;
well-orders of arbitrary set-sized subsets form an ordinary class;
well-orders of the whole surreal line are themselves classes, and their
totality belongs to a higher logical level. We develop these settings and
distinguish set-like from unrestricted class well-orders.

For an infinite set-sized alphabet \(X\), the existing ProveIt theory gives
\(\card{\WO(X)}=2^{\card X}\) and the exact ordinal embedding spectrum
\(\beta<(\card X)^+\). We apply it to surreal birthday fragments. For
arbitrary set-length surreal words, we give an explicit sign-sequence
embedding into \(\No\), with birthday bounds, and identify the
one-sided cuts created by limit-length words.

For the whole surreal line, we define lexicographic comparison directly on
labeled class well-orders, without assuming ordinal enumerations or a class
of class order types. Every such well-order has a canonical initial segment
of type \(\Ord\); its remaining tail determines a convex fiber. In the
set-like fragment, bounded-support permutations of one global enumeration
form an ordinary class isomorphic to \(\No\), and interpolate every
uniformly set-indexed strict cut of the entire fragment. The fragment
contains every class linear order, admits binary class-word coding, and
cannot have an exhaustive uniform class of set-codes. The unrestricted
order has adjacent pairs, all arising from finite residual alphabets.
An omitted-range construction exhibits a proper-class cut filled by a
non-set-like well-order. In a full inaccessible-universe interpretation,
the unrestricted order does not embed into its set-like fragment.

The proofs use \(\ZF\), \(\ZFC\), or \(\GBC\), as specified, with
a further uniform nonembedding theorem in \(\KM+\GC\). We give
universe-relative and proof-assistant formulations, and propose further
research on the higher-order structure.

% ---- hand chunk abstract_merge

\merge\ This report merges four manuscripts written independently on
2 October 2026 on the same question and delivered together in batch~80:
source~11 (the base, whose abstract is printed above), and sources~12, 09
and~08. They prove the same core up to four times. Each shared theorem is
printed once; the other sources' versions are recorded in numbered notes,
and their genuinely different arguments are kept as marked routes. Source~12
adds an exact dichotomy at birthday cutoffs: for an infinite cardinal
$\kappa$ and $\mu=2^{<\kappa}$, the minimal-length enumeration order
$\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ is equimorphic with the binary cube
$\B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa}$ (ordinal product) exactly when $\kappa$ is a singular
strong limit, and with $\B_\mu$ otherwise, with $\kappa=\beth_\omega$ as an
unconditional example. It also adds a second direct comparison of all class
well-orders, by their largest common labelled prefix, and an exact external
identification of both global orders in a full-class model at an inaccessible
cardinal. Source~09 adds bounded reflection of set-indexed families, an
explicit unfilled $(\Ord,\Ord)$ configuration, and the embeddability in $\No$
of every uniformly class-coded family. Source~08 adds a separator of exactly
prescribed birthday, the failure of finite support, and relation-code
lexicography of all class well-orders. Source~11's choiceless proof answers
source~12's question on the weak-choice strength; the direct comparisons of
sources~11 and~12 answer source~08's question on comparing non-set-like class
well-orders; and source~12's dichotomy answers in part a question of the
repository's report on the reals. The whole set-sized general-alphabet layer
re-proves that report and is printed with pointers to it, never as new.
\end{abstract}

The two pairs of archives sharing a name (08 and~09, 11 and~12) are not
editions of one another. Every pair of the four texts has word 8-gram overlap
of 0.6--1.1\,\% (preamble boilerplate), no member file is shared, the pins
differ within each pair, and the result sets differ. They are four
independent manuscripts on one spine; none supersedes another, and all four
are merged (union).

Source~11 is the base. Its global-choice equivalence is proved over $\GB$
without set choice, the weakest base theory of the four
(\cref{swo:cf:choice}); it covers set alphabets, set-length words, and
set-like and unrestricted class well-orders; and it alone attributes the
general set-alphabet theorems to the repository's report on the reals,
\path{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/lexicographic-well-orderings-of-reals/}
(cited below as the reals report, labels \code{lwo:}).

\subsection{How the four texts are printed}\label{swo:sub:printing}

\merge\ Source~11's text is printed in full and in its own order. The other
sources' material is inserted where it belongs.
\begin{itemize}
\item Their introductions (\cref{swo:sec:intros}) and foundational sections
are printed whole, as sections headed ``Source~$N$''.
\item A result that a printed result already proves by the same argument is
recorded in a numbered \emph{Note}: the note gives the source's statement,
its number in that source, and what differs (hypotheses, strength, extra
remarks). Prose of the source that makes a point not made elsewhere is kept
in the note.
\item A genuinely different proof is printed in full as a marked route
(``Source 12's route'', ``second proof''). A result proved only by one
source is printed in full where it belongs, with its source named in the
heading or the first sentence.
\item Every label of every source survives, with the source's prefix
(\code{swo:} for~11, \code{swo:st:} for~12, \code{swo:ec:} for~09,
\code{swo:sk:} for~08). Numbered statements without a label in their source
carry \code{swo:}\emph{sub}\code{:n}\emph{number}, for example
\code{swo:st:n10.4} for source~12's Corollary~10.4. \Cref{swo:app:crosswalk}
maps every numbered statement of the four sources to its place here.
\item Text written for the merge is marked \merge. Dated notes of
2~October 2026 record where one source answers another
(\cref{swo:sec:answers}).
\end{itemize}
Statements and sections are numbered by their place in this report; source
numbers appear in headings, notes and \cref{swo:app:crosswalk}. Apart from
the renamings of \cref{swo:sec:notation} and the corrections listed in
\cref{swo:sub:choices}, the sources' text is verbatim. Statements of the
reals report and of other repository reports are cited by label name, as
the sources do; those reports are not reproduced.

% ---- hand chunk notation
\subsection{One notation for four sources}\label{swo:sec:notation}

\merge\ The sources' symbols collide. The following convention is fixed
before any of them is used; every renaming is listed, and the tempting false
reading is printed where confusion is likely.

\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.70\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Here & Meaning, and the sources' symbols\\
\midrule\endhead
$\GB$, $\GBC$ &
$\GB$ has a $\ZF$ set part, elementary class comprehension and class
replacement, and \emph{no} choice principle, as in the foundations report
(\code{found:sub:gbconvention}) and the collection's notation guide;
$\GBC=\GB+\GC$. Sources~09 and~12 analyse the necessity of choice ``over GB
plus set-level AC'' (09, \cref{swo:ec:prop:baseline-choice}; 12,
\cref{swo:st:thm:choice}); that theory is written $\GB+\mathrm{AC}$ in the
merge text. Source~11 works over $\GB$ itself. \emph{False reading:} the
letters GB in sources~09 and~12 do not include set choice here.\\[3pt]
$\bd(x)$ &
Birthday, the length of the sign sequence (08, 09; the foundations and
birthday-cutoff reports). Source~11's $\ell(x)$ and source~12's $b(s)$ are
renamed $\bd(x)$, $\bd(s)$. In sources~08 and~09 the letter $b$ is the
baseline enumeration, never a birthday.\\[3pt]
$\No_{<\kappa}$ &
$\{x\in\No:\bd(x)<\kappa\}$, the birthday cutoff (foundations report
$\No_{<\alpha}$). Renamed: source~08's $X_\lambda$, $X_\kappa$; source~09's
$X_\theta$ (its $\kappa_\theta=\card{\No_{<\theta}}$); source~11's $X_\kappa$;
source~12's $S_\kappa$. The reals report writes $S_{<\theta}$.
\emph{False reading:} source~12's $S_\kappa$ is $\No_{<\kappa}=S_{<\kappa}$,
not $S_{<\kappa^+}$.\\[3pt]
$\bl(L)$ &
Binary coding length $\min\{\gamma:L\emb\B_\gamma\}$, as in the reals report
(\code{lwo:def:binary-length}). Source~12 prints the same symbol $\bl$ and
calls it the ``binary coding rank'' (its macro was named \verb|\rk|); its
prose is kept. $\rk(x)$ is the von Neumann rank of a set (source~11's
\verb|\rk|), used only in the proof of \cref{swo:cf:choice}. \emph{False
reading:} the ``rank'' of source~12 is $\bl$, not $\rk$.\\[3pt]
$\WO(X)$, $\WO_\alpha(X)$ &
All exhaustive well-order enumerations of a set $X$ (lengths below
$\card X^+$), and those of length exactly $\alpha$, compared at the first
unequal entry in the given order of $X$ (reals report's $\W$, $\W_\lambda$).
The minimal slice is $\WO_{\card X}(X)$. Source~11's $\mathcal P_\lambda(X)$
is renamed $\WO_\lambda(X)$; source~12's script $\mathscr W$ is printed
$\WO$.\\[3pt]
$\Words(X)$, $\Words$, $\WordsAll$ &
$\Words(X)$: injective words of length below $\card X^+$ over a set $X$
(source~11). $\Words=\Words(\No)$: the class of all injective set-length
surreal words, proper prefixes first (source~11's $\mathcal I$; source~08's
$\I(\No)$; source~09's $\mathcal T$, renamed). $\WordsAll$: all set-length
words, not necessarily injective (source~11's $\mathcal T$). \emph{False
reading:} source~09's $\mathcal T$ was the injective class. The reals
report's $\mathcal I$ (injections $\kappa\to\mathbb R$) is unrelated.\\[3pt]
$\Hsl(\No)$, $\Hall(\No)$ &
Set-like global well-orders of $\No$, equivalently class bijections
$\Ord\to\No$; and all class well-orders of $\No$. Both are predicates on
classes (``metanotation''), never classes of classes. Source~12's
$\mathscr E(\No)$, written $\mathrm{SL}(\No)$ in its macros, and its
$\WO_{\mathrm{cl}}(\No)$; source~09's $\mathscr E$; source~08's ``virtual''
$\mathscr E$ are renamed. $\Jinj$ is source~11's collection of class
injections $\Ord\to\No$.\\[3pt]
$\B_\gamma$, $\BOrd$ &
$\B_\gamma=(\two^\gamma,<_\lex)$ (reals report, 08, 12). $\BOrd$: class binary
words on $\Ord$ compared at their least disagreement (source~08's
$\B_{\Ord}$; source~12's $\mathfrak B$, renamed; source~11 writes
$\two^{\Ord}$).\\[3pt]
$\equi$, $\emb$ &
Equimorphism (embeddings both ways; not isomorphism) and order embedding.
Source~11's $\approx_{\mathrm{emb}}$ is printed $\equi$.\\[3pt]
$\Pbd(e_0)$, $\Psupp(b)$ &
The \emph{core}: enumerations that agree with a fixed baseline enumeration
outside a set of positions. Source~11 codes them by normalized permutations
$\sigma:\lambda\to\lambda$ ($\Pbd(e_0)$, \cref{swo:gl:bounded-codes});
sources~09 and~08 by fixed-point-free bijections $\sigma:S\to S$ of a set $S$
of ordinals (source~09's $\mathcal C_b$, source~08's $\mathcal P_b$, both
written $\Psupp(b)$ here; source~08's code carrier $\mathcal P$ is
$\Psupp$, and its power-set $\mathcal P(V_\kappa)$ keeps the letter
$\mathcal P$). The two codings have the same evaluations: restrict the
identity extension of a support code to $\lambda=\sup\{\xi+1:\xi\in S\}$,
and conversely discard fixed points. With the same baseline, every order
statement about one is a statement about the other.\\[3pt]
$e_0$, $B$, $b$ &
The fixed baseline class bijection $\Ord\to\No$ of sources~11, 12, and~09
and~08 respectively.\\[3pt]
$d(F,G)$, $\Delta(E,F)$, $\delta(e,f)$ &
The least ordinal at which two class functions on $\Ord$ differ (sources~11,
09, 08).\\[3pt]
$\eta_{\Ord}$, $\eta_\kappa$ &
The set-cut property, and its version for cuts of fewer than $\kappa$
elements, empty sides allowed (11, \cref{swo:n3.1}; 08,
\cref{swo:sk:def:eta}); source~09 writes ``set-cut property''. The
subscript is a cardinal bound, not an $\aleph$-index.\\[3pt]
Termination &
Sign sequences compare with $-<\text{termination}<+$ (numerical order);
words compare with proper prefixes first. Exhaustive enumerations of one set
are prefix-free, so no convention is needed there.\\[3pt]
(S08.$n$) &
Source~08's displayed equations (1)--(13) keep their numbers as
(S08.1)--(S08.13). (S08.10), an inequality inside a proof recorded in
\cref{swo:sk:thm:orbitcut}, is described there, not displayed.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

% ---- hand chunk notation81 (batch 81, write 1 of 4)
\subsubsection{Addendum of 3 October 2026: notation for sources 13--18}\label{swo:sub:notation81}

\merge\ The six batch-81 sources (\cref{swo:sub:batch81}) reuse the symbols
above with other meanings, and several reuse this report's macro names for
other objects. The convention below is fixed before any of their material is
printed; it applies to Parts~VI--IX and changes nothing in Parts~I--V. Every
renaming is listed; the tempting false reading is printed where confusion is
likely.

\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.70\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Here & Meaning, and the batch-81 sources' symbols\\
\midrule\endhead
$\kappa$, $\mu$, $r$, $q$ &
$\kappa$ is an infinite cardinal used as a birthday cutoff,
$\mu=2^{<\kappa}=\card{\No_{<\kappa}}$, $r=\cf\kappa$ and $q=\cf\mu$, as in
source~17. Renamed: source~18's $\nu$, $\tau$ (printed $r$, $q$; in source~18's
general principle, $\nu$ stays the point character of an abstract order $D$);
source~14's $\tau=\cf\kappa$ (printed $r$); source~15's $\delta=\cf\kappa$ in its
saturation section (printed $r$). Source~16 writes $\kappa$ for the
cardinality of a general alphabet and $\theta$ for the cutoff; in its text
these are printed $\mu$ and $\kappa$. Sources~13--18's $X_\kappa$ and
source~16's $X_\theta$ are printed $\No_{<\kappa}$. \emph{False reading:} $r$ is
a cofinality, never a coding length (next row).\\[3pt]
$X$, $\card X=\mu$ &
A general alphabet: an infinite linearly ordered set, of cardinality $\mu$
(source~18's convention; source~16's $\kappa$).\\[3pt]
$\rho_\kappa$, $\B_\sigma\times_\lex m$ &
Source~15's $r_\kappa$ ($\kappa\cdot\kappa$ at a singular strong-limit cutoff,
$\mu$ otherwise; by \cref{swo:st:thm:transition} it is
$\bl(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa}))$), renamed to keep $r=\cf\kappa$; and source~15's
$D_{\sigma,m}=\B_\sigma\times_{\lex}\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$, the binary cube with a
finite chain of $m$ points as last coordinate, renamed because source~15's
core is $\mathcal D$. Source~18's letter $\rho$ for a regular cardinal is
printed $\theta$, as in source~17.\\[3pt]
$\dens$, $\cell$, $\weight$ &
Density, cellularity (a supremum, not necessarily attained) and weight of an
order topology, as in the reals report; sources~15--18 write $d$, $c$, $w$.
Cellularity is never written $c$. Source~15's $d(a,b)$ (a first-difference
coordinate) and source~18's $d_*=\mu^{<\mu}$ keep their letters.\\[3pt]
$\ci$, characters &
$\ci$ is coinitiality. The left and right characters of a point $x$ are
$\cf(L_{<x})$ and $\ci(L_{>x})$ (sources~17, 18; source~15 writes
$\operatorname{cf}^-(f)$, $\operatorname{ci}^+(f)$). A point character, the
global cofinality of the order, and the character of a gap are three
different invariants (source~18's warning).\\[3pt]
$\GapChar(L)$, $\Reg$ &
The set of characters $(\cf A,\ci B)$ of the gaps $(A,B)$ of $L$, a gap having
nonempty sides with no endpoints (principal cuts and the external ends are
excluded). Source~17's $\Sigma(L)$ and source~18's $\operatorname{GapSpec}(L)$
are printed with the reals report's symbol $\GapChar$; the report
\path{surreal-fields-across-universes} writes $\operatorname{GapSpec}$. $\Reg$
is the class of infinite regular cardinals (source~17's
$\operatorname{Reg}_\infty$; the reals report writes $\Reg(<\kappa)$ for those
below $\kappa$).\\[3pt]
$\eta_\nu$ &
Source~17: for regular $\nu$, every cut $A<B$ with $\card A,\card B<\nu$ is
filled; sources~15 and~18: every cut with $\card A+\card B<\nu$ (resp.\
$\card{A\cup B}<\nu$) is filled, for any infinite cardinal $\nu$. Empty sides
are allowed throughout. For regular $\nu$ the three definitions agree with
this report's $\eta_\kappa$ (\cref{swo:sec:notation}), and every threshold
statement printed concerns the regular cardinal $r$.\\[3pt]
$P$, $[p]$, $C_p$; straddling &
$P=\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ is the minimal slice (sources~17, 18; source~15
writes $L$). For an injective prefix $p$, $[p]$ (source~17) and $C_p$
(sources~16, 18, and source~11 in Part~IV) are the same cylinder. A cylinder
that meets both sides of a cut \emph{straddles} it; this is the word of
sources~16, 17 and~18 and is used in all batch-81 Parts. Sources~13 and~14 say
that a prefix \emph{crosses} a cut, source~15 that it is \emph{persistent};
these words are explained where printed.\\[3pt]
$\WordsAll$, $\Words$, $\WordsInc$ &
All set-length words, injective set-length words, numerically increasing
set-length words. Source~16's $\mathcal T$, $\mathcal T^{\mathrm{inj}}$,
$\mathcal T^{\mathrm{inc}}$, whose macro \verb|\Words| names \emph{all} words
while this report's \verb|\Words| names the injective ones; source~13's
$\mathcal T$, $\mathcal I$ agree with this report. Source~18's centered classes $\mathcal C$, $\mathcal C_{\mathrm{inj}}$,
$\mathcal C_{\mathrm{inc}}$ are printed $\WordsAll_c$, $\Words_c$,
$\WordsInc_c$ (its $\mathcal C$ would clash with $\mathcal C_b$). Source~16's
termination types $T_{\min}$, $T_\kappa$ are printed $\mathbf T_{\min}$,
$\mathbf T_\kappa$. \emph{False reading:} source~16's $\mathcal T$ is not the
injective class.\\[3pt]
$\Hsl(\No)$, $\Hall(\No)$ &
As in \cref{swo:sec:notation}. Renamed: $\mathfrak E$ (sources~13, 14, 16, 17)
for set-like global orders (source~15 already writes
$\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{sl}}$); source~14's $\mathfrak W$ (macro \verb|\Hwell|),
source~16's $\mathfrak H$ and source~17's $\mathfrak W$ (macro \verb|\HWO|) for
all class well-orders (sources~13 and~15 already write
$\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{all}}$). Source~18's macros \verb|\Hsl|, \verb|\Hall|
print script $\mathscr H_{\mathrm{sl}}$, $\mathscr H_{\mathrm{all}}$ there and
$\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{sl}}$, $\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{all}}$ here.\\[3pt]
$\Jinj$ &
Class injections $\Ord\to\No$, as source~11's $\Jinj$ (sources~13, 14, 17 write
$\mathfrak J$ with the macro \verb|\J|; source~16 writes $\mathfrak I$ with
the macro \verb|\I|; source~15's $\mathfrak I$). \emph{False reading:}
source~16's \verb|\I| is not this report's $\I=\mathcal I$, the class of
injective set-length words.\\[3pt]
$\Pbd(b)$, $\Psupp(b)$ &
The core, as in \cref{swo:sec:notation}, in the source's own coding: normalized
permutations $\Pbd(b)$ for sources~15 ($\mathcal D$) and~16 ($\mathcal P_b$);
fixed-point-free support codes $\Psupp(b)$ for sources~13 ($\mathcal D$), 14
($\mathcal P_b$), 17 ($\mathcal C_b$) and~18 ($\mathcal C_b$, macro
\verb|\Pbd|). \emph{False reading:} source~18's \verb|\Pbd| is the support
coding, not this report's $\Pbd(e_0)$; the two codings have the same
evaluations.\\[3pt]
$\mathcal P_{<\kappa}(b)$ &
Source~14's support-cardinality layer: codes whose support has cardinality
less than $\kappa$. \emph{False reading:} it is not $\Pbd$, whose subscript
means bounded (set-sized) support; source~14 itself warns that a cardinality
filtration and an ordinal-height filtration must not share the word
``bounded''.\\[3pt]
$\equi$, $<_\lex$ &
Equimorphism is $\equi$ for all six sources (source~16's
$\simeq_{\mathrm{emb}}$; source~18's macro \verb|\equim| prints
$\rightleftarrows$ there and $\simeq_e$ here). Source~14's macro \verb|\lex|
prints the relation $<_{\mathrm{lex}}$; this report's \verb|\lex| is the
subscript $\mathrm{lex}$, so source~14's relation is printed $<_\lex$.
Source~18's bold $\mathbf B_\gamma$ is printed $\B_\gamma$.\\[3pt]
Cuts of the core &
A normalized class cut $(L,U)$ has a lower side $L$ without greatest element;
the cut of an injective branch $F$ is written $L_F$ (source~16's $L_F$,
source~17's $L_f$; source~14's $D_F$, $D^\pm_p$, $D_{p,H}$ and source~13's
$(L,U)$ are explained where printed).\\[3pt]
$\varepsilon$, $\End$, $\dagger$ &
A formal termination symbol adjoined to the alphabet of a word order; it is
never a surreal number. Source~16's $\varepsilon$ sits at a cut
$L_\varepsilon<\varepsilon<R_\varepsilon$ of $\No$, and its $0^-$, $0^+$ sit
immediately below and above zero; source~14 writes $\End$; source~18's
centered $\dagger$ takes the place of zero, which is then not a letter, and
its $\dagger_-$, $\dagger_+$ sit immediately below and above an available
zero. Source~13's central termination pads a word with zeros, zero again not
being a letter. Source~18 writes $\ell(p)$ for the length of a word $p$ and
$\mathcal J$ for the increasing words, printed $\WordsInc$. \emph{False
reading:} the Conway sign order $-<\End<+$ is not prefix-first dictionary
order.\\[3pt]
(S$NN$.$n$), labels &
Displayed equations of source~$NN$ keep their numbers as (S$NN$.$n$), as for
source~08. Labels: \code{swo:fs:} (13, replacing its \code{swf:}),
\code{swo:tc:} (14), \code{swo:cb:} (15, keeping its internal \code{cce:} and
\code{new:}, since dropping them would merge two of its labels),
\code{swo:ds:} (16), \code{swo:gs:} (17), \code{swo:sh:} (18, keeping its
internal \code{wv:}); unlabelled numbered statements get
\code{swo:}\emph{sub}\code{:n}\emph{number}; labels written for the merge in
Part~VI begin \code{swo:vi:}, in Part~VII \code{swo:vii:}.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

% ---- hand chunk claims
\subsection{What the report claims, and what it does not}\label{swo:sec:claims}

\merge\ The report claims the written proofs printed below, each in the
foundation its statement names ($\ZF$, $\ZFC$, $\GB$, $\GB+\mathrm{AC}$,
$\GBC$, $\KM+\GC$, or $\ZFC$ with a strongly inaccessible cardinal). The
results new against the repository are: the singular transition at birthday
cutoffs (\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}); the choiceless global-choice
equivalence (\cref{swo:cf:choice}); the direct comparison of all class
well-orders (\cref{swo:cf:lex}, \cref{swo:st:thm:fulllex}), its canonical
initial part and convex fibres, and its adjacency criterion
(\cref{swo:cf:core,swo:cf:fibers,swo:cf:adjacency}); the core of
bounded-support perturbations and its interpolation theorem
(\cref{swo:gl:interpolation,swo:gl:pbd-no}); binary-class equimorphism and
the diagonal non-coding theorems (\cref{swo:gl:binary,swo:gl:diagonal,swo:st:thm:diagonal});
omitted ranges (\cref{swo:gl:omitted,swo:gl:raw-cut,swo:ec:thm:class-gap});
bounded reflection (\cref{swo:ec:thm:reflection}); the failure of finite
support (\cref{swo:sk:prop:finitesupport}); relation-code lexicography
(\cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode}); explicit word coding and the word-cut
classification (\cref{swo:sw:code,swo:sw:cutclassification}); and the
universe models and non-embedding theorems
(\cref{swo:thm:universe-nonembedding,swo:thm:km-nonembedding,swo:st:thm:model,swo:ec:thm:inaccessible}).
None of these is claimed to be historically first.

It does \emph{not} claim the following; every limitation stated by any
source is kept.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{No novelty for re-proofs.} The set-sized general-alphabet layer
(prefix-freeness, cardinality $2^{\card X}$, universality, no
$\card X^+$-chain, ordinal and cube spectra, coding length $\card X^+$,
extrema, adjacency, $n!$ blocks, strict cubes) re-proves the reals report for
every infinite linear alphabet and is printed with pointers to it
(\cref{swo:sub:lwo-pointer}). Universality and homogeneity of $\No$ are
classical (Ehrlich \cite{Ehrlich2012}; Hamkins \cite{HamkinsUniversal}); all
four sources say so. The coding of sets behind the choice equivalence is, in
source~09's words, a standard well-founded-coding argument; the connection is
discussed by Hamkins \cite{HamkinsChoice,HamkinsGC}.
\item \emph{Source~11} (its Status paragraph, its delivery README's ``Scope
and verification'' and its audit): no machine verification, no Lean build, no
new Lean file; no bibliographic priority for constructions proved in the
report; research questions are proposed directions, not a certified list of
published open problems; the repository audit was targeted and
repository-wide correctness was not assessed; the finite checks do not
verify transfinite proofs, cardinal arithmetic or class comprehension. Its
README states: ``The mathematical proofs underwent a separate proof review.''
That is the delivery's own statement; no such review is recorded in the
repository, and this report does not rely on it.
\item \emph{Source~12} (title page, its Section~1.2, and
\code{12-singular-RESEARCH\_STATUS.md}, ``What is not claimed''):
AI-assisted, unrefereed; historical priority not claimed; no previously
catalogued open problem is claimed settled, nor that its exact formulations
are new; no new Lean or Rocq verification; the Python checks are not
substitutes for the proofs; the bounded theorem is not a construction of a
full class model at a singular cardinal; the full collection of class
well-orders is not treated as a set or ordinary class at the same
foundational level; no universal semantic decoder for formula codes is
assumed; its nine research questions are proposed continuations; its source
search was not sufficient to establish originality.
\item \emph{Source~09} (title page, its Appendix~A, and
\code{09-core-RESEARCH\_STATUS.md}, ``Important boundaries''): AI-assisted,
not peer-reviewed or Lean-verified, no historical priority; classical
ingredients and repository results are distinguished from its constructions;
no class whose members are proper classes is formed; the global-enumeration
representation covers set-like class well-orders only, and no complete
classification of the relation-code comparison is claimed; the core realizes
uniform set-sized cuts, not all proper-class cuts (an explicit counterexample
is supplied); for fixed alphabets the absence of $\kappa^+$ and its reverse
is not claimed to be a sufficient embeddability criterion; automorphisms and
higher-level cuts are not classified; its research directions are proposals;
literature and repository searches were targeted, not exhaustive.
\item \emph{Source~08} (its Status box and delivery README): explicit
derivations, not a claim of historical priority; not independently refereed
and not checked in Lean; no new Lean formalization; repository declarations
are separated from proposed extensions; repository sources were read but not
rebuilt; the finite tests do not establish any transfinite or
class-theoretic theorem; every use of ``class'' is relative to the stated
foundational model.
\item \emph{Proposed Lean modules are proposals.} The module names in the
Lean plans of \cref{swo:sec:formalization,swo:st:sec:formalization,swo:ec:sec:formalization,swo:sk:sec:lean}
do not exist in the repository; the existing declarations they cite do
(\cref{swo:sec:formal}).
\item \emph{Review status.} The repository's Hilbert's-tenth research tree
holds a written-proof review of the four archives
(\path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Papers/research-wip/native-stream-queue/review_batch80_surreal.md},
commit \code{c338541e3}). It found their principal arguments sound under the
stated foundations, with one scoped correction to source~08, which is applied
here (\cref{swo:sk:sec:foundations}), and it reran the four finite checks.
A second note there authenticates the placement
(\path{review_surreal_placement_ccc046989.md}, commit \code{3aa123856}). That
review is pinned to the delivered archives and does not cover the text
written for this merge; it is not an entry of the surreal collection's review
record, where this report's review is pending.
(\merge\ Added 3 October 2026: the same tree has since reviewed the merged
text as well. Its synthesis review
(\path{review_surreal_synthesis_0be9b9134.md}, commit \code{280583d2f}) read
every passage written for the merge and found no mathematical defect under
the stated hypotheses, but two overstatements, both corrected here in
place: the choice recursion of \cref{swo:cf:choice} had been called a
``coherent'' sequence of well-orders in \cref{swo:rem:answered-choice} and
\cref{swo:st:n14.5}, and the README had understated the largest delivered
file. Its preservation audit
(\path{review_surreal_transfer_0be9b9134.md}, commit \code{afb706742})
accounts for all 166 formal statements of the four sources (124 printed
with normalized-exact bodies, 41 as duplicate-result notes, one question
inside an answered remark). Neither is an entry of the collection's review
record either.)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Formal status}\label{swo:sec:formal}

\merge\ No statement of this report is formalized, and no \code{swo:} label
has a Lean mapping in the collection's formalization ledger
(\path{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/FORMALIZATION.md}). Placement in the
surreal collection, beside the Lean foundations of
\path{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/Surreal/Foundations/}, confers no formal status.

Four inputs that the sources use \emph{are} proved in Lean, in the
universe-relative form recorded in that ledger, where a ``set'' of surreals is
a family indexed by a \code{Small.\{u\}} type and the sign carrier lives in
\code{Type (u+1)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item properness and bounded birthdays (\code{found:prop:proper};
\cref{swo:sk:prop:size}, first three clauses): \code{SignSequence},
\code{birthdays\_bounded}, \code{small\_bounded}, \code{not\_small} in
\path{SignSequence.lean};
\item filling of small cuts (\code{found:sub:cutdata}):
\code{small\_cut\_fillers} in \path{SignSequenceCut.lean} and
\code{exists\_separator\_of\_small\_sets} in
\path{SignSequenceCutOperation.lean};
\item a separator of exactly a prescribed birthday above all option
birthdays, \cref{swo:sk:thm:cut}: \code{exists\_cut\_separator\_of\_birthday\_lt}
in \path{SignSequenceCut.lean}, by the same route as source~08's proof
(the complete lexicographic lattice of Boolean words of one length, which is
\cref{swo:sk:lem:binarycomplete}, there taken from Mathlib);
\item the inconsistency of unrestricted cuts (\code{found:prop:allcuts}):
\code{noUnrestrictedCuts} in \path{SizeObstructions.lean}.
\end{itemize}
The other declarations the sources cite exist at the current revision:

% ---- hand chunk answers
\subsection{Where one source answers another}\label{swo:sec:answers}

\merge\ Dated notes, 2 October 2026.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Source~12's question on the weak-choice strength}
(\cref{swo:st:n14.5}, and its \cref{swo:st:n4.2}): answered by source~11.
Over $\GB$ without set choice, a class well-order of $\No$ exists exactly when
global choice holds (\cref{swo:cf:choice}); see \cref{swo:rem:answered-choice}.
The same theorem shows that the set-choice hypothesis of source~09's
\cref{swo:ec:prop:baseline-choice} is unnecessary. Source~09's direction on
the exact axiomatic strength of \emph{other} universality and homogeneity
assertions (\cref{swo:ec:sec:research}) is unaffected.
\item \emph{Source~08's question~11, comparison of non-set-like class
well-orders} (\cref{swo:sk:sec:research}): answered by sources~11 and~12.
\Cref{swo:cf:lex,swo:st:thm:fulllex} compare all class well-orders of a
labelled class directly, with elementary comprehension, without $\ETR$ and
without comparing abstract class order types; this also supplies the single
comparison that source~08's \cref{swo:sk:sec:nonsetlike} says does not follow
by aligning domains (see the note after it). Source~09's relation-code
direction concerns a different comparison and stays open.
\item \emph{Source~08's question~3, singular birthdays and code lengths}:
answered in part by source~12's \cref{swo:st:thm:transition}: at a singular
cutoff the least binary coding length of the fixed-length enumeration order is
$\kappa\cdot\kappa$ when $\kappa$ is a strong limit and $2^{<\kappa}$
otherwise. Which cuts fail at singular cutoffs stays open.
\item \emph{Source~08's question~2, invariants at regular cutoffs}: partly
answered by source~11's \cref{swo:sw:permutationeta} (the minimal slice is
$\eta_\kappa$, with every global and local cofinality and coinitiality
$\kappa$); density, cellularity and the cut cofinalities beyond these stay
open.
\item \emph{The reals report's question ``Other ground orders''}
(its Section ``Further research questions'', \code{lwo:sec:research}, which
names ``set-sized suborders of surreal numbers''): answered in part by
\cref{swo:st:thm:transition} for $X=\No_{<\kappa}$ and the minimal stratum
($n=0$): $\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\equi\B_\mu$, except at singular strong-limit
$\kappa$, where it is $\equi\B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa}$ and not $\equi\B_\kappa$.
It stays open for $n>0$, for longer strata, and for other alphabets
(source~12's \cref{swo:st:n14.1}, source~08's question~4).
\item \emph{Hamkins's question whether universality of the surreal line is a
weak global choice principle} \cite{HamkinsUnivChoice}: not settled here, as
source~12 says after its \cref{swo:st:n14.5}.
\end{enumerate}

The six are independent texts, not editions of one another. Every pair shares
0.6--1.4\,\% of word 8-grams (preamble, this report's title and address,
bibliography), no member file is shared, and the theorem sets differ.
Sources~14 and~17 share only the inner directory name
\code{surreal\_well\_orders\_II} and a ``II'' in the title; source~18's
archive name extends source~15's, but its pin, structure and results differ.
None supersedes another; all six are merged (union). Sources~13--17 read this
report at commit \code{0be9b9134}, before the corrections of \code{0f52c0dca};
source~18 read it at \code{83befe707}, after them, and before sources~13--17
arrived, so it never saw them.

They overlap heavily with each other and with this report. The
represented-cut theorem that answers source~11's \cref{swo:n16.1} is proved
in all six by one argument; all six re-prove the core layer of Part~IV
(fresh separators, completion, interpolation, the core isomorphic to $\No$,
the diagonal theorem, direct comparison); and the set-sized layer re-proves
the reals report. What is new is a second wave of results on questions of
this report: the topology, cut spectra and coding lengths of the set-sized
strata at birthday cutoffs; the termination classification of word orders;
the trichotomy, characters, automorphism orbits, absoluteness and support
thresholds for cuts of the set-coded core; the canonical block decomposition
of class well-orders with explicit condensation histories; product
(non)absorption; a $\Sigma^1_1$ comprehension bound; and the countable models.

The addition is printed by theme, not by source, because the sources overlap:
Part~VI (\cref{swo:part:strata}) prints the set-sized strata; Part~VII
(\cref{swo:part:conventions}) prints the six sources' maps of interpretations,
the word orders, the termination conventions and the other comparison orders.
The rules of
\cref{swo:sub:printing} apply unchanged: each shared result is printed once,
with every source that proves it named; the other versions are numbered
notes, genuinely different proofs are marked routes, and every label of every
source survives with the prefixes of \cref{swo:sub:notation81}. Re-proofs of
results of this report or of other repository reports are printed as notes
with pointers, never with a novelty claim.

\emph{Status.} Sources~13, 14, 15 and~17 call themselves AI-assisted and
unrefereed; source~18's validation ``consists of conventional proofs and
independent AI-assisted proof review''; source~16 says its checks are ``not
external peer review''. None claims historical priority, peer review or a
machine-checked formalization, and none is formalized here. The status files
of sources~13, 14, 16 and~17 are shipped in this directory under their
prefixed names.

% ---- source 11, lines 127-189
\section{The objects being studied}\label{swo:sec:objects}

There are two orders in this problem. Each object under consideration is
itself a well-order of some collection of surreal numbers. We then compare
these objects lexicographically using the \emph{numerical} order on their
surreal labels. This second order need not be a well-order. Indeed, the
orders below contain abundant descending sequences.

Write \(L\emb M\) for an order embedding and \(L\equi M\) for embeddings
in both directions. Equimorphism does not imply isomorphism.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.41\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Object & Elements & Logical size and interpretation\\
\midrule
\(\WO(X)\) & Ordinal-indexed bijections onto a fixed set \(X\subseteq\No\)
& A set; ordinary \(\ZFC\) order theory\\[4pt]
\(\Words(\No)\) & Injective set-length ordinal words in \(\No\)
& A class of sets; well-orders of arbitrary set-sized subsets\\[4pt]
\(\Hsl(\No)\) & Set-like class well-orders of all \(\No\), equivalently
class bijections \(\Ord\to\No\)
& A higher-order totality; use class variables or uniform families\\[4pt]
\(\Hall(\No)\) & All class well-orders of all \(\No\), including
non-set-like ones
& A higher-order totality; direct labeled comparison is needed\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The fraktur notation is deliberate. It does not declare a class with
proper classes as members. Statements about \(\Hsl\) and \(\Hall\)
are formulas with class variables, or statements relative to a specified
larger universe. An embedding of an ordinary class order into one of these
totalities is a uniform family: one class relation encodes the object
assigned to each set-valued index.

\paragraph{Principal answers.}
For a fixed infinite alphabet \(X\), the exact ordinal ceiling is
\((\card X)^+\). Passing to all set-length surreal words removes that
ceiling: there is an explicit embedding of the entire class into
\(\No\), and the singleton words embed \(\No\) in the other direction.
The two orders are nevertheless not isomorphic, because limit-length
words have set-sized left cofinality.

Under global choice, the set-like global fragment contains a uniformly
indexed copy of every class linear order. This includes every set ordinal,
\(\Ord\), reverse \(\Ord\), \(\Ord+\Ord\), and the whole numerical
surreal order. Yet the entire fragment cannot be exhausted by one uniform
class of set-codes. Class universality of a subfamily is compatible with
a larger totality of class objects.

The unrestricted global order is different again. It has adjacent pairs
and convex residual fibers. Every raw well-order begins with a canonical
\(\Ord\)-sequence; if that sequence omits labels, those labels form a
later well-ordered tail. This decomposition permits a precise study of
\emph{all} well-orders without treating them all as set-like.
It also supplies a nonembedding obstruction: the unrestricted order has
too many disjoint two-point fibers to embed into the set-like order in
the full containing-universe model. A related uniform definability
obstruction is provable in \(\KM+\GC\).


% ---- source 11, lines 190-249
\section{Relationship to ProveIt and the literature}\label{swo:sec:sources}

We inspected the repository at the immutable revision
\[
 \texttt{ae7c1e6aae3dc58c9c638b35646865ad6c10387d}.
\]
The closest precursor is the report on lexicographic well-orderings of
the reals \cite{ProveItReals}, in the subdirectory
\path{lexicographic-well-orderings-of-reals/}
of the repository's ordinal-order reports.
Its general alphabet theorems already establish the cardinality,
universality, and ordinal cutoff for every infinite linearly ordered
set \(X\). Their application to birthday fragments is a transfer of
existing results, not a new generic alphabet theorem.

That report also proves, with \(\mathfrak c=2^{\aleph_0}\),
\[
 \No_{<\theta}\emb\WO(\mathbb R)\quad(\theta<\mathfrak c^+),
 \qquad
 \No_{<\mathfrak c^+}\not\emb\WO(\mathbb R).
\]
The negative assertion follows from the all-plus chain of length
\(\mathfrak c^+\). It warns against taking an incoherent union of
stagewise embeddings.

The repository's foundational report \cite{ProveItFoundations} supplies
the relevant size discipline: individual surreals are set-coded, bounded
fragments are sets, arbitrary class cuts need not be fillable, and
quantifying over class functions does not create a class of those
functions. Its local set-valued recursion arguments are directly useful.
A nearby vector-space report uses the same distinction to avoid an
unnecessary \(\ETR\) hypothesis \cite{ProveItVector}.

The sign-sequence presentation is standard \cite{Gonshor,BagayokoHoeven}.
Absolute universality and homogeneity of the surreal order are part of
the established theory discussed by Ehrlich \cite{Ehrlich2012}.
We supply the short recursion arguments needed here.

\paragraph{The Kanovei--Shelah connection.}
There is a methodological connection, but the objects are different.
Kanovei and Shelah's actual index consists of maps
\[
 a:\mathfrak c\longrightarrow\mathcal P(\mathbb N)
\]
whose ranges are ultrafilters. They compare these maps lexicographically
using the binary order on \(\mathcal P(\mathbb N)\)
\cite[Section 1]{KanoveiShelah}. The maps need not be injective and do
not enumerate the entire real line. Their construction illustrates how
a definable linear order on many codes can replace a definably selected
well-order. The present problem shares that idea while introducing a
different level of class-size complexity.

\paragraph{Contribution and attribution.}
The central developments here are explicit surreal-word compression,
direct comparison of unrestricted class well-orders, their canonical
initial-\(\Ord\) decomposition, the bounded-support interpolation
theorem, and the relation between omitted ranges and non-set-like tails.
No claim about real-specific countability, separability, or gap spectra
is transferred to surreal alphabets without proof.

\section{The other sources' introductions}\label{swo:sec:intros}

\merge\ Each of the other manuscripts opens with its own statement of the
problem, of its relation to ProveIt, and of the Kanovei--Shelah connection.
These introductions are printed whole, one subsection each, followed by
notes where the merge corrects or qualifies them. Their summaries of results
are not repeated claims: every result they announce is printed, once, in the

% ---- source 12, lines 149-231
\subsection{Source 12: scope, predecessors, and the results at a glance}
\label{swo:st:sec:scope}

The phrase ``well-orders of surreal numbers'' hides several distinct
objects. The usual numerical order of $\No$ is not a well-order. One may
instead put an unrelated well-order on the entire surreal carrier, or
consider numerical well-orders on subclasses, or work with an ordinary
set of bounded-birthday surreals. This article treats all three viewpoints,
but the principal object is the lexicographic comparison of
\emph{exhaustive well-orders of a fixed carrier}.

The supplied ProveIt repository contains a substantial predecessor,
\emph{Lexicographic Orders of the Well-Orderings of the Reals}, merging
three reports and distinguishing the full enumeration order from its
minimal-length slice \cite{ProveItReals}. The inspected README explicitly
marks that report as unrefereed and not formalized. Its real-number
spectra, binary coding, finite-tail geometry, and warnings about
isomorphism versus equimorphism motivate the present work. The repository
also contains a Lean development of simplest elements of convex sets of
sign sequences \cite{ProveItSimplicity}. These are useful foundations, not a
formal verification of the new theorems below.

We pinned the audit to repository commit
\begin{center}
\small\texttt{63bb8b1d99359e7188abf0a33fb152e121858150}.
\end{center}
The audit was targeted, not an exhaustive review of the repository.
All essential arguments used here are written out, so no unreviewed
research claim from the repository is required as a black box.

\subsubsection{Three different orders}

For an infinite set-sized linear order $X$ of cardinality $\mu$, define
\[
 \WO_\alpha(X)=\{e:\alpha\longrightarrow X:e\text{ is bijective}\},
 \qquad
 \WO(X)=\bigcup_{\mu\leq\alpha<\mu^+}\WO_\alpha(X).
\]
They are ordered by their first unequal entry, using the given order of
$X$, not the well-order being represented.

For the proper class $\No$, let $\Hsl(\No)$ denote, as a \emph{predicate on
classes}, the class bijections $E:\Ord\to\No$. These represent exactly the
set-like global well-orders. Let $\Hall(\No)$ denote the predicate
of being an arbitrary class well-order relation on $\No$.
Neither displayed notation asserts the existence of a class having these
class-sized objects as members.

The comparison on $\Hsl(\No)$ uses a least ordinal of disagreement.
The comparison on $\Hall(\No)$ uses a largest common
\emph{labelled} initial segment. The latter can be a proper class, and
this is essential rather than a cosmetic extension.

\subsubsection{What is proved, and what is not being claimed}

The principal developments are the elementary common-prefix theorem
(\cref{swo:st:thm:fulllex}), the global-choice equivalence
(\cref{swo:st:thm:choice}), the non-enumerability theorem
(\cref{swo:st:thm:diagonal}), and the singular transition
(\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}). The full and minimal bounded spectra are proved
in \cref{swo:st:sec:bounded,swo:st:sec:transition}. The difference between the dense
set-like order and finite-tail geometry in the full order is proved in
\cref{swo:st:sec:setlike,swo:st:sec:fullgeometry}.

These are mathematical claims with supplied proofs. We do not claim
that they settle a previously catalogued open problem, that their exact
formulations are historically new, or that they have been independently
refereed. The proposed research questions at the end are precisely that:
questions suggested by the results, not an assertion of a complete
literature census.

\subsubsection{The Kanovei--Shelah connection}

Kanovei and Shelah order maps
$a:\mathfrak c\to\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$ whose ranges are ultrafilters,
using a fixed lexicographic order on $\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$
\cite[\S1]{KanoveiShelah}. Their maps need not be bijections, and their index is not
the literal collection of all well-orders of $\mathbb R$, still less all
class well-orders of $\No$. The useful common idea is to compare
representations at their first difference instead of choosing one
canonical representative. Their index construction remains set-sized.
Moving to all global surreal well-orders creates an additional size level
that their construction does not remove.

% ---- source 09, lines 136-208
\subsection{Source 09: the question and the principal conclusions}
\label{swo:ec:sec:intro}
A surreal number is a set-sized object, but the totality $\No$ of surreal
numbers is a proper class. Consequently, taking ``all well-orders'' changes
logical level when one passes from $\mathbb R$ to $\No$. The main task is
not merely to replace the real alphabet by a larger alphabet. It is to
specify which objects are being ordered and which collections of those
objects are available in the foundational theory.

Throughout, the word \emph{embedding} means an order-preserving and
order-reflecting injection. It need not preserve cuts, suprema, the order
topology, birthdays, simplicity, or field operations. An order is
\emph{equimorphic} with another when each embeds into the other; this is
weaker than isomorphism.

\subsubsection{Three different carriers}
For a set $X\subseteq\No$, let $\WO(X)$ consist of all bijections
$e:\alpha\to X$, with $\alpha$ an ordinal. These are exactly the exhaustive
enumerations associated with well-orders on the fixed labeled set $X$.
For different $e,f$, comparison takes place at the first unequal value.
Because both are exhaustive, neither can be a proper initial segment of
the other.

Let $\Words$ instead contain every set-length injective word
$p:\alpha\to\No$, allowing its range to vary. Here proper-prefix pairs do
occur, so an end-of-word convention is necessary. We use the convention
that a proper prefix comes first. This is ordinary dictionary order,
\emph{not} the convention used to compare surreal sign sequences.

Finally, let $\Hsl$ be informal notation for the global class bijections
$E:\Ord\to\No$. Each represents a set-like global well-order. In
$\GBC$, $\Hsl$ is notation for a condition on class variables, not a class
whose members are these class functions. A precise, ordinary class
substitute is the set-coded core $\Psupp(b)$ of eventual perturbations of a
fixed $b:\Ord\to\No$.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Main global conclusion.}
In $\GBC$, for every fixed global enumeration $b$,
\[
                 (\Psupp(b),<_{\lex})\cong(\No,<).
\]
The core is order-dense among all global enumerations and even supplies
interpolants for every uniformly set-indexed separated cut involving
arbitrary global enumerations. Nevertheless, there is no uniform
class-indexed enumeration of \emph{all} global enumerations, and certain
proper-class-sized separated families have no interpolant at all.
\end{resultbox}

\subsubsection{How this extends the repository's real-line investigation}
The inspected ProveIt snapshot contains a substantial merged report on
lexicographic well-orderings of the reals \cite{ProveItReals}. That report
studies ordinal spectra, binary coding, finite tails, local characters,
and gaps. Its set-sized perspective is useful here, but its continuum
bounds cannot simply be applied to the entire surreal class. We reprove
the set-sized arguments needed below for an arbitrary linearly ordered
set, without importing unverified transfinite claims from that report.

The genuinely global development is organized around four concrete
constructions: bounded-support extension of an injective prefix;
set-indexed lexicographic interpolation; an eventually-equal surreal
core; and diagonalization at independent pairs of positions. These
constructions are the main contribution of this manuscript. Whether
particular formulations have appeared previously would require a more
exhaustive literature search; they are not presented as established
historical firsts.

Kanovei and Shelah's nonstandard-model construction provides motivation
for lexicographically ordering redundant codes. Their actual index is
not the collection of all real well-orders: it consists of maps from the
continuum cardinal to $\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$ whose ranges are ultrafilters,
ordered at their first disagreement \cite[\S1]{KanoveiShelah}. This distinction
remains essential in any proposed surreal analogue.

% ---- source 08, lines 128-225
\subsection{Source 08: the question, disambiguated}\label{swo:sk:sec:question}

The phrase ``well-orders of surreal numbers'' has two independent
ambiguities. First, is the carrier a set of surreal numbers or the whole
proper class? Second, is a well-order required to be the restriction of
the numerical order, or is it a new ordering of the labelled carrier?
A third choice enters when the well-orders themselves are compared:
which data are read lexicographically?

This paper uses the following principal convention. An arbitrary
well-order on a labelled carrier is represented by its exhaustive
increasing enumeration \emph{in that well-order}. Two enumerations are
then compared at their first unequal entry, using the \emph{numerical}
order of their surreal values. The enumerations themselves need not be
numerically increasing. This distinction is indispensable.

For example, the two lists $(0,1,-1)$ and $(0,-1,1)$ represent two
well-orders of the same three-element set. The second list is smaller in
our lexicographic order, since $-1<1$. Both underlying relations have
ordinal type $3$. Passing to ordinal isomorphism types would identify
them and destroy the object being studied.

\subsubsection{Five different carriers}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{.18\textwidth}Y Y@{}}
\toprule
Object & Elements or parameters & Foundational size \\
\midrule
$\W(X)$ & Exhaustive enumerations of one fixed set $X$ & A set in $\ZFC$ \\
$\I(\No)$ & Injective ordinal-length, set-sized words in $\No$ & A class of sets \\
$\Hsl$ & Class bijections $\Ord\to\No$ & A virtual collection of classes \\
$\Psupp(b)$ & Set codes for set-supported changes of a fixed $b$ & A class of sets \\
Arbitrary class well-orders & Class relations, possibly not set-like & A virtual collection; another comparison rule is needed \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

A \emph{virtual collection} is notation for a condition on class
variables, not a new object whose members are proper classes. Thus
``$e\in\Hsl$'' abbreviates ``$e$ is a class bijection from $\Ord$ onto
$\No$.'' All statements involving several such parameters can be
translated into the two-sorted language of class set theory. Whenever a
family of classes is used, it is represented by one uniform class
relation. This convention is made precise in \cref{swo:sk:sec:global}.

\subsubsection{Main conclusions}

The central distinction is between a very large ambient order and its
class-sized dense skeleton. Fix a global enumeration $b:\Ord\to\No$.
Write $\Psupp(b)$ for canonical set codes for permutations $\sigma$ of
$\Ord$ that equal the identity outside a set, ordered by the numerical
lexicographic comparison of $b\circ\sigma$. We prove
\[
                 (\Psupp(b),<_{b})\cong(\No,<).                 \tag{S08.1}\label{swo:sk:eq:t1}
\]
Moreover, if uniformly given set-sized families $L,R$ of arbitrary global
enumerations satisfy $L<_{\lex}R$, then some $b\circ\sigma$ strictly
separates them. The separator is in the same fixed skeleton regardless
of the supports, or lack of supports, of the input enumerations.

This yields a useful answer to the embedding question. Every
\emph{set} linear order, every ordinal, and indeed every
\emph{class linear order whose elements are sets} embeds into
$\Psupp(b)$. In particular, $\Ord$, $\Ord+1$, $\Ord+\Ord$, their reversals,
$\No$, and each set-indexed lexicographic power of $\No$ embed. This does
not say that the full virtual order $\Hsl$ is itself a class order or is
isomorphic to $\No$.

At a fixed set-sized carrier $X$, the answer is different. If
$|X|=\kappa$ is infinite, then
\[
 |\W(X)|=2^\kappa,
 \qquad
 \beta\emb\W(X)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \beta<\kappa^+.
                                                               \tag{S08.2}\label{swo:sk:eq:t2}
\]
Thus making the carrier larger changes the allowed ordinal spectrum;
there is no legitimate substitution of a ``cardinal $|\No|$'' into \eqref{swo:sk:eq:t2}.

\subsubsection{Relationship to existing work}

The set-sized part extends the exhaustive-enumeration viewpoint of the
prior working manuscript on the continuum \cite{Batch76Source02}. Its results are
reproved here in a form that does not assume separability of the
alphabet. The standard surreal background is the sign-sequence and
cut-filling viewpoint associated with Conway, Gonshor, and Alling
\cite{Conway,Gonshor,Alling}; Ehrlich's work places the surreal continuum
in a broader universality framework \cite{Ehrlich2012}.

The immediate formalization base is the inspected ProveIt sign-sequence
construction and its small-cut theorem \cite{ProveItSigns,ProveItCutFill}. We do not
need the repository's field arithmetic or stronger research claims.
The resemblance to the Kanovei--Shelah definable hyperreal construction
is real but limited: their actual index consists of fixed-length maps
whose ranges are ultrafilters, not literally all well-orders of the
reals \cite{KanoveiShelah}. We return to this distinction in \cref{swo:sk:sec:KS}.

\subsection{Source 08: the Kanovei--Shelah connection, carefully stated}\label{swo:sk:sec:KS}

Kanovei and Shelah's definable nonstandard model uses an index $A$ of
maps
\[
                 a:\mathfrak c\longrightarrow\mathcal P(\mathbb N)
\]
whose ranges are ultrafilters. They compare these maps lexicographically
using the binary characteristic-function order on subsets of
$\mathbb N$. Repetitions are allowed; the index is not a well-order
\cite{KanoveiShelah}. It is therefore not literally the order of all bijective
well-order enumerations of $\mathbb R$.

The relevant common idea is that ordinal-indexed codes can be linearly
compared at their first unequal value even when no definable choice of
one representative from each coded object is supplied. Our paired
codes, terminal codes, and virtual relations explore that order-theoretic
idea in the surreal setting. They do not provide a class ultrapower or
a transfer theorem.

An extension to a class-sized ultrapower would need separate decisions
about admissible predicates, function families, equivalence-class
coding, truth or satisfaction, and the exact size of saturation.
Neither a dense index order nor a surreal-sized skeleton settles these
questions. In particular, the set-cut interpolation theorem is an
order-theoretic theorem, not a claim of saturation for every language
or every class-sized family of types.

sources (source~11 in \cref{swo:sec:sources}, source~12 in
\cref{swo:st:sec:scope}, source~09 in \cref{swo:ec:sec:intro}, source~08
above) and by the reals report itself, which analyses the actual index
(\code{lwo:eq:KS}, \code{lwo:sec:KS-topology}, \code{lwo:rk:sec:KS}). The
uniform constructions it suggests are research questions here
(\cref{swo:n16.10}; source~09's and source~08's last directions).

\section{Surreal order facts and foundational conventions}\label{swo:sec:prelim}

\subsection{Signs, birthdays, and set cuts}

A surreal number is represented by a sign sequence
\[
 s:\alpha\longrightarrow\{-,+\}
\]
of set-ordinal length \(\alpha\), its birthday \(\bd(s)\). Numerical
comparison at the first disagreement uses
\[
 -\ <\ \text{termination}\ <\ +.
\]
This differs from dictionary order with every proper prefix smaller
than every extension. A sign sequence can be on either side of an
extension, depending on its next sign.

For an ordinal \(\theta\), let
\[
 \No_{<\theta}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\theta}\{-,+\}^{\alpha}.
\]
It is a set. The full \(\No\) is proper-class-sized, because all-plus
sequences embed all ordinals. Every set of surreals has bounded birthdays
by Replacement.

\begin{definition}\label{swo:n3.1}
A class linear order \(L\) has the \emph{set-cut property}, called
\(\eta_{\Ord}\) here, if for all sets \(A,B\subseteq L\) with \(A<B\)
there is \(x\) with \(A<x<B\). Empty sides are allowed. For a cardinal
\(\kappa\), the set-sized notation \(\eta_\kappa\) requires the same
property when \(\card{A\cup B}<\kappa\).
\end{definition}

The surreal cut construction supplies this property for \(\No\)
\cite[Section 2]{BagayokoHoeven}. A separator can be chosen canonically
as the unique simplest separator. This statement concerns \emph{sets}
of options; applying it to the whole class against the empty right side
would be inconsistent.

\begin{lemma}[Deleting a set]\label{swo:lem:delete}
If \(S\subseteq\No\) is a set, then \(\No\setminus S\) has the
set-cut property. Every nonempty open interval of \(\No\) is a
proper class.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let \(A<B\) be sets in \(\No\setminus S\). First choose \(x\) with
\(A<x<B\), and put
\[
 S_-=\{s\in S:s\leq x\},\qquad S_+=\{s\in S:x<s\}.
\]
Then \(A\cup S_-<B\cup S_+\). A separator of this new set cut
avoids both parts of \(S\). The argument allows empty sides.
If an interval \((a,b)\) were a set \(S\), applying the result to
\(\{a\}<\{b\}\) in \(\No\setminus S\) would be impossible.
\end{proof}

Choosing both separators by simplicity makes the deletion argument
uniform in the set parameters without global choice.


% ---- hand chunk avoid12_head

\merge\ \emph{Third route to the first clause of \cref{swo:lem:delete}
(source~12, Lemma~6.2).} Source~12 avoids a forbidden set by using that open
intervals of $\No$ are proper classes.

% ---- source 12, lines 580-589
\begin{lemma}[Avoiding a forbidden set]\label[lemma]{swo:st:lem:avoid}
If $A<B$ are sets of surreals and $D$ is a set, then some separator
$z$ of $A,B$ lies outside $D$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $u$ between $A$ and $B$, and then $v$ between $\{u\}$ and $B$.
Every point of $(u,v)$ separates $A,B$, and that interval is a proper
class. It cannot be contained in $D$. With a fixed global well-order,
choose the first acceptable point, making the operation uniform.
\end{proof}

% ---- hand chunk toolkit08_head

\merge\ Source~08 opens with its own toolkit, printed next. Its
\cref{swo:sk:prop:size} restates \code{found:prop:proper} (Lean-proved in
universe-relative form; \cref{swo:sec:formal}) and adds that neither the
numerical order nor ``birthday first'' well-orders $\No$, which source~12
also proves (\cref{swo:st:sec:choice}). Its \cref{swo:sk:thm:cut} sharpens the
birthday bound of the cut theorem to a separator of \emph{exactly} any
prescribed birthday above the option birthdays; this is proved in Lean as
\code{exists\_cut\_separator\_of\_birthday\_lt}, by the same complete-lattice
argument (\cref{swo:sk:lem:binarycomplete}). Its \cref{swo:sk:cor:fresh}
contains the first clause of \cref{swo:lem:delete} with a prescribed
birthday, and its \cref{swo:sk:def:eta} is \cref{swo:n3.1}.

\subsection{Source 08: surreal order and size, the minimal toolkit}\label{swo:sk:sec:toolkit}

We take a surreal number to be a sign sequence
\[
 s:\alpha\longrightarrow\{-,+\},\qquad \alpha\in\Ord.
\]
Its birthday is $\bd(s)=\alpha$. To compare two sequences, extend them
by a termination symbol $0$ and compare at the first disagreement using
$-<0<+$. This is the numerical order. The simplicity relation is proper
initial-segment extension, and is not the numerical order.

\begin{proposition}\label{swo:sk:prop:size}
Every birthday-bounded collection of surreals is a set, every set of
surreals has bounded birthdays, and $\No$ is a proper class. Neither the
numerical order nor ``birthday first, then numerical order'' is a
well-order of $\No$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed ordinal $\lambda$, the sign sequences of length below
$\lambda$ form the set $\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}\{-,+\}^{\alpha}$.
Replacement bounds the birthdays of any given set. Conversely, the
all-plus sequences give a distinct surreal for every ordinal, so a set
containing all surreals would bound all ordinals.
The negative integers already descend numerically. For the second
claim, let $s_n$ have birthday $\omega$, with $n$ initial minus signs
followed by plus signs forever. At coordinate $n$ one has
$s_{n+1}(n)=-<+=s_n(n)$, so
$s_0>s_1>s_2>\cdots$ within a single birthday level.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A birthday-controlled separator theorem}

We record a proof of the exact order-theoretic fact used later. It also
explains the repository's bounded-word implementation \cite{ProveItCutFill}.

\begin{lemma}\label{swo:sk:lem:binarycomplete}
For every set ordinal $\beta$, the binary lexicographic order
$\B_\beta=(\two^\beta,<_{\lex})$ is a complete lattice.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The least and greatest elements are the constant $0$ and $1$ words.
Given a set $A$ of words, form $w$ recursively. At coordinate $\xi$, put
$w(\xi)=1$ exactly when some $a\in A$ agrees with the already constructed
prefix $w\restr\xi$ and has $a(\xi)=1$; otherwise put $0$.
If some $a\in A$ exceeded $w$, its first disagreement would contradict
the rule for a $0$ coordinate. If $v<w$, then at their first
disagreement $\xi$ we have $v(\xi)=0$, $w(\xi)=1$, and a witness
$a\in A$ agreeing with $w$ through that $1$ coordinate. Thus $v<a$ and
$v$ is not an upper bound. This proves that $w=\sup A$, including
$A=\varnothing$. Infima follow by reversing all bits.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Birthday-controlled cut filling]\label{swo:sk:thm:cut}
Let $A,B$ be sets of surreals with $A<B$, meaning $a<b$ for all
$a\in A$, $b\in B$. If $\beta$ is an ordinal strictly greater than every
birthday in $A\cup B$, then a surreal $z$ of birthday exactly $\beta$
satisfies $A<z<B$. Either side may be empty.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a shorter word $x$, let $u_\beta(x)$ be the word of length $\beta$
obtained by appending a plus to $x$ and then filling all remaining
coordinates with minuses. It is the least full-length word strictly
above $x$. Dually, let $v_\beta(x)$ be $x$ followed by a minus and then
pluses; it is the greatest full-length word strictly below $x$.
These assertions follow by inspecting the first differing sign: an
extension above $x$ must choose plus when $x$ terminates, and the
least possible continuation then uses only minuses.

If $x<y$ and both have length below $\beta$, then
$u_\beta(x)\leq v_\beta(y)$. Indeed $u_\beta(x)\leq y$ by the same
first-disagreement check; equality is impossible because their
birthdays differ. Applying the dual greatest-word property gives the
claim. By \cref{swo:sk:lem:binarycomplete}, full-length sign words have a
supremum, and
\[
       z=\sup\{u_\beta(a):a\in A\}
\]
satisfies $a<u_\beta(a)\leq z$ for every $a\in A$ and
$z\leq v_\beta(b)<b$ for every $b\in B$. All words in this complete
lattice have birthday exactly $\beta$. If $A$ is empty, its supremum
is the all-minus word. The vacuous case $\beta=0$ causes no problem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Fresh separators]\label{swo:sk:cor:fresh}
If $A<B$ are sets and $S$ is a set of forbidden surreals, a separator
$z\notin S$ exists. It can be required to have any specified birthday
strictly exceeding all birthdays in $A\cup B\cup S$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}\label{swo:sk:def:eta}
A class linear order is an $\eta_{\Ord}$-order if every pair of
\emph{sets} $A<B$ has a strict separator, including empty sides. For an
infinite cardinal $\kappa$, an $\eta_\kappa$-order satisfies the same
condition when $|A\cup B|<\kappa$.
\end{definition}

The notation $\eta_\kappa$ here uses a cardinal bound directly; it should
not be confused with conventions in which the subscript is the index
$\alpha$ in $\aleph_\alpha$. By \cref{swo:sk:thm:cut}, $\No$ is an
$\eta_{\Ord}$-order. Every interval is a proper class: if $(a,b)$ were a
set, a separator of $\{a\}\cup(a,b)$ and $\{b\}$ would be a new member
of the same interval above all its members. No set is cofinal or
coinitial in $\No$.

% ---- source 11, lines 309-329
\subsection{Which recursion principle is used?}

Here \(\GB\) has a \(\ZF\) set part, elementary class comprehension,
and class replacement; choice is stated separately.
We use \(\GBC=\GB+\GC\), which includes set choice.
Cardinality calculations and cardinal-valued cofinality statements
throughout the article are read in \(\ZFC\), or in the set part of
\(\GBC\). The explicitly definable sign code and the direct
set-cut constructions retain their stated choice-free strength.

Ordinary recursion along \(\Ord\), with a \emph{set} output at every
stage and a rule using the set of earlier values and fixed class
parameters, is available in \(\GB\). On each set ordinal perform the
usual recursion with those parameters. Uniqueness makes the local
solutions agree; elementary comprehension assembles their graph.

This is different from arbitrary elementary transfinite recursion,
\(\ETR\), whose stages may themselves be classes. The latter supports
iterated truth predicates and is stronger than \(\GBC\)
\cite{GitmanHamkins}. The prefix searches, partial permutations, and
back-and-forth maps used here have set-sized stages.

$\GB+\mathrm{AC}$ (\cref{swo:sec:notation}).

\subsection{Source 12: the surreal carrier and the foundational conventions}
\label{swo:st:sec:foundations}

\subsubsection{Sign sequences}

We use the sign-sequence presentation
\[
 \No=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Ord}\{-,+\}^{\alpha}.
\]
Each individual sequence is a set. Its length $\bd(s)=\lh(s)$ is its
birthday. At the first difference, signs and termination are compared by
\[
 -\ <\ \End\ <\ +.
\]
This is the usual numerical order, not the ordinary ``shorter comes
first'' dictionary convention. Simplicity is the separate relation of
being a proper sign prefix. The standard surreal construction and its
simplicity structure are treated by Gonshor and Ehrlich
\cite{Gonshor,Ehrlich2012}.

We shall use the following standard feature, also obtainable directly
from the construction: whenever $A,B$ are \emph{sets} of surreals with
$A<B$, there is a surreal $z$ with $A<z<B$. There is a unique such
separator of minimum birthday. Moreover, if every member of $A\cup B$
has birthday less than an ordinal $\gamma$, a separator can be constructed
with birthday at most $\gamma$. This is interpolation, not Dedekind
completeness: no least upper bound is being asserted.

Every set of surreals has a birthday bound, by Replacement. Every
nonempty numerical interval contains a proper class of surreals. To see
the latter without arithmetic, start with $a<b$ and recursively choose
$x_\alpha$ between $\{a\}\cup\{x_\xi:\xi<\alpha\}$ and $\{b\}$.
The previous values form a set at each stage. Thus one obtains distinct
points for all ordinals $\alpha$.

\subsubsection{Well-orders and set-likeness}

A class well-order on a class $X$ is a strict linear class relation $R$
for which every nonempty subclass has an $R$-least element. In GBC this
agrees with the usual formulations using nonempty set subsets or absence
of descending $\omega$-sequences; the relationship is discussed in
\cite[\S3]{HamkinsWoodin}. We use strict relations throughout.

It is \emph{set-like} if
\[
 \pred_R(x)=\{y\in X:yRx\}
\]
is a set for every $x$. A proper-class set-like well-order is isomorphic
to $\Ord$: map $x$ to the ordinal type of its set of predecessors. The
range is an initial segment of $\Ord$, and cannot be a set ordinal when
$X$ is proper. This construction uses only set-sized order types.

Not all class well-orders are set-like. The ordered sums
$\Ord+1$, $\Ord+\Ord$, and $\Ord+\Ord+1$ are examples. Their notation
means ordered sums of class orders, not new von Neumann ordinals.

\subsubsection{Foundational levels}

Our set-sized results are theorems of ZFC. Our global results are in GBC,
meaning Gödel--Bernays set theory with global choice. When analyzing the
necessity of choice we work over GB plus ordinary, set-level AC.
The full collection of global well-orders is handled by formulas with
class variables, not by pretending that a proper class is an element of
another GBC class.

Kelley--Morse theory allows stronger comprehension, but its classes still
have sets, not proper classes, as members. Moving from GBC to KM does not
by itself create a powerclass object of all subclasses of $\No$.
One can instead use a larger set-theoretic universe, a set model with a
specified collection of classes, or an explicitly higher-order theory.
These are distinct interpretations and will be kept distinct.

\subsubsection{Why an ordinal-length recursion here need not invoke ETR}

A recursion on $\Ord$ that produces one \emph{set} at each stage and uses
the set of previous values is available by the ordinary recursion theorem
with class parameters in GB. In contrast, ETR permits a genuinely
class-valued recursive stage, represented by a class of pairs of a stage
and an arbitrary set. Even a truth hierarchy brings stronger principles
into play \cite[\S2]{HamkinsWoodin}. Our forth constructions, block lengths, and
canonical enumeration of a class with set-sized initial segments are
set-valued recursions. We do not invoke a universal satisfaction predicate
or unrestricted class-valued recursion.

% ---- source 09, lines 210-309
\subsection{Source 09: foundational conventions and the surreal order}
\label{swo:ec:sec:foundations}
\subsubsection{Sets, classes, and the meaning of a family}
All set-sized results are in $\ZFC$. Global results are in $\GBC$, here
meaning G\"odel--Bernays class theory with global choice. Classes have sets
as members. A proper class cannot be an element of another class. An
expression such as ``the set of class functions $E_i$'' is therefore
replaced by a \emph{uniform family}: a class relation
\[
       F\subseteq I\times\Ord\times\No
\]
whose $i$th section is the graph of $E_i$. The index $I$ may be a set or a
class of sets. If $I$ is a set, this is a uniformly set-indexed family,
not a literal set of proper classes.

This distinction does not prevent quantification over individual class
variables. For example, ``for every two class bijections $E,F$, there is
a least ordinal where they differ'' is a legitimate $\GBC$ assertion.
What is unavailable is a class having all such bijections as members.
Rangel and Mariano explicitly distinguish the set-subsets of a class
from a nonexistent class of all its subclasses \cite[\S1.1.1]{RochaRangelMariano}.

Global choice will be used in its standard consequence that a proper
class can be enumerated by $\Ord$ with set-sized initial segments. One
way to obtain this consequence is to well-order each rank layer using a
global choice function, concatenate the layers, and collapse the resulting
set-like well-order. Restricting such an order to any proper subclass
again gives order type $\Ord$.

\subsubsection{Sign sequences and the cut property}
Use the sign-sequence realization
\[
   \No=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Ord}\{-,+\}^{\alpha}.
\]
Each displayed element is a set function. Its domain is its
\emph{birthday}, denoted $\bd(x)$. Numerical order compares the first
different sign using
\[
                  -\;<\;\text{termination}\;<\;+.
\]
It is different both from ordering by birthday and from the dictionary
order on arbitrary surreal-valued words used later. The all-plus word
of length $\alpha$ represents the ordinal $\alpha$.

The standard simplicity/cut theorem says that, for sets $A,B\subseteq\No$
with $A<B$, there is a unique simplest surreal strictly between them.
Here $A<B$ means $a<b$ for every $a\in A,b\in B$, with empty sides
allowed. We write this simplest separator as $\{A\mid B\}$. A useful
birthday bound is
\begin{equation}
 \bd\{A\mid B\}\le
       \sup\{\bd(x)+1:x\in A\cup B\}.
 \label{swo:ec:eq:cut-birthday}
\end{equation}
These are standard sign-sequence facts \cite{Gonshor}; the inspected
repository also contains explicit small-cut and birthday-bound
statements on its sign carrier \cite{ProveItCuts}.

In particular, every set of surreal numbers has strict upper and lower
bounds. No set is cofinal or coinitial in $\No$, and no set is the whole
of $\No$. These assertions do not authorize a cut constructor with
arbitrary proper-class option families.

\begin{definition}\label{swo:ec:n2.1}
A class linear order $D$ has the \emph{set-cut property} if every pair of
sets $A,B\subseteq D$ satisfying $A<B$ has an element $d\in D$ with
$A<d<B$. Empty sides are allowed.
\end{definition}
The surreal order has the set-cut property. In terminology used in the
literature this is an absolute or class analogue of an $\eta$-order.
We use the explicit definition rather than relying on potentially
ambiguous subscripts such as $\eta_{\Ord}$.

\begin{lemma}[Avoiding a prescribed set]\label{swo:ec:lem:avoid}
If $D$ has the set-cut property and $U\subseteq D$ is a set, then
$D\setminus U$ also has the set-cut property.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $A,B\subseteq D\setminus U$ be sets with $A<B$, and put
\[
 U_0=\{u\in U:A<u<B\}.
\]
Then $A\cup U_0<B$. Choose $x$ with $A\cup U_0<x<B$. This $x$ is in the
original open cut. If it belonged to $U$, it would belong to $U_0$, giving
$x<x$. Thus $x\notin U$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{The precise recursion strength used here}
We repeatedly construct a sequence of \emph{sets} indexed by ordinals,
where the next value is a uniquely specified set obtained from the
set-sized previous history and fixed class parameters. This recursion
is available in $\GBC$: set recursion with class parameters constructs
all set-length approximations, uniqueness makes them compatible, and
elementary class comprehension joins them.

This is not the principle $\ETR$, whose stages can themselves be proper
classes. The latter is genuinely stronger than $\GBC$ and must not be
silently invoked by saying ``perform class recursion''
\cite[\S2]{GHHSW}. Every global recursion in the proofs below is explicitly
of the former, set-valued kind.
