\section{A formalization architecture with explicit size boundaries (source 12)}
\label{swo:st:sec:formalization}

\subsection{Start with a universe-polymorphic set theorem}

The most efficient verified starting point is independent of surreal
arithmetic. For a type $X$ with a linear order, represent a well-order on
$X$ by its binary relation and a proof of well-foundedness and linearity.
Define the common prefix by equality of predecessor sets and equality of
the restricted relations, exactly as in \cref{swo:st:sec:commonprefix}.
Prove maximality, distinct next labels, and transitivity of comparison.
This gives a generic theorem reusable for reals, surreal cutoffs, and
other ordered alphabets.

For a fixed ordinal domain, represent an enumeration as an equivalence
between an ordinal's carrier and $X$. For all well-orders of a small $X$,
using relations on $X$ often avoids unnecessarily quantifying over every
ordinal in a larger universe. The ordinal enumeration can be recovered
later from the well-order type. This is a representation choice, not an
identification of all ordinals with a small type.

\subsection{Use the existing sign-sequence foundations selectively}

The inspected repository file
\begin{quote}\small
\path{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/Surreal/Foundations/SignSequenceSimplicity.lean}
\end{quote}
contains, among others,
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
isPrefix\_of\_minimum\_birthday\par
existsUnique\_prefix\_of\_ordConnected\par
existsUnique\_minimum\_birthday\_of\_ordConnected\par
existsUnique\_simplest\_separator
\end{quote}
\cite{ProveItSimplicity}. Its comments explicitly distinguish uniqueness of a
simplest separator from existence of separators for arbitrary small cuts.
The last theorem assumes a separator exists. It must not be imported as
though it independently established all cut-filling instances.

A suitable interface for the order-theoretic development needs a birthday
map, a numerical linear order, sign concatenation and truncation, the
first-difference comparison, and small-cut existence with a birthday
bound. Field operations can be omitted except in illustrative examples.

\subsection{Suggested proof modules}

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.65\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Module & Principal obligations\\
\midrule
\endhead
\texttt{CommonPrefix} & The elementary definition; initiality; maximality;
next-label inequality; generic full lexical linearity.\\
\texttt{ExhaustiveLex} & Prefix-freeness; equivalence with ordinal
first-difference comparison; transport between relations and enumerations.\\
\texttt{BinaryCubes} & Cylinder fusion; strict hierarchy;
characteristic-cut embeddings; precise ordinal concatenation.\\
\texttt{PermutationCoding} & Pair orientation, product scheduler,
transpositions, and exhaustive output.\\
\texttt{BirthdayCutoffs} & Size of $\No_{<\kappa}$; prefix-free numerical code;
cone partition; regular and singular sums.\\
\texttt{ClassFamilies} & A formal set/class model, sections of class
relations, baseline enumeration, and diagonal non-enumerability.\\
\texttt{UniverseModels} & A chosen inaccessible-rank interpretation;
internal/external well-foundedness and identification of the two orders.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The finite common-prefix theorem should be proved directly for relations,
not by first developing all of ordinal arithmetic. The scheduler proof
should record both surjectivity and the first-row-appearance invariant;
a bare cardinal pairing equivalence is insufficient. The singular theorem
must use ordinal sums, never a cardinal cast that silently loses order.

\subsection{Universe accounting}

Schematically, a sign-sequence type built from small ordinal lengths has
one universe level for its ordinal index and potentially a higher level
for the total carrier. One should retain explicit levels rather than
promise a concrete declaration before checking the chosen ordinal API.
A bounded carrier with a specified small birthday bound is the cleanest
first implementation.

Three operations must remain visibly different: taking a subtype of one
fixed surreal carrier; lifting an existing carrier to a larger universe;
and constructing all surreals of the larger universe. The third adds new
birthdays and is not merely a type lift. Likewise, a type of relations on
a fixed carrier can exist at a higher level without becoming a same-level
class of all class relations.

For global theorems, two safe routes are available. One may formalize GB
or GBC models and express theorems about their class predicates. Or one
may formalize the bounded analogue inside a hierarchy of explicit
universes and separately prove its interpretation in a set/class model.
Neither route requires an inconsistent type of all types.

\subsection{Axioms, kernels, and honest validation}

A full formalization should record the uses of classical logic, set
choice, global choice, and any ambient model-existence assumptions.
The common-prefix comparison should not acquire an ETR assumption merely
because abstract class order comparison sometimes uses one.
The inaccessible-rank example is conditional on that ambient cardinal,
whereas the bounded singular theorem needs no inaccessible cardinal.

The present package includes exact executable tests of finite shadows:
sign coding, pair coding, the scheduler, the common-prefix formula, and
finite adjacency. These are useful implementation checks and
counterexample detectors. They are not kernel-checked proofs of any
infinite or class theorem, and no supplied file is advertised as such.

\section{Formalization strategies and paradox avoidance (source 09)}
\label{swo:ec:sec:formalization}
\subsection{Four suitable frameworks}
\paragraph{Birthday-bounded $\ZFC$.}
For the most immediately formalizable results, fix $\No_{<\theta}$ and a
length bound. Everything is then a set: relations, permutations, words,
embeddings, and counterexamples. The exact ordinal spectrum and finite
residual adjacency results belong here. No inaccessible cardinal is
needed.

\paragraph{Two-sorted $\GBC$.}
Use set variables for surreal numbers, support codes, ordinals, and
bounded prefixes; use class variables for global enumerations. Treat
$E<_{\lex}F$ as a formula on class variables. State family theorems with
an explicit uniform ternary relation. The core is a genuine class
linear order. This framework proves all the global results above without
a class-of-classes object.

\paragraph{A larger universe or explicit higher sorts.}
To make the entire collection of smaller-universe global enumerations
an object, put it in a larger universe, or use separate sorts for sets,
classes, and collections of classes. Membership and comprehension must
be stratified. The inaccessible model in \cref{swo:ec:sec:universe} demonstrates
one controlled interpretation. A higher-sort theory requires its own
axioms; merely writing a new symbol for all classes does not specify one.

\paragraph{Universe-polymorphic dependent type theory.}
A type-theoretic formalization can construct a carrier relative to a
universe, then form its function types at an appropriate larger level.
It must not infer that those functions are indexed by the original
small ordinals or that every large subtype is small. Universe
polymorphism handles repeated constructions; it does not identify the
universe levels.

\subsection{Kelley--Morse does not create classes of classes}
$\KM$ strengthens class comprehension, but its ordinary two-sorted
interpretation still has sets as the members of classes. It does not
turn proper classes into elements of other classes. Similarly, adding
$\ETR$ resolves certain class-valued recursion problems but does not
invalidate \cref{swo:ec:thm:diagonal}. These are separate issues: recursion
strength, comprehension strength, and object level.

For non-set-like class well-orders, recursion along predecessors may
need class-valued histories. One should therefore not reuse our
set-valued recursion justification without checking the new hypotheses.
The literature on $\ETR$ supplies the appropriate distinctions
\cite{GHHSW,HamkinsRecursion}.

\subsection{Concrete reuse of the ProveIt sign-sequence foundation}
At the pinned snapshot, the foundational module declares
\begin{verbatim}
structure SignSequence : Type (u + 1) where
  birthday : Ordinal.{u}
  signAt : Ordinal.{u} -> SignType
  signAt_ne_zero_iff : ...
\end{verbatim}
The ASCII arrow above is only a typographical transcription. The
actual module supplies a linear order via zero-extended signs and
contains the following relevant named declarations \cite{ProveItSigns}:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lY@{}}
\toprule
Declaration & Role in this project\\
\midrule
\texttt{lt\_iff} & First-disagreement characterization of sign order.\\
\texttt{ordinalOrderEmbedding} & Increasing embedding of small ordinals.\\
\texttt{birthdays\_bounded} & Birthday bounds for explicitly small families.\\
\texttt{small\_bounded} & Smallness of bounded sign fragments.\\
\texttt{not\_small} & The entire carrier does not fit in the lower universe.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The companion cut-operation file contains \texttt{cut\_realizes} and
\texttt{birthday\_cut\_le\_iSup}, together with the separator theorem
\texttt{exists\_separator\_of\_small\_sets} \cite{ProveItCuts}. Its documentation
explicitly distinguishes this order/cut development from supplying field
operations. The present proofs need only the order/cut development.

\subsection{A proposed Lean dependency plan}
A practical implementation should proceed in the following dependency
order, keeping the formal claims smaller than the whole manuscript at
first.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.]
\item \textbf{Fixed-alphabet enumeration order.} Represent an enumeration
by its ordinal domain and an equivalence onto a fixed small alphabet.
Prove prefix-freeness before defining variable-domain first disagreement.
Then prove pair-switch coding, cardinality bounds, and the
$\kappa^+$ stabilization obstruction.
\item \textbf{Small injective prefixes.} Prove the extension lemma for a
partial injection into a sufficiently large ordinal. Keep the equal
cardinality of both complements as an explicit lemma. This part is
independent of surreals.
\item \textbf{Abstract small-cut alphabets.} Parameterize the avoidance
and forced-prefix interpolation lemmas by a linear order with separators
for explicitly small option families. Instantiate with the existing
sign carrier only afterwards.
\item \textbf{Coded support core.} Use a small support, a permutation on
it, and a no-fixed-point condition, or use bounded permutations followed
by a proved equivalence relation and canonical normalization. Supply a
baseline equivalence as an explicit parameter.
\item \textbf{Large-family interface.} Represent rows by a single
function or relation, retain its universe levels, and prove bounded
reflection for small index types. State separately which large index
orders possess an ordinal enumeration with small initial segments.
\item \textbf{Back-and-forth and the diagonal.} Formalize the
set-valued recursion invariant. Only after its smallness obligations
are discharged should the abstract core isomorphism and no-coding
statements be instantiated.
\end{enumerate}

In a universe-indexed version, a proposed baseline has the shape
$\mathrm{Ordinal}.\{u\}\simeq\mathrm{SignSequence}.\{u\}$. Its existence
is not inferred here merely because both types live in
$\mathrm{Type}(u+1)$; equal universe level does not imply equipotence.
Likewise, the type of \emph{all} functions between these carriers can
be formed in the ambient type theory without becoming a small-universe
class of set codes. This is exactly the distinction exhibited by
\cref{swo:ec:thm:inaccessible}.

\subsection{A checklist of invalid shortcuts}
The following shortcuts would change the mathematical problem or
introduce an unjustified assumption:
\begin{itemize}
\item treating $\No$ as a set in its original universe, or treating all
global enumerations as members of an ordinary class;
\item using an arbitrary class option family in the surreal cut
constructor, or replacing set-indexed interpolation by class-indexed
interpolation;
\item assuming every class well-order has type $\Ord$, or confusing the
well-order represented by an enumeration with the lexicographic order
of enumerations;
\item completing a partial injection without checking complement
cardinalities, or using a set of individually given class witnesses
without a uniform family relation;
\item transferring a proof of equimorphism to isomorphism, or assuming
an order embedding preserves ambient suprema;
\item attributing the new global core and diagonal theorems to existing
Lean declarations merely because the requisite sign-cut primitives
are present.
\end{itemize}

\section{A Lean formalization plan tied to ProveIt (source 08)}\label{swo:sk:sec:lean}

The repository was inspected at commit
\begin{center}
\texttt{ae7c1e6aae3dc58c9c638b35646865ad6c10387d}.
\end{center}
The relevant project is \texttt{Algebra/SurrealNumbers}. Its README
explicitly distinguishes research drafts from machine-checked results
and describes an axiom audit \cite{ProveItSurrealREADME}. This manuscript uses
that distinction rather than treating all repository prose as a
formal theorem.

\subsection{Inspected declarations}

The source \texttt{Surreal/Foundations/SignSequence.lean} defines a
universe-indexed carrier of the form
\begin{lstlisting}
structure SignSequence : Type (u + 1) where
  birthday : Ordinal.{u}
  signAt : Ordinal.{u} -> SignType
  signAt_ne_zero_iff :
    forall i, Iff (Not (signAt i = 0)) (i < birthday)
\end{lstlisting}
This display is an ASCII rendering of the inspected declaration, not a
new compiled file. Its zero extension uses the ordered values minus,
termination, and plus. The source gives the numerical linear order via
Mathlib's lexicographic function order and proves
\texttt{lt\_iff}, \texttt{ordinalOrderEmbedding},
\texttt{birthdays\_bounded}, \texttt{small\_strict\_upper\_bounds},
\texttt{not\_small}, and \texttt{small\_bounded} \cite{ProveItSigns}.

Two declarations in \texttt{SignSequenceCut.lean} are particularly relevant:
\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak cut\_\allowbreak separator\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak birthday\_\allowbreak lt}
and \texttt{small\_cut\_fillers}. The construction uses full-length binary words,
uses their complete lexicographic order, and produces a separator of
exactly a prescribed birthday above all option birthdays
\cite{ProveItCutFill}. This is the formal landing point for
\cref{swo:sk:thm:cut,swo:sk:cor:fresh}; it is stronger than merely having a dense
linear order.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.29\textwidth}Y@{}}
\toprule
Inspected ingredient & Role in a formalization \\
\midrule
\texttt{SignSequence.lt\_iff} & Express every comparison by its least differing coordinate \\
\texttt{birthdays\_bounded} & Bound options indexed by a lower-universe small type \\
\texttt{not\_small} & Exclude collapsing the whole carrier into the birthday universe \\
\texttt{small\_bounded} & Represent a fixed birthday fragment by a genuinely small type \\
\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak cut\_\allowbreak separator\_\allowbreak of\_\allowbreak birthday\_\allowbreak lt} & Choose a separator outside a small forbidden family by increasing its birthday \\
\texttt{small\_cut\_fillers} & Discharge the ordered-cut interface used by the abstract arguments \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\subsection{Smallness is not the same as having a type}

In Lean, \texttt{SignSequence.\{u\}} and
\texttt{Ordinal.\{u\}} live in \texttt{Type (u + 1)}.
They are not small relative to universe \texttt{u}. A
\texttt{Set SignSequence} is a predicate on the whole larger carrier;
it need not be a small family of surreals. Thus a formal cut theorem
must carry a condition such as \texttt{Small.\{u\} I} on its index type,
not merely declare its options to be a \texttt{Set}.

The same applies to permutations. A predicate-defined support in the
larger universe is not automatically an admissible set support at the
smaller level. The orbit should use explicit small support codes or
an equivalent \texttt{Small.\{u\}} hypothesis. Otherwise the crucial
prefix bound and the intended distinction between skeleton and full
enumeration space are lost.

One safe interface keeps an equivalence
\begin{lstlisting}
b : Equiv Ordinal.{u} SignSequence.{u}
\end{lstlisting}
as an explicit parameter. The present manuscript does not
claim that the repository already constructs this equivalence with
all the required relative-smallness properties. An implementation
must either prove that baseline theorem in its universe framework or
retain the baseline as a stated assumption.

\subsection{Suggested module boundaries}

The following are proposed modules, not existing files or completed
formalizations.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*]
\item \texttt{WellOrders/Exhaustive.lean}: exhaustive enumerations of a
fixed carrier, no-prefix ambiguity, first-disagreement order, and
relations versus enumerations.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/PairCoding.lean}: binary pair blocks,
small-order universality, cardinality, prefix stabilization, and the
exact ordinal spectrum.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/FiniteTails.lean}: cylinder extrema, adjacency,
and finite-condensation blocks.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/PrefixExtension.lean}: small partial injections,
choice of a larger support bound, complement bijections, and canonical
moved-point codes.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/SetCuts.lean}: the active-row invariant, the
bound on cross-disagreement coordinates, stopping, and strict
separation before completion.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/BoundedPermutations.lean}: the code carrier,
its small-cut interface, and the relative-universe form of the
surreal-skeleton theorem.
\item \texttt{WellOrders/ClassBinaryCoding.lean}: terminal block codes,
ordinal concatenation, and uniform diagonalization in the chosen
higher-type representation.
\end{enumerate}

The first three modules can be developed without surreal arithmetic or
class metatheory. The next three isolate the delicate smallness and
extension arguments. The final module should be delayed until the
meaning of a uniform operator on larger function types is fixed.

\subsection{Two complementary implementation routes}

A \emph{cutoff-first} implementation proves theorems for
$\No_{<\kappa}$ and $\W_\kappa(\No_{<\kappa})$ with explicit regularity
and cardinal hypotheses. All carriers are ordinary types and the
formal statements closely match \cref{swo:sk:thm:cutoff}. This is the simplest
route to substantial checked mathematics.

A \emph{universe-relative} implementation abstracts over a large
ordered carrier, a lower-universe small-cut operation, and a baseline
equivalence. It formalizes the local orbit argument relative to that
notion of smallness. Care is needed when proving back-and-forth: an
arbitrary well-order of a large type may have initial segments too
large for the small-cut operation. One must use an enumeration whose
proper initial segments are small, rather than invoke an unrestricted
large-type choice order.

A third option is a deep formalization of a two-sorted class theory or
of a specified model $(M,\mathcal C)$. This is the best route for
comparing axiomatic strengths and internal/external semantics, but is
not necessary to certify the set-sized combinatorics.

\subsection{Trust boundary}

The cited repository files were read, not rebuilt in this work. No new
Lean module accompanies this manuscript, and no new kernel-checking
claim is made. A future implementation should pin the repository and
Mathlib revisions, compile in dependency order, reject unfinished
proofs, and inspect the resulting axiom dependencies. Finite tests of
codes, even exhaustive ones, cannot certify the class interpolation or
back-and-forth arguments.

% ---- hand chunk formal_after

\merge\ The four plans agree on a first milestone that is independent of
surreal arithmetic: the predecessor or common-prefix comparison of
well-orders on a small carrier, prefix-freeness and the
successor-cardinal obstruction, then bounded-support prefix completion and
small-family interpolation, reusing the existing small-cut interface. This
is a proposal recorded for the collection's formalization work; no such
module exists.

\part{Set-sized strata: topology, cut spectra and block types}\label{swo:part:strata}

\merge\ (3 October 2026.) This Part opens the batch-81 addition of six
further manuscripts, sources~13--18 (\cref{swo:sub:batch81}); its notation is
fixed in \cref{swo:sub:notation81}. It prints everything the six sources prove
about set-sized enumeration orders: the density and cellularity of every
stratum $\WO_\alpha(X)$ of every infinite linear alphabet
(\cref{swo:ds:sec:topology}), conditional incompleteness and global
characters (\cref{swo:ds:sec:gaps}), the cut spectrum of the alphabet
$\No_{<\kappa}$ (\cref{swo:gs:sec:alphabet}), the characters, fusion theorem,
exact gap spectrum and gap multiplicities of the minimal slice
$\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ (\cref{swo:vi:sec:minimal}), the coding lengths,
cube spectra and density of the longer strata $\WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})$
(\cref{swo:cb:new:sec:block-types}), and the six sources' set-sized baselines
and warnings (\cref{swo:vi:sec:baselines}). \Cref{swo:vi:sec:status} records
which questions of this report and of the reals report the Part answers.

The bases are chosen by generality, as in Parts~I--V: source~16 for density,
cellularity and incompleteness (every infinite alphabet, every stratum),
source~17 for the alphabet and the minimal slice (every infinite cardinal
cutoff, with no hypothesis on $2^{<\kappa}$), source~15 for the longer strata
(the only source treating them). Source~18, written before sources~13--17 had
arrived and without knowledge of them, proves most of the minimal-slice
results a third time; its density, characters and interpolation proofs differ
in detail and are printed as routes, its gap-spectrum proof repeats
source~17's and is recorded in notes, and its general permutation principle
and exact gap multiplicities are new and printed in full. The whole Part is
set theory in $\ZFC$; no class is involved.

Throughout the Part, $\kappa$ is an infinite cardinal used as a birthday
cutoff, $\mu=2^{<\kappa}=\card{\No_{<\kappa}}$, $r=\cf\kappa$ and
$q=\cf\mu$; a general alphabet $X$ is an infinite linearly ordered set of
cardinality $\mu$. Source~16 wrote $\kappa$ for $\card X$ and $\theta$ for the
cutoff, and source~18 wrote $\nu$, $\tau$ for $r$, $q$; their text is printed
in the convention above (\cref{swo:sub:notation81}).

% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 470-596
\section{Density and cellularity at every fixed type (source 16)}\label{swo:ds:sec:topology}

\merge\ Source~16's Section~5, printed whole. Its $\kappa$ (the cardinality
of $X$) is printed $\mu$, its cutoff $\theta$ is printed $\kappa$, and its
$d$, $c$ are printed $\dens$, $\cell$ (\cref{swo:sub:notation81}); its
equations keep their numbers as (S16.$n$). Source~18 proves the same theorem
independently, with a different lemma and a different lower-bound family
(\cref{swo:sh:sec:new-bounded-invariants}); the reals report proves the case
$X=\mathbb R$ (\cref{swo:vi:note:lwo-density}).

% ---- source 16 resumes (Section 5)

For a set linear order $L$ with its \emph{order topology}, let $\dens(L)$ be the
least cardinality of a dense subset. Let $\cell(L)$ be the supremum of the
cardinalities of families of pairwise disjoint nonempty open sets.
Always $\cell(L)\le \dens(L)\le|L|$. A prefix cylinder is the collection of
enumerations extending one fixed injective prefix. Cylinders are convex,
but need not themselves be open. Our lower bounds use actual open
intervals lying inside cylinders; our upper bounds use a family that meets
every nonempty open set.

\begin{lemma}[No jump between distinct short-prefix branches]\label{swo:ds:lem:nojump}
If $e<f$ in $\WO_\alpha(X)$ first differ at a coordinate
$\delta<\mu$, then they are not adjacent.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $p=e\res\delta=f\res\delta$ and $a=e(\delta)<b=f(\delta)$.
If there is no point between $e$ and $f$, then $e$ must be the maximum of
its $p\cat a$ cylinder, and $f$ the minimum of its $p\cat b$ cylinder.
Any ascent in the remaining suffix of $e$ can be swapped to produce a
larger member of its cylinder. Thus that suffix is numerically strictly
decreasing. Similarly, the suffix of $f$ must be strictly increasing.
Their common remaining label set is
$X\setminus(\ran(p)\cup\{a,b\})$, of cardinality $\mu$. Its numerical
order would be both a well-order and a reverse well-order. An infinite
linear order cannot have both properties. This contradiction proves the
claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact density and cellularity]\label{swo:ds:thm:density}
Let $|X|=\mu$ be infinite and $|\alpha|=\mu$. Then
\[
 \dens(\WO_\alpha(X))=\cell(\WO_\alpha(X))=
 \begin{cases}
 \mu^{<\mu},&\alpha=\mu,\ \mu+1,\text{ or }\mu+2,\\
 2^\mu,&\text{all other }\alpha.
 \end{cases}
\tag{S16.3}\label{swo:ds:eq:density}
\]
Moreover $\dens(\WO(X))=\cell(\WO(X))=2^\mu$.
No regularity assumption is needed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For any $\delta<\mu$ there are $\mu^{|\delta|}$ injective prefixes
of length $\delta$, and each extends to an admissible enumeration. One
way to see the lower count is to choose, for each coordinate, its label in
a separate $\mu$-sized cell of a partition of $X$; the choices are
automatically injective. Every such cylinder contains at least three
points and hence a nonempty ambient open interval. Distinct cylinders at
one level are disjoint and convex. Consequently
\[
 \cell(\WO_\alpha(X))\ge
 \sup_{\delta<\mu}\mu^{|\delta|}=\mu^{<\mu}.
\]

Suppose $\alpha=\mu+n$, $n\le2$. Group enumerations by their common
prefix of length $\mu$. A nonempty group has exactly $n!$ points,
coming from the permutations of the omitted $n$ labels. Thus every group
has at most two points. By \Cref{swo:ds:lem:nojump}, distinct groups have
dense quotient order: if two groups were consecutive, the last point of
the first and first point of the second would be adjacent and differ
before $\mu$.

It follows that every nonempty open set contains a cylinder specified
before $\mu$, except possibly a singleton consisting of an isolated
global endpoint. Indeed, select a group strictly between the relevant
boundary groups, choose a member of it, and bound its first disagreement
with the boundaries by a coordinate below $\mu$. The resulting prefix
cylinder lies inside the open set. Select one completion for each
injective prefix of length less than $\mu$ and add any isolated global
endpoints. This gives a dense set of size at most
\[
 \sum_{\delta<\mu}\mu^{|\delta|}=\mu^{<\mu}.
\]
Together with the lower bound this proves the first line of
\eqref{swo:ds:eq:density}, including singular $\mu$.

If $\alpha=\mu+n$ for finite $n\ge3$, fix $F\subseteq X$ of size $n$.
There are $2^\mu$ bijections $p:\mu\to X\setminus F$. The
completion cylinder for each is a finite convex chain of $n!\ge6$
points. An interior point has both immediate neighbors in that chain;
the interval between those neighbors is its singleton, an open set in
the entire order. Different prefixes give $2^\mu$ disjoint open
singletons.

In every remaining case $\alpha\ge\mu+\omega$. Let $\tau$ be the
order type of the interval $[\mu,\alpha)$, so $\alpha=\mu+\tau$
and $|\tau|$ is infinite. Choose $Y\subseteq X$ with
$|Y|=|\tau|$ and $|X\setminus Y|=\mu$. Each of the $2^\mu$
bijections $p:\mu\to X\setminus Y$ gives a convex cylinder of
completions with residual order $\WO_\tau(Y)$. Every such cylinder has
at least three points, so supplies a nonempty ambient open interval.
These intervals are pairwise disjoint. The cardinal upper bound
$|\WO_\alpha(X)|\le2^\mu$ finishes the proof for all strata.

For $\WO(X)$ use the same construction with a fixed residual set of
three labels. The resulting six-point cylinders are convex even when all
enumeration lengths are admitted. Thus $\cell(\WO(X))\ge2^\mu$, and the
cardinality bound gives equality throughout.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why two terminal labels differ from three]\label{swo:ds:n5.3}
There can be $2^\mu$ adjacent pairs in $\WO_{\mu+2}(X)$ while its
density is only $\mu^{<\mu}$. An adjacent pair does not contain an
open interval. Three or more terminal labels give a permutation block
with an interior point, and that point is isolated. This is why the
topological transition occurs at $\mu+3$, rather than $\mu+1$ or
$\mu+2$. If $\mu^{<\mu}=2^\mu$, the two displayed values
coincide; the structural distinction still holds.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Birthday-stage topology]\label{swo:ds:cor:birthdaytopology}
For every infinite cardinal $\kappa$, put $\mu=2^{<\kappa}$.
Formula~\eqref{swo:ds:eq:density} applies to $\No_{<\kappa}$, whose
cardinality is $\mu$.
If $\kappa$ is regular and $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, the first three strata
have density and cellularity $\kappa$, whereas all later strata and the
full order have density and cellularity $2^\kappa$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Only the last simplification needs comment. Regularity and
$2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ imply $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$: for
$\lambda<\kappa$, every function $\lambda\to\kappa$ has bounded range,
and for $\rho<\kappa$ one has
$\rho^\lambda\le2^{\rho\cdot\lambda}\le\kappa$. Taking the union of
$\kappa$ bounds proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[\merge; the reals report's case]\label{swo:vi:note:lwo-density}
For $X=\mathbb R$, the reals report proves
$\dens(\WO_\alpha)=\cell(\WO_\alpha)=2^{<\mathfrak c}$ for
$\alpha\in\{\mathfrak c,\mathfrak c+1,\mathfrak c+2\}$ and $2^{\mathfrak c}$ for
$\mathfrak c+3\le\alpha<\mathfrak c^+$ (\code{lwo:prop:all-strata-density}), with
the adjacent pairs at $\mathfrak c+2$ and the isolated points from
$\mathfrak c+3$ on (\code{lwo:prop:finite-topology}) and the minimal slice in
\code{lwo:prop:fixed-topology}. Since $\mathfrak c^\lambda=2^\lambda$ for
infinite $\lambda<\mathfrak c$, one has $\mathfrak c^{<\mathfrak c}=2^{<\mathfrak c}$,
and \cref{swo:ds:thm:density} with $X=\mathbb R$ is exactly that result, by
the same two mechanisms (short-prefix cylinders, and the finite terminal
permutation block). Source~16 does not cite these statements; it cites the
reals report only for the set baseline (\cref{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum}). New in
source~16 is the generality: every infinite linear alphabet, of any
cardinality including singular ones and $\aleph_0$, with no density or
saturation assumption on $X$.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[\merge; the birthday-cutoff instances, and why three formulas agree]\label{swo:vi:note:instances}
For $X=\No_{<\kappa}$, \cref{swo:ds:cor:birthdaytopology} gives
$\dens=\cell=\mu^{<\mu}$ on $\WO_\mu$, $\WO_{\mu+1}$, $\WO_{\mu+2}$ and
$2^\mu$ on every longer stratum. Three other sources compute instances:
source~17 for the minimal slice ($\dens=\weight=\cell=\mu^{<\mu}$,
\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}), source~18 for every stratum of every alphabet
(\cref{swo:sh:thm:new-minimal-density,swo:sh:thm:new-all-types-density},
printed next), and source~15 for the block types $\WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}$, with
the value $2^{<\mu}$ when $\rho_\kappa=\mu$, $\gamma=1$ and $n\le2$, and $2^\mu$
otherwise (\cref{swo:cb:new:cor:block-density}). The formulas agree. If
$\mu>\kappa$ then $\mu\le2^\kappa$, so for $\lambda<\mu$ one has
$\mu^\lambda\le2^{\kappa\cdot\lambda}=2^{\max(\kappa,\lambda)}\le2^{<\mu}$,
whence $\mu^{<\mu}=2^{<\mu}$. If $\mu=\kappa$ is regular then $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$
and $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ (proof of \cref{swo:ds:cor:birthdaytopology}). If
$\mu=\kappa$ is singular, $\kappa$ is a strong limit, $\rho_\kappa=\kappa\cdot\kappa$
falls under source~15's second case, and
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=2^\kappa$ (\cref{swo:sh:cor:new-regular-singular-invariants}).
In every case the four sources give the same cardinal.
\end{note}

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 493-664
\subsection{Source 18's route: exact cardinal invariants of bounded enumeration orders}
\label{swo:sh:sec:new-bounded-invariants}

\merge\ Source~18's Section~4 up to its residual-alphabet subsection,
printed whole as a second route to \cref{swo:ds:thm:density}. Source~18
presents these theorems as advances; within batch~81 source~16 proves them too,
and source~18 could not know it (its pin precedes the arrival of
sources~13--17). Source~18's status table says that its proof review
``corrected the arbitrary-alphabet density construction to include possible
isolated global endpoints''; source~16's proof treats the same endpoints
(``except possibly a singleton consisting of an isolated global endpoint'').
Its remaining subsections are printed in \cref{swo:vi:sub:sh-chars}, its
residual-alphabet lemmas in \cref{swo:sh:lem:new-residual-alphabet,swo:sh:lem:new-cardinal-cofinalities}.

% ---- source 18 resumes (Section 4)

Throughout this section, $X$ is an infinite linearly ordered set of
cardinality $\mu$, identified with its initial ordinal when it is used as
an index.  If $|\alpha|=\mu$, write
\[
 \WO_\alpha(X)=\{e:\alpha\longrightarrow X:e\text{ is bijective}\},
\]
ordered by the first unequal coordinate.  A proper-prefix comparison
never occurs between two exhaustive enumerations of the same carrier.
We use $\dens(L)$ for the least cardinality of a topologically dense subset
of the order topology, and $\cell(L)$ for the supremum of the cardinalities
of pairwise disjoint families of nonempty open sets.  The lower-bound
families constructed below attain the asserted cellularities, so no
convention concerning whether the supremum is attained affects the
answers.  All assertions in this section are theorems of ZFC.

\subsubsection{Prefix cylinders and the minimal enumeration type}

For an injective prefix $p:\delta\to X$, $\delta\leq\alpha$, its cylinder
is
\[
 C_p=\{e\in\WO_\alpha(X):e\restriction\delta=p\}.
\]
Every cylinder is convex.  If $\delta<\mu$, the cylinder is nonempty:
both the unused alphabet and the remaining index set have cardinality
$\mu$, so a bijection between them completes the prefix.  Such a
cylinder has at least three elements, obtained, for example, by
permuting three unused labels in a fixed completion.  It therefore
contains a nonempty open interval of the ambient enumeration order.
The entire cylinder need not be open; the proof uses only the open
interval it contains.

\begin{lemma}[The dense order of cofinite prefixes]
\label{swo:sh:lem:new-cofinite-prefixes}
For $n<\omega$, let
\[
 Q_n(X)=\{p:\mu\to X:p\text{ is injective and }
                       |X\setminus\operatorname{ran}p|=n\}.
\]
With first-difference comparison, $Q_n(X)$ is a dense linear order.
Consequently $\WO_\mu(X)=Q_0(X)$ is dense.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Take $p<p'$ and let $\delta<\mu$ be their first disagreement.  If there
are $\delta<\beta<\gamma<\mu$ with $p(\beta)<p(\gamma)$, interchanging
these two entries gives $p<s<p'$, with $s\in Q_n(X)$.  Similarly, a
descent $p'(\beta)>p'(\gamma)$ after $\delta$ gives $p<s<p'$ by an
interchange in $p'$.  If neither operation is available, the tail of
$p$ after $\delta$ is strictly decreasing and the tail of $p'$ after
$\delta$ is strictly increasing.  Their ranges have an infinite common
subset: they omit from $X$ only their common prefix, of size less than
$\mu$, two first-disagreement labels, and two finite sets of omitted
labels.  On that common subset the numerical order would be both a
well-order and a reverse well-order.  This is impossible for an
infinite set.  Indeed, an infinite well-order contains an initial
suborder of type $\omega$, which has no largest element.  Thus one of
the interchanges is available.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact density and cellularity at the minimal type]
\label{swo:sh:thm:new-minimal-density}
For every infinite linearly ordered alphabet $X$ of cardinality $\mu$,
\[
 \boxed{\quad \dens(\WO_\mu(X))
                 =\cell(\WO_\mu(X))=\mu^{<\mu}.\quad}
\]
No regularity assumption on $\mu$, and no density assumption on $X$,
is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are $\mu^{<\mu}$ injective prefixes of length less than $\mu$.
For the upper bound, choose one completion for each such prefix.  Given
$e<f$, density supplies $g$ strictly between them.  Choose
$\delta<\mu$ larger than both first-disagreement coordinates of
$(e,g)$ and $(g,f)$.  Then
$C_{g\restriction\delta}\subseteq(e,f)$, so the selected completions
are dense.  Since a dense linear order with at least two points has no
isolated endpoint, meeting all nonempty open intervals also gives
topological density.  This proves $d\leq\mu^{<\mu}$, and a dense set
meets every member of a disjoint nonempty open family, giving $c\leq d$.

For the lower bound, choose distinct labels $a_\delta\in X$ for
$\delta<\mu$.  For each $\delta$, take every injective prefix of
length $1+\delta$ whose first label is $a_\delta$.  There are
$\mu^{|\delta|}$ such prefixes.  To justify this count for infinite
$\delta$, functions $\delta\to\mu$ can be encoded as injective maps
$\delta\to\delta\times\mu\cong\mu$ by recording the coordinate
alongside its value; the reverse inequality is immediate.  The finite
cases are equally direct.  Prefixes in different chosen levels have
different first labels, while two distinct prefixes within a level
have equal lengths and disagree.  Their cylinders are therefore
pairwise disjoint.  Choose one nonempty open interval in each cylinder.
The resulting disjoint family has cardinality
\[
 \sum_{\delta<\mu}\mu^{|\delta|}=\mu^{<\mu},
\]
because $1+\delta<\mu$ for every $\delta<\mu$.  This proves the lower
bound and also proves that the stated cellularity is attained.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A sharp transition at the third terminal coordinate}

\begin{theorem}[Density and cellularity for every fixed type]
\label{swo:sh:thm:new-all-types-density}
If $|X|=\mu$ is infinite and $\mu\leq\alpha<\mu^+$, then
\[
 \boxed{
 \dens(\WO_\alpha(X))=\cell(\WO_\alpha(X))=
 \begin{cases}
  \mu^{<\mu},&\alpha\in\{\mu,\mu+1,\mu+2\},\\[2pt]
  2^\mu,&\mu+3\leq\alpha<\mu^+.
 \end{cases}}
\]
These formulas are valid for arbitrary numerical orders on $X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First take $\alpha=\mu+n$ with $n\leq2$.  Restriction to the first
$\mu$ coordinates exhibits $\WO_{\mu+n}(X)$ as a lexicographic
sum over $Q_n(X)$ of finite fibers of size $n!$; the omitted $n$ labels
are the only possible final labels.  Each fiber has at most two points.
By \Cref{swo:sh:lem:new-cofinite-prefixes}, the quotient $Q_n(X)$ is
dense.  Thus every nonempty open interval in
$\WO_{\mu+n}(X)$ contains a point whose $\mu$-prefix lies
strictly between the prefixes of its two bounds: if the bounds had
the same prefix, their fiber of at most two points would have empty
open interval.  A prefix of this intermediate point of length less
than $\mu$, taken beyond both relevant first disagreements, has its
entire cylinder inside the interval.  Choose one completion of every
prefix of length less than $\mu$, and also include the global least
and greatest points whenever they exist.  The resulting family is
topologically dense: it meets every nonempty open interval, while
the added endpoints handle a possible isolated endpoint in a two-point
terminal fiber.  Adding at most two points does not change its
cardinality bound $\mu^{<\mu}$.  The antichain of cylinders from the
preceding proof gives the matching lower bound.  This argument
explicitly deals with the possible jumps
when $n=2$; it does not assume that every cylinder is open, or that
every interval contains a point separated from both bounds merely by
choosing an arbitrary intermediate point.

Now suppose $\alpha\geq\mu+3$, and write $\alpha=\mu+\beta$, where
$\beta$ is the order type of its tail after the first $\mu$ positions.
Choose $T\subseteq X$ with $|T|=|\beta|$ and
$|X\setminus T|=\mu$.  If $|\beta|=\mu$, use a partition of $X$ into
two $\mu$-element sets; otherwise remove a set of size $|\beta|$.
There are $2^\mu$ bijections $p:\mu\to X\setminus T$.  This standard
count follows either from the cardinality theorem for well-order
enumerations or directly by orienting $\mu$ disjoint pairs of labels.
For each such $p$, the cylinder $C_p$ consists of all continuations
that biject $\beta$ onto $T$.  Since $\beta\geq3$, it contains at least
three points and hence a nonempty open interval.  The cylinders are
pairwise disjoint because the prefixes are distinct and have the same
length.  We obtain $2^\mu$ disjoint nonempty open intervals.  On the
other hand, $|\WO_\alpha(X)|\leq\mu^\mu=2^\mu$, so no density
or cellularity can exceed $2^\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{swo:sh:n4.4}
For $\mu^{<\mu}<2^\mu$, the values for $\mu+2$ and $\mu+3$ differ.
Thus merely adding one terminal coordinate can change both density
and cellularity.  For singular strong-limit $\mu$, both expressions
are already $2^\mu$, so the geometric distinction remains but the
cardinal invariants coincide.  The formula is a new consequence beyond
the baseline's finite-residual adjacency classification; it is not a
new claim about the already-established ordinal embedding spectrum.
\end{remark}

% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 597-670
\section{Countable incompleteness and global characters (source 16)}\label{swo:ds:sec:gaps}

\merge\ Source~16's Section~6 up to its subsection on regular saturated
stages, which is printed in \cref{swo:vi:sub:ds-regular}.

% ---- source 16 resumes (Section 6)

Conditional Dedekind completeness means that every nonempty bounded-above
set has a supremum, equivalently every nonempty bounded-below set has an
infimum. It should not be confused with filling all small strict cuts,
with order density, or with the completeness of a separately adjoined
Dedekind completion.

\begin{theorem}[Every infinite stratum is incomplete]\label{swo:ds:thm:incomplete}
For every infinite linearly ordered set $X$ and every ordinal $\alpha$
with $|\alpha|=|X|$, $\WO_\alpha(X)$ is not conditionally Dedekind
complete. A countable bounded monotone family witnesses this failure.
The same conclusion holds for $\WO(X)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every infinite linear order contains an increasing or decreasing
$\omega$-sequence. For completeness, choose a countable set of distinct
points and color each pair of its indices according to the numerical
comparison; the infinite Ramsey theorem gives such a sequence.
First assume $a_0<a_1<a_2<\cdots$ in $X$, and put $p(n)=a_{2n}$.

For each $n\ge0$ choose $U_n$ extending the finite prefix
\[
 (a_0,a_2,\ldots,a_{2n-2},a_{2n+1}),
\]
and for $n\ge1$ choose $L_n$ extending
\[
 (a_0,a_2,\ldots,a_{2n-2},a_{2n-1}).
\]
All these prefixes extend to the prescribed type $\alpha$. First
disagreement gives $L_n<L_{n+1}$, $U_{n+1}<U_n$, and $L_n<U_m$ for
all $n,m$. Hence $(U_n)$ is bounded below.

Let $g$ be any lower bound. If it first differs from the infinite prefix
$p$ at a finite coordinate $j$, it must differ downward: an upward
difference would give $g>U_{j+1}$. Consequently $g<L_n$ for every
$n>j$, so it is not the greatest lower bound.

Otherwise $g$ agrees with all of $p$. Its remaining suffix contains all
odd labels $a_{2n+1}$. That suffix cannot be numerically strictly
decreasing: the positions of these increasing labels would form an
infinite descending sequence of ordinals. It therefore has an ascent.
Swap the two labels of such an ascent. The new enumeration has the same
type, is strictly above $g$, and still agrees with $p$ at every finite
coordinate. It remains below every $U_n$. Again $g$ is not greatest.
If no completion of $p$ with the specified type exists, this second case
simply never occurs.

Thus $(U_n)$ has no infimum. Reversing the numerical alphabet treats a
decreasing $\omega$-sequence and yields the dual failure of a supremum.
The argument for the full order is identical: swapping an ascent preserves
whatever ordinal type the candidate bound happens to have.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Global characters from the first label]\label{swo:ds:prop:globalcf}
If $X$ has no greatest element, then
\[
 \cf(\WO_\alpha(X))=\cf(\WO(X))=\cf(X)
\]
for every admissible infinite $\alpha$. There is a dual statement for
coinitiality when $X$ has no least element. In particular, for every
infinite cardinal cutoff $\kappa$, all the fixed strata and the full
order on $\No_{<\kappa}$ have global cofinality and coinitiality $\cf(\kappa)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Complete a cofinal family of possible first labels to enumerations. It is
cofinal in the lexicographic order. Conversely, the first labels of a
cofinal family of enumerations must be cofinal in $X$, or a larger first
label would put an enumeration above the entire family. For $\No_{<\kappa}$,
the ordinals below $\kappa$ are numerically cofinal and the negatives
coinitial. Any family with bounded birthdays is bounded numerically, so
the least possible size is $\cf(\kappa)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[\merge; global characters in sources~15, 17 and~18]\label{swo:vi:note:globalcf}
For the minimal slice of $\No_{<\kappa}$, the global part of
\cref{swo:ds:prop:globalcf} ($\cf=\ci=r$) is also proved by source~17
(\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}, with the same value for every cylinder in
\cref{swo:gs:lem:cylinder-base}), by source~15 (\cref{swo:cb:new:thm:local-characters})
and by source~18 (\cref{swo:sh:thm:new-minimal-characters}, also for
$\WO_{\mu+n}$, and \cref{swo:sh:lem:gap-cylinders} for cylinders), each by the
same first-label projection. Source~16's proposition is the general form: it
holds for every stratum and for the full order $\WO(X)$ of any alphabet
without a greatest element. Source~16's subsection ``Regular saturated
stages'', the last part of its Section~6, is printed in
\cref{swo:vi:sub:ds-regular} with the other gap spectra.
\end{note}

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 428-578
\section{The cut spectrum of the alphabet \texorpdfstring{$\No_{<\kappa}$}{No<kappa} (source 17)}
\label{swo:gs:sec:alphabet}

\merge\ Source~17's Section~4, printed whole. Its $\Sigma(L)$ is printed
$\GapChar(L)$ (the reals report's symbol), and its displayed equations keep
their numbers as (S17.$n$). At regular uncountable $\kappa$ its main theorem
is already in the repository (\cref{swo:vi:note:alphabet-univ}). Sources~15
and~18 prove the alphabet lemmas again; their statements follow in
\cref{swo:sh:lem:new-residual-alphabet,swo:cb:new:lem:power-sup-cofinality,swo:sh:lem:alphabet-gap-spectrum}.

% ---- source 17 resumes (Section 4)

For the remainder of the set-sized analysis, fix an infinite cardinal
$\kappa$, regarded as an initial ordinal, and write
\[\tag{S17.3}\label{swo:gs:eq:parameters}
 X=\No_{<\kappa},\qquad \mu=2^{<\kappa},\qquad
 r=\cf\kappa,\qquad q=\cf\mu.
\]
Here $\No_{<\kappa}$ consists of all sign sequences of ordinal length below
$\kappa$, ordered by first difference with $-<\text{termination}<+$.
We write $\Reg$ for the infinite regular cardinals.
No arithmetic closure of $X$ is assumed. In particular, no field hypothesis
is being inferred from the mere fact that $\kappa$ is a cardinal.

\begin{definition}[Characters and genuine gaps]\label{swo:gs:n4.1}
For a point $x$ of a dense order $L$ without endpoints, its left and right
characters are $\cf(L_{<x})$ and $\ci(L_{>x})$. A \emph{gap} is a partition
$L=A\sqcup B$ into nonempty initial and final parts, with $A<B$, such that
$A$ has no greatest element and $B$ no least element. Its character is
$(\cf A,\ci B)$; $\GapChar(L)$ denotes the set of these pairs. Thus a split
immediately before or after an actual point is not a gap. Cofinalities of
orders with a greatest element would be $1$, but do not occur in
$\GapChar(L)$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Size, intervals, and deletion}
\begin{lemma}\label{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size}
The order $X$ has cardinality $\mu$. Every nonempty open interval and every
nonempty open ray in $X$ has cardinality $\mu$. Consequently, deletion of
fewer than $\mu$ labels leaves an order-dense, cofinal and coinitial
suborder, with $\mu$ labels still in each nonempty interval and ray.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
There are $2^{|\alpha|}$ sign sequences of length $\alpha$, so
$|X|=\sum_{\alpha<\kappa}2^{|\alpha|}=2^{<\kappa}$; the supremum on the
right is at least $\kappa$ and absorbs the number of summands.
Given $a<b$, choose a sign sequence $z$ strictly between them, of length
below $\kappa$. A sufficiently long extension of $z$ fixes its comparisons
with $a,b$ before any subsequent signs. Hence an entire sign cone lies
inside $(a,b)$. If its fixed prefix has length $\delta<\kappa$, then all
extensions of lengths $\delta+\gamma$, $\gamma<\kappa$, remain below
$\kappa$: an infinite initial ordinal is closed under sums of two smaller
ordinals. This cone has $\mu$ elements. The same construction applies to
rays. Removing fewer than $\mu$ elements cannot empty a set of cardinality
$\mu$ and leaves its cardinality unchanged, even when $\mu$ is singular.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character}
The cofinality and coinitiality of $X$ are $r$, and every point of $X$ has
left and right character $r$. The same assertions hold in
$Y=X\setminus Z$ whenever $|Z|<\mu$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The ordinal surreals below $\kappa$ are cofinal, and a cofinal sequence of
ordinals of length $r$ gives an upper bound $r$. A family of fewer than
$r$ sign sequences has bounded birthdays below $\kappa$ and is bounded
above and below by ordinal surreals, proving the reverse bound.

At $x$, the sequences
\[
 x\cat-\cat+^\gamma\quad\text{and}\quad
 x\cat+\cat-^\gamma \qquad(\gamma<\kappa)
\]
are respectively cofinal below and coinitial above $x$; a cofinal choice
of $\gamma$'s of length $r$ suffices. Conversely, fewer than $r$ points
have bounded birthdays. A longer sign extension of $x$ lies between
$x$ and all the points on the appropriate side. Hence the character is
not smaller than $r$.

For $Y$, replace each member of a cofinal or coinitial sequence by a
remaining label in an appropriate interval. Such labels exist by
\Cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size}. Any smaller sequence cofinal in $Y$ would
also be cofinal in the relevant interval or ray of $X$, by density.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The alphabet's own gaps}
The alphabet is not Dedekind complete. Even at a regular uncountable
cutoff, its gaps need not have two equal characters. Distinguishing this
fact from its uniform \emph{point} characters is essential.

\begin{theorem}[Cut spectrum of the birthday alphabet]\label{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps}
\[
 \GapChar(X)=\{(r,r)\}\ \cup\
 \{(\theta,r),(r,\theta):\theta\in\Reg,\ \theta<\kappa\}.
\]
If $|Z|<\mu$, the same formula holds for $X\setminus Z$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $(A,B)$ be a gap of $X$. Define a sign sequence $s$ of length $\kappa$
by set recursion: at stage $\alpha$, the already defined prefix
$s\res\alpha$ is in $X$, so put $s(\alpha)=+$ if that prefix belongs to
$A$, and $s(\alpha)=-$ if it belongs to $B$. Put
\[
 I_+=\{\alpha<\kappa:s(\alpha)=+\},\qquad
 I_-=\{\alpha<\kappa:s(\alpha)=-\}.
\]
The nodes $s\res\alpha$ for $\alpha\in I_+$ increase and are cofinal in
$A$; the nodes for $\alpha\in I_-$ decrease and are coinitial in $B$.
To verify cofinality, compare any $x\in A$ with the path $s$. If $x$ is a
path prefix it is one of the displayed lower nodes. Otherwise the first
disagreement cannot place $x$ to the right of the chosen path: at a minus
choice the corresponding node is in $B$. It therefore places $x$ below a
lower node. The dual argument handles $B$.

Neither index set has a greatest element, since the cut is a gap. At
least one is unbounded in $\kappa$ and has cofinality $r$. An unbounded
other side also has cofinality $r$; a bounded other side has an infinite
regular cofinality $\theta<\kappa$. This proves the upper inclusion.
Conversely, the path $+^\theta\cat-^{\kappa}$, with the evident length
$\kappa$ interpretation, has lower and upper index cofinalities
$(\theta,r)$. Reversing signs gives $(r,\theta)$. A path with both signs
cofinal in $\kappa$ gives $(r,r)$. In each case the induced partition has
no endpoint, as the associated node sequences have no last member.

Now let $Y=X\setminus Z$ be as stated. Density of $Y$ implies that a gap
of $Y$ corresponds either to a gap of $X$ or to a single removed point of
$X$: there cannot be two $X$-points between the two sides, because a
$Y$-point would lie between them. At an $X$-gap, density preserves both
cofinalities by choosing intermediate $Y$-points. At a removed point both
characters are $r$ by \Cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character}. Conversely, every
$X$-gap survives restriction to $Y$, with the same characters. This proves
the deletion assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A warning about equal-character guesses]\label{swo:gs:n4.5}
For $\kappa>\omega$, the path which first has $\omega$ plus signs and then
only minus signs determines an $(\omega,r)$ gap. It lies at a boundary of
a limit sign cone. The cone itself contains many surreal numbers; none is
its missing boundary point. Thus uniform point character $r$ does not mean
that every gap has character $(r,r)$.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}\label{swo:gs:lem:cofinality-inequality}
One has $\mu\ge\kappa$ and $q\ge r$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first assertion follows from the count in
\Cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size}. If the increasing cardinal sequence
$2^\nu$, $\nu<\kappa$, never reaches its supremum $\mu$, a purported
cofinal family in $\mu$ of size below $r$ is bounded by some $2^\nu$:
choose an index bound for each of its members and then use
$\cf\kappa=r$ to bound all those indices. Thus $\cf\mu\ge r$.
If the supremum is attained, the powers are constantly $\mu$ for all
sufficiently large cardinals $\nu<\kappa$. K\"onig's inequality
$\cf(2^\nu)>\nu$ then gives $\cf\mu\ge\kappa$. For completeness, that
inequality follows from $\rho^{\cf\rho}>\rho$ for infinite cardinals
$\rho$, applied to $\rho=2^\nu$: if $\cf\rho\le\nu$, then
$\rho^{\cf\rho}\le(2^\nu)^\nu=2^\nu$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[\merge; the repository's case of \cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps}]\label{swo:vi:note:alphabet-univ}
At a regular uncountable cutoff $\kappa$, \cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps} is
the case $M=N$ of the cutoff gap decomposition of the report
\path{surreal-fields-across-universes} (\code{univ:gs:lem:boundary}), quoted in
\path{birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets} (\code{hset:rem:cutoffgaps}): with
no fresh signs, the gap pairs of $\No_{<\kappa}$ are $(\theta,\kappa)$ and
$(\kappa,\theta)$ for every infinite regular $\theta\le\kappa$, realized by the
same sign paths $+^\theta\cat-^\kappa$. Source~17 does not cite these reports.
New in source~17 are the singular cutoffs, where the bounded side ranges over
the regular $\theta<\kappa$ while the unbounded side has character
$r=\cf\kappa<\kappa$; the countable cutoff $\kappa=\omega$, where only
$(\omega,\omega)$ occurs; and the deletion clause.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~18, Lemmas~4.5 and~5.3, and source~15, Lemma~9.1]\label{swo:sh:lem:new-residual-alphabet}\label{swo:sh:lem:gap-small-deletion}\label{swo:cb:new:lem:full-size-intervals}
Source~18's Lemma~4.5 (``Large intervals and unchanged cut sides after
deletion''): \emph{every nonempty numerical open interval in $\No_{<\kappa}$
has cardinality $\mu$; if $U\subseteq\No_{<\kappa}$ has cardinality less than
$\mu$, then $\No_{<\kappa}\setminus U$ meets every such interval; in
particular (1) $\cf(\No_{<\kappa})=\ci(\No_{<\kappa})=r$, (2) immediately below
or above any point the cofinality or coinitiality is $r$, and (3) every
nonprincipal side of a cut retains its cofinality or coinitiality after
deletion of $U$, as do the two open sides at a point, even a deleted one}
(an endpoint retained in the residual alphabet still gives a principal side
of character~$1$). Its Lemma~5.3 (``Small deletions preserve order
density'') repeats this for $R=X\setminus U$ and adds that \emph{every proper
gap of $R$ has character in $\GapChar(X)\cup\{(r,r)\}$}. Source~15's Lemma~9.1
(``Full-size numerical intervals''): \emph{every nonempty open interval of
$\No_{<\kappa}$, and every nonempty open ray, contains a sign-prefix cone
order-isomorphic to $\No_{<\kappa}$; in particular each has cardinality $\mu$.}
These are \cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size,swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character} and the
deletion clause of \cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps}, by the same sign-cone
argument. Source~15 makes the cone explicit in three cases (neither endpoint a
prefix of the other: take $a\cat+$; $a$ a proper prefix of $b$: take
$b\cat-$; $b$ a proper prefix of $a$: take $a\cat+$), and source~18 obtains
the cone from the birthday-controlled separator theorem
(\cref{swo:sk:sec:toolkit}) and adds an explicit approach to a point from
below by $x\cat-\cat+^\gamma$, $\gamma$ cofinal in $\kappa$, and from above by
the sign-reversed words.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~15, Lemma~9.3, and source~18, Lemma~4.6]\label{swo:cb:new:lem:power-sup-cofinality}\label{swo:sh:lem:new-cardinal-cofinalities}
Both sources state more than \cref{swo:gs:lem:cofinality-inequality}:
\emph{$q\geq r$; if the supremum $2^{<\kappa}$ is not attained then $q=r$, and
if it is attained, $2^\lambda=\mu$ for some cardinal $\lambda<\kappa$, then
$q\geq\kappa$.} The proofs are source~17's: cofinal exponents give $q\le r$ in
the unattained case, a shorter cofinal family would have bounded exponent
witnesses, and in the attained case K\"onig's inequality
$\cf(2^\lambda)>\lambda$ for all $\lambda$ between the attaining exponent and
$\kappa$ gives $q\ge\kappa$.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~18, Lemma~5.2, ``Alphabet gap spectrum'']\label{swo:sh:lem:alphabet-gap-spectrum}
\emph{For $X=\No_{<\kappa}$, $\GapChar(X)=\{(r,r)\}\cup\{(\theta,r),(r,\theta):
\theta<\kappa$ an infinite regular cardinal$\}$; every point has left and right
character $r$, and the global cofinality and coinitiality are also $r$.} This
is \cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps} with
\cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character}, by the same recursion $s(\alpha)=+$ if and
only if $s\res\alpha\in A$. Source~18 adds the warning that the lemma
``corrects a tempting but false simplification: unfilled alphabet cuts need
not all have character $(r,r)$''; at $\kappa=\omega_1$ the cut below all
positive infinite elements, represented by $+^\omega-^{\omega_1}$, has
character $(\omega,\omega_1)$, and ``the asymmetric alphabet gaps will,
however, already be covered by the longer-prefix construction in $P$''. This
is the point of \cref{swo:gs:n4.5}.
\end{note}

% ---- hand chunk minimal slice
\section{The minimal slice \texorpdfstring{$\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$}{W\_mu(No<kappa)} (source 17; routes of sources 15, 16 and 18)}\label{swo:vi:sec:minimal}

\merge\ Source~17's Sections~5 and~6 are printed in their order, with two
insertions: source~18's general permutation principle
(\cref{swo:vi:sub:sh-general}), stated before source~17's exact gap spectrum
because that theorem and source~16's are instances of it, and source~18's
multiplicities (\cref{swo:vi:sub:sh-mult}). The second and third routes to the
characters and the saturation threshold (sources~15 and~18) follow source~17's
saturation corollary. Source~17 writes $[p]$ for the cylinder of an injective
prefix $p$; sources~16 and~18 write $C_p$ for the same set. All three call a
cylinder meeting both sides of a cut \emph{straddling}.

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 579-656
\subsection{Exact local and topological invariants of the minimal slice (source 17)}
\label{swo:gs:sec:topology}

Put $P=\WO_\mu(X)$. For an injective prefix $p:\alpha\to X$ with
$\alpha<\mu$, let
\[
 [p]=\{f\in P:f\res\alpha=p\},\qquad Y_p=X\setminus\ran p.
\]
The set $Y_p$ has cardinality $\mu$. Every such prefix has a completion:
the remaining ordinal interval $[\alpha,\mu)$ has order type $\mu$, so a
bijection from it to $Y_p$ suffices. This elementary extension fact would
be false for arbitrary prescribed prefixes of length $\mu$.

\begin{lemma}[The cylinder base]\label{swo:gs:lem:cylinder-base}
Every $[p]$ is nonempty, convex, and clopen in the order topology of $P$.
The cylinders form a base. No cylinder has an endpoint. Moreover,
\[
 \cf[p]=\ci[p]=r.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Convexity is the first-difference property. Given $f\in[p]$, change its
entry at coordinate $\alpha$ to a fresh smaller or larger label and then
complete. \Cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size} provides such labels after deletion
of $f[\alpha]$. This produces bounds on either side of $f$ within $[p]$,
proving openness and absence of endpoints. All length-$\alpha$ cylinders
partition $P$, so the complement is also open. For $a<f<b$, a prefix of
$f$ extending beyond its first differences with $a,b$ has its entire
cylinder inside $(a,b)$. This proves the base assertion.

The first unused coordinate maps the cylinder onto $Y_p$ in lexicographic
blocks. Choosing one completion for cofinally many first unused labels
shows $\cf[p]\le\cf Y_p=r$. Conversely, the first unused entries of a
cofinal family in $[p]$ must be cofinal in $Y_p$, since otherwise a larger
entry starts a cylinder above the entire family. Coinitiality is dual.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Characters and size invariants]\label{swo:gs:thm:invariants}
Every point $f\in P$ has character $(q,q)$, and
\[\tag{S17.4}\label{swo:gs:eq:invariants}
 \cf P=\ci P=r,\qquad \dens(P)=\weight(P)=\cell(P)=\mu^{<\mu}.
\]
Here $\dens$ is density, $\weight$ is topological weight, and $\cell$ is cellularity
\emph{as the supremum} of cardinalities of pairwise disjoint families of
nonempty open sets. In particular, $P$ is zero-dimensional. The formula
for $\cell$ does not assert that its supremum is always attained by one family.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For each coordinate $\delta<\mu$, choose $f^-_\delta<f<f^+_\delta$ whose
first difference from $f$ is exactly $\delta$. Such choices are possible
by the preceding lemma. Along a cofinal set of coordinates of order type
$q$, the lower choices are increasing and cofinal below $f$, and the
upper choices are decreasing and coinitial above it. Conversely, for a
family of fewer than $q$ elements below $f$, the first-difference
coordinates are bounded below $\mu$. A lower approximation differing
later lies above them all. Thus neither point character is below $q$.
The global cofinalities are \Cref{swo:gs:lem:cylinder-base} at the root.

The number of prefixes is at most
$\sum_{\alpha<\mu}\mu^{|\alpha|}=\mu^{<\mu}$, giving that upper bound
for weight, density, and cellularity. At each fixed length $\alpha$, the
cylinders are disjoint nonempty open sets. For $1\le\lambda<\mu$, the
number of injective $\lambda$-words over $X$ is $\mu^\lambda$: a fixed
injection $j:\lambda\times X\to X$ sends any function $h$ to the injection
$\xi\mapsto j(\xi,h(\xi))$. Hence both density and cellularity are at least
$\mu^\lambda$ for every such $\lambda$. Taking suprema gives the reverse
bound. Finally $d\le w$ and $c\le w$ complete the equalities. The clopen
base gives zero-dimensionality.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{swo:gs:n5.3}
The point character is controlled by $\cf\mu$, whereas the cofinality of
a whole cylinder is controlled by $\cf\kappa$. These can differ.
Confusing the two would lose the central feature of the cut classification
below. Hereditary density also equals $\mu^{<\mu}$, since every subspace
has weight at most $\weight(P)$ and $P$ itself attains this density.
\end{remark}

\begin{note}[Source~17, Proposition~5.4, ``Set-alphabet background, with a direct proof'']\label{swo:gs:prop:local-universality}
\emph{The order $P$ has cardinality $2^\mu$. Every linear order of cardinality
at most $\mu$ embeds into every nonempty open interval of $P$. An ordinal
$\beta$ embeds in $P$ if and only if $\beta<\mu^+$; the same bound holds for
reverse well-ordered suborders.} Source~17 calls these ``already part of the
source's theory''. They are the set baseline (\cref{swo:sw:setspectrum};
reals report \code{lwo:prop:cardinality}, \code{lwo:thm:ordinal-spectrum},
\code{lwo:sp:thm:universality}) applied inside a cylinder, whose residual
alphabet has size $\mu$, together with \cref{swo:gs:lem:cylinder-base}; the
proof is the pair-orientation, initial-segment and coordinate-stabilization
argument of \cref{swo:sw:fixed}.
\end{note}

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 696-724
\subsubsection{Fusion with an exhaustiveness requirement}
\begin{theorem}[A dense-open fusion theorem]\label{swo:gs:thm:fusion}
The intersection of fewer than $q$ open sets is open. The intersection of
at most $q$ dense open subsets of $P$ is dense. In particular, when
$q=\omega$, $P$ is a Baire space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At a point in fewer than $q$ open sets, choose a cylinder neighborhood for
each. Their prefix lengths have supremum below $\mu$, so the longest
union prefix gives a cylinder inside the intersection.

Let $(U_i)_{i<q}$ be dense open sets and start with any nonempty cylinder.
Write $X=\bigcup_{i<q}X_i$, where the $X_i$ are increasing and each has
cardinality below $\mu$. Recursively refine the current prefix to a
cylinder contained in $U_i$, using density and the cylinder base, and then
append every member of $X_i$ not yet used, in a set well-order of length
below $\mu$. At stages below $q$, the accumulated length is still below
$\mu$, including at limit stages. At the end the union has length exactly
$\mu$ and range $X$: its length is at most $\mu$, and it has used $\mu$
distinct labels. It is therefore an element of $P$, belongs to every
$U_i$, and extends the starting prefix. Fewer dense open sets can be
padded by copies of $P$.
\end{proof}

The coverage step is essential. Bare prefix fusion of length $\mu$ can
produce an injective word omitting a label, which is not a point of $P$.
The theorem does not claim a complete metrizability or a general
isomorphism classification.

\begin{note}[\merge; repository analogues and the regular size-matched case]\label{swo:vi:note:slice-analogues}
For regular $\kappa$ with $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$ one has $\mu=r=q=\kappa$, and the
character part of \cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants} is source~11's
\cref{swo:sw:permutationeta} (every local and global character $\kappa$, and
$\eta_\kappa$). For $X=\mathbb R$ the reals report proves the analogues:
point characters $\cf\mathfrak c$ and global characters $\omega$
(\code{lwo:prop:fixed-topology}), weight $2^{<\mathfrak c}$
(\code{lwo:sp:prop:minimalweight}), and that fewer than $\cf\mathfrak c$ open sets
have open intersection (\code{lwo:rk:cor:Pspace}). Sources~15, 17 and~18 do not
cite these. New in sources~15, 17 and~18 is the alphabet $\No_{<\kappa}$ at
every infinite cardinal $\kappa$, in particular the separation of the point
character $q=\cf\mu$ from the global character $r=\cf\kappa$; new in
source~17 is the second half of \cref{swo:gs:thm:fusion}, where every
fusion sequence must also exhaust the alphabet.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~15, Theorem~9.2, ``Local and global cofinalities'']\label{swo:cb:new:thm:local-characters}
Source~15 writes $L=\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ for $P$, and
$\operatorname{cf}^-(f)$, $\operatorname{ci}^+(f)$ for the cofinality of
$L_{<f}$ and the coinitiality of $L_{>f}$. \emph{For every $f\in L$,
$\operatorname{cf}^{-}(f)=\operatorname{ci}^{+}(f)=\cf\mu$; the global
cofinality and coinitiality are $\cf\kappa$; consequently the ordinary local
topological character of every point of $L$ is $\cf\mu$.} This is the
character part of \cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}, by the same argument (a swap of
the entry at $\eta$ with a later occurrence of a fresh smaller or larger label
gives $f^\mp_\eta$ with first difference exactly $\eta$; cofinally many $\eta$
suffice, fewer than $\cf\mu$ first differences are bounded). Source~15 adds the
local base of size $\cf\mu$ formed by pairs from the two sides, and the
examples: ``at the cutoff $\kappa=\omega_1$ the alphabet cardinal is
$\mu=2^{\aleph_0}$. The global cofinality is always $\omega_1$, whereas the
local character is $\cf(2^{\aleph_0})$. Under cardinal arithmetic making these
cardinals different, the distinction survives at every point of the
permutation order. At the singular strong-limit cutoff $\kappa=\beth_\omega$,
every point has countable local character, even though the density and
cellularity of the minimal slice are $2^\kappa$.''
\end{note}

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 725-830
\subsection{The complete nonprincipal cut spectrum of the minimal slice (source 17)}
\label{swo:gs:sec:cuts}

\subsubsection{Three ways that a cut can occur}
For a gap $(A,B)$ of $P$, say that a cylinder $[p]$ \emph{straddles} the
cut when it meets both $A$ and $B$. There is at most one straddling cylinder
at any fixed length, because the cylinders at that length are disjoint
convex blocks.

\begin{lemma}[Cut trichotomy]\label{swo:gs:lem:cut-trichotomy}
Every gap of $P$ is of exactly one of the following structural forms,
using the first stage at which the construction stops.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{A coordinate cut.} Some straddling $[p]$, of length
$\alpha<\mu$, has no straddling child. Its children are partitioned by a
proper cut of the residual alphabet $Y_p$.
\item \textbf{A limit-prefix boundary.} At a nonzero limit
$\delta<\mu$, all shorter prefixes on a coherent path straddle, but the
union cylinder $[p]$ lies wholly on one side. The cut is exactly the left
or right boundary of $[p]$.
\item \textbf{An omitted-range branch.} A coherent prefix straddles at
every length below $\mu$. Their union is an injection
$f:\mu\to X$ which is not surjective, and the gap is comparison with $f$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Begin at the empty prefix, whose cylinder straddles. At a successor stage,
continue into the unique straddling child when it exists; otherwise every
child belongs wholly to one side, giving case 1. At a limit
$\delta<\mu$, the union is an injective prefix and has a nonempty cylinder.
If that cylinder does not straddle, it is wholly on one side. Every word
strictly below it differs at an earlier coordinate, and is below a lower
member of an earlier straddling cylinder, so belongs to $A$. The dual
argument holds above the cylinder. This proves exactly the boundary
assertion in case 2.

If the process continues through all lengths below $\mu$, let $f$ be the
union injection. For any $g\in P$ different from $f$, its first difference
determines its cut side: a later straddling cylinder gives witnesses which
force the orientation. If $f$ were surjective it would itself belong to
$P$. Whichever side contains it would then have an endpoint, contrary to
the gap hypothesis. Thus $f$ is not surjective, and all words in $P$ are
classified by their comparison with it. These alternatives cover the
set recursion through $\mu$, and are disjoint by the first stopping stage.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Characters in the three cases]\label{swo:gs:lem:cut-characters}
In case 1, the gap character belongs to $\GapChar(X)\cup\{(r,r)\}$.
In case 2 it is respectively $(\cf\delta,r)$ or $(r,\cf\delta)$.
In case 3 it is $(q,q)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In case 1 partition the residual alphabet into nonempty parts $U<V$,
according as a child lies on the lower or upper side. If $U$ has no
largest label, the lower character is $\cf U$; choose one point in
cofinally many children. If $U$ has largest label $u$, the last lower
child is cofinal on the lower side and has cofinality $r$. The upper side
has the dual description. When neither side has an endpoint,
\Cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps} applies to $Y_p$. When one has an endpoint,
the other has character $r$ by \Cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character}; the
endpoint side is replaced by an entire child of character $r$.

At the left boundary of a limit cylinder $[p]$, lower approximations
obtained by a smaller fresh entry at coordinates cofinal in $\delta$ are
cofinal below the cylinder. No family of fewer than $\cf\delta$ can be
cofinal, since its first disagreements with $p$ would be bounded below
$\delta$. The upper side has $[p]$ as a coinitial subset, and the cylinder
has coinitiality $r$. The right boundary is dual.

For an omitted-range branch, approximations from either side at cofinally
many coordinates give upper bounds $q$ for both characters. Fewer than
$q$ disagreements are bounded, so a later approximation is closer to the
branch. This proves equality.
\end{proof}

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1282-1317
\subsection{A general permutation-order principle (source 18)}
\label{swo:vi:sub:sh-general}

The tree argument is not specific to surreal numbers.  It separates
the contribution of the alphabet from the new cuts introduced by
long prefixes and omitted branches.

\begin{theorem}[Gap spectrum under permutation expansion]
\label{swo:sh:thm:general-permutation-gap-spectrum}
Let $D$ be a dense linear order without endpoints of infinite
cardinality $\mu$.  Assume that every nonempty open interval has
cardinality $\mu$, every point has the same two-sided character
$\nu$, and both global cofinalities are also $\nu$.  Let
$\operatorname{Perm}_\mu(D)$ be the lexicographic order on bijections
$\mu\to D$, and put $q=\operatorname{cf}\mu$.  Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
\GapChar(\operatorname{Perm}_\mu(D))
={}&\GapChar(D)
 \cup\{(\nu,\nu),(q,q)\}\\
 &{}\cup\{(\theta,\nu),(\nu,\theta):
       \theta<\mu\text{ is an infinite regular cardinal}\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Small deletions leave an order dense in $D$, so the residual-cut
analysis and the straddling-cylinder trichotomy apply verbatim.
These give the upper bound.  All the explicitly added pairs are
attained by the same prefix-boundary and omitted-branch constructions.
Finally, each gap of $D$ lifts to a gap of the permutation order by
placing a permutation on the left or right according to its first
digit.  With neither digit side principal, the two characters are
unchanged.  This gives the reverse inclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[\merge\ The steps the proof leaves to the reader]
Source~18 writes ``apply verbatim''; the two facts it uses are written out
here. Put $P=\operatorname{Perm}_\mu(D)$.

\emph{Small deletions add only $(\nu,\nu)$.} Let $U\subseteq D$ with
$\card U<\mu$ and $R=D\setminus U$. Every interval of $D$ has $\mu$ points, so
$R$ meets every interval of $D$; in particular $R$ is dense, has no endpoints,
and has global cofinalities $\nu$ and point characters $(\nu,\nu)$, because
cofinal and coinitial families transfer between $D$ and $R$ through points of
the other order. Let $(A,B)$ be a gap of $R$. Two points of $D$ cannot lie
between $A$ and $B$, since a point of $R$ would lie between them. If no point
of $D$ lies between them, the downward closure of $A$ and the upward closure of
$B$ in $D$ form a gap of $D$ with the same characters. If one point $x$ of $D$
lies between them, then $x\in U$, $A$ is cofinal in $D_{<x}$ and $B$ coinitial in
$D_{>x}$, and the character is the point character $(\nu,\nu)$ of $x$. Hence
$\GapChar(R)\subseteq\GapChar(D)\cup\{(\nu,\nu)\}$.

\emph{The three cases.} Let $(A,B)$ be a gap of $P$. The prefixes $p$ of
length below $\mu$ whose cylinders $C_p$ meet both sides form a chain under
extension, as in \cref{swo:gs:lem:cut-trichotomy}. If there is a longest one,
$p$, the residual alphabet $R_p=D\setminus\ran p$ (with $\card{\ran p}<\mu$) is
cut into the digits whose child cylinders lie in $A$ and those whose child
cylinders lie in $B$; both parts are nonempty and, $R_p$ being dense, they do
not both have endpoints. If neither has one, the character of $(A,B)$ is the
character of this gap of $R_p$, which lies in $\GapChar(D)\cup\{(\nu,\nu)\}$ by
the first step: $A\cap C_p$ is cofinal in $A$ and is the lexicographic sum of
the lower child cylinders. If the lower part has a last digit $u$, $A$ is
cofinally the child cylinder $C_{p\cat u}$, whose cofinality is that of its
residual alphabet, $\nu$, while $B$ approaches $u$ from above in $R_p$, with
coinitiality $\nu$; the dual case is the same, so the character is
$(\nu,\nu)$. If the straddling lengths have a limit supremum $\delta<\mu$, the
union $p$ has a nonempty cylinder lying on one side, the gap is a boundary of
$C_p$, and its character is $(\cf\delta,\nu)$ or $(\nu,\cf\delta)$: the
approximations with a smaller (larger) unused digit at coordinates cofinal in
$\delta$ exist because rays of $D$ contain intervals of size $\mu$, and $C_p$ has
global characters $\nu$. If the lengths are cofinal in $\mu$, the union is a
nonsurjective injection $\mu\to D$ and the character is $(q,q)$, exactly as in
case~3 of \cref{swo:gs:lem:cut-characters}. This proves the upper bound.

\emph{Attainment.} For a regular $\theta<\mu$, the two boundaries of the
cylinder of an injective prefix of length $\theta$ have characters $(\theta,\nu)$
and $(\nu,\theta)$; the lower boundary of a one-digit cylinder
$C_{\langle x\rangle}$ has character $(\nu,\nu)$, since its lower side
approaches the digit $x$ from below and $C_{\langle x\rangle}$ has coinitiality
$\nu$; a bijection of $\mu$ onto $D\setminus\{x\}$ gives $(q,q)$; and a gap of $D$
lifts at the first digit, as source~18 says.
\end{proof}

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 725-830 (chunk lines 77-107)
\begin{theorem}[Exact gap spectrum]\label{swo:gs:thm:gap-spectrum}
For every infinite cardinal $\kappa$, with the parameters in
\eqref{swo:gs:eq:parameters},
\[\tag{S17.5}\label{swo:gs:eq:gap-spectrum}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \GapChar\bigl(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\bigr)
 =\{(r,r),(q,q)\}\ \cup\
 \{(\theta,r),(r,\theta):\theta\in\Reg,\ \theta<\mu\}.}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The upper inclusion follows from the two preceding lemmas and
$\kappa\le\mu$: alphabet gap pairs involving a regular cardinal below
$\kappa$ already belong to the displayed set.

For $(r,r)$, fix a label $x\in X$ and place on the lower side every word
whose first entry is at most $x$, and on the upper side every word whose
first entry exceeds $x$. The last lower child has cofinality $r$, and the
upper first-entry ray has coinitiality $r$. Neither side has an endpoint.

For any infinite regular $\theta<\mu$, choose an injective prefix of
length $\theta$. Its two boundary cuts have characters $(\theta,r)$ and
$(r,\theta)$ by the limit-boundary calculation. The fresh smaller and
larger labels used there exist at every coordinate, so both sides of
both cuts are nonempty.

Finally choose a bijection $f:\mu\to X\setminus\{x\}$ for some label
$x$. Each proper prefix is completable in $P$, but $f$ itself is not in
$P$. Its comparison cut is a gap of character $(q,q)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[\merge; \cref{swo:gs:thm:gap-spectrum} as an instance]\label{swo:vi:note:gs-instance}
$D=\No_{<\kappa}$ satisfies the hypotheses of
\cref{swo:sh:thm:general-permutation-gap-spectrum} with $\nu=r$:
\cref{swo:gs:lem:alphabet-size,swo:gs:lem:alphabet-character} give the interval
size and the uniform point and global characters. With
$\GapChar(\No_{<\kappa})$ from \cref{swo:gs:thm:alphabet-gaps}, whose
asymmetric pairs ($\theta<\kappa$) are among those with $\theta<\mu$, the
principle returns exactly \eqref{swo:gs:eq:gap-spectrum}. Source~17's proof
above is the direct one.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~18, Theorem~5.1, ``Exact gap spectrum'', and Lemma~5.4]\label{swo:sh:thm:exact-gap-spectrum}\label{swo:sh:lem:gap-cylinders}\label{swo:sh:sec:exact-gap-spectrum}
Source~18's Section~5 ``settles the cut-spectrum question for the
minimal-length permutation order at every infinite cardinal birthday cutoff''.
It writes $P$ for the bijections $\mu\to\No_{<\kappa}$, defines a
\emph{proper Dedekind gap} as a partition $P=A\sqcup B$ into nonempty sets with
$A<B$, $A$ without greatest and $B$ without least element, of character
