$(\cf A,\ci B)$, and writes $\GapChar(P)$ for the set of characters (``this
convention excludes principal cuts and the two external ends''). Its
Theorem~5.1 is \eqref{swo:gs:eq:gap-spectrum} together with: \emph{every
displayed pair is attained; (1) every point of $P$ has left and right
character $q$; (2) the global cofinality and coinitiality are $r$; (3) the
principal cuts have character $(1,q)$ or $(q,1)$; (4) if empty sides are
assigned cofinality $0$, the external ends have character $(0,r)$ and $(r,0)$.}
Its Lemma~5.4: \emph{every cylinder $C_p$ has cofinality and coinitiality $r$
and no endpoints; if $p$ has nonzero limit length $\delta<\mu$, the lower
boundary of $C_p$ is a proper gap of character $(\cf\delta,r)$ and the upper
boundary one of character $(r,\cf\delta)$.} These are
\cref{swo:gs:lem:cylinder-base}, case~2 of \cref{swo:gs:lem:cut-characters} and
\cref{swo:gs:thm:gap-spectrum,swo:gs:thm:invariants}, and source~18's proof is
source~17's: the straddling prefixes form a chain under extension, and its
three cases (a longest straddling prefix, a limit supremum $\delta<\mu$, lengths
cofinal in $\mu$) are the three cases of \cref{swo:gs:lem:cut-trichotomy}, in
the same order, with the same character computations. Source~18 attains
$(r,r)$ by the boundary of a successor-length cylinder
$C_{\langle x\rangle}$ (the exterior side approaches the digit $x$) rather than
by source~17's first-entry split; ``every infinite regular $\theta<\mu$ can be
used as $\delta$'' in Lemma~5.4, so the asymmetric pairs are attained
``independently of which of them already occur as gaps in the alphabet''.
\end{note}

% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 671-734
\subsection{Source 16's route: regular saturated stages over any saturated alphabet}
\label{swo:vi:sub:ds-regular}

\merge\ The last subsection of source~16's Section~6. It is printed here,
after source~17's spectrum, because it computes the same spectrum for the
regular size-matched case and generalizes it to every regular saturated
alphabet of the same size (\cref{swo:vi:note:ds-instance}).

% ---- source 16 resumes (Subsection 6.1)

Suppose $\mu$ is regular infinite and $X$ is an $\eta_\mu$ order of
size $\mu$. The earlier report proves that $\WO_\mu(X)$ is itself
$\eta_\mu$, with every local and global cofinality and coinitiality
$\mu$ (\cref{swo:sw:permutationeta}).
Here is the mechanism. If one side is empty, choose a fresh first label
beyond all first labels on the other side and complete it. For two
nonempty families of fewer than $\mu$ lower and upper enumerations,
bound their cross first disagreements below $\mu$.
Follow their forced common coordinates until the active next labels have
strictly separated lower and upper sets. Choose a fresh interpolating
label using saturation, and complete the resulting prefix. Fewer than
$\mu$ used labels can be avoided by a further small cut. The procedure
must stop before the bound is passed. Small cut filling gives the lower
character bound; \Cref{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum} gives the upper bound.

\begin{theorem}[Complete gap-character spectrum]\label{swo:ds:thm:gap-spectrum}
Under the preceding hypotheses, the cofinality pairs of proper unfilled
Dedekind cuts in $\WO_\mu(X)$ are exactly
\[
 (\mu,\lambda)\quad\text{and}\quad(\lambda,\mu),
 \qquad \omega\le\lambda\le\mu,\quad\lambda\text{ regular}.
\tag{S16.4}\label{swo:ds:eq:gap-spectrum}
\]
Both sides of a cut here are nonempty, and neither has an endpoint.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The two characters are infinite regular cardinals. Neither exceeds
$\mu$, because a larger character would produce a monotone sequence
of type $\mu^+$, contrary to \Cref{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum}. They cannot
both be below $\mu$: cofinal and coinitial sets of those sizes would
form a strict small cut, and saturation would insert a point in the
supposed unfilled cut. This proves necessity.

For an infinite regular $\lambda<\mu$, fix an injective prefix $p$ of
length $\lambda$. Its completion cylinder $C_p$ is convex and has no
endpoints; its cofinality and coinitiality are $\mu$, by the same
saturation argument. The cut just above the entire cylinder has lower
character $\mu$. Enumerations above the cylinder first differ upward
from $p$ at some coordinate below $\lambda$. Choose such upward
deviations at a cofinal sequence of coordinates in $\lambda$; their
completions form a coinitial family of size $\lambda$ on that upper
side. Fewer than $\lambda$ deviation coordinates are bounded, and a
later upward deviation lies below all of them. Thus the upper character
is exactly $\lambda$. The cylinder has no maximum and the upper side
has no minimum, so this cut is unfilled. The lower edge gives
$(\lambda,\mu)$.

For $(\mu,\mu)$ fix $a\in X$ and split the order according to
first label $\le a$ or $>a$. The lower side is cofinally the cylinder
with first label $a$, of character $\mu$. The upper side has
coinitiality $\mu$, inherited from the native segment above $a$.
Both sides have no endpoint. This realizes the final case.
\end{proof}

For regular $\kappa$ satisfying $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, these conclusions
apply to $\No_{<\kappa}$. They answer the earlier report's regular-stage
density, cellularity, and cut-character questions under precisely those
hypotheses. Singular stages and regular stages of larger cardinality
require separate analysis; the theorem does not erase the singular
coding phenomenon in \eqref{swo:ds:eq:singular}.

\begin{note}[\merge; source~16's theorem as an instance]\label{swo:vi:note:ds-instance}
An $\eta_\mu$ order $X$ of regular cardinality $\mu$ satisfies the hypotheses
of \cref{swo:sh:thm:general-permutation-gap-spectrum} with $\nu=\cf\mu=\mu$:
it is dense without endpoints, its intervals have size $\mu$, and every point
and both ends have character $\mu$, by saturation and $\card X=\mu$. An
$\eta_\mu$ order has no gap with both characters below $\mu$, so the principle
gives the pairs $(\mu,\theta)$, $(\theta,\mu)$ with $\theta\le\mu$ regular,
which is \eqref{swo:ds:eq:gap-spectrum}. For $X=\No_{<\kappa}$ with $\kappa$
regular and $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, \cref{swo:ds:thm:gap-spectrum} and
\cref{swo:gs:n6.7} coincide. Source~17 is more general in the cutoff (any
$\kappa$, no hypothesis on $2^{<\kappa}$), source~16 in the alphabet (any
regular saturated alphabet). For $X=\mathbb R$ the reals report's
\code{lwo:rk:thm:Pgaps} is the corresponding spectrum,
$\{(\omega,\theta),(\theta,\omega):\theta<\mathfrak c\text{ regular}\}
\cup\{(\cf\mathfrak c,\cf\mathfrak c)\}$; it is source~17's formula with
$r=\omega$, $q=\cf\mathfrak c$, $\mu=\mathfrak c$ and the alphabet spectrum
$\GapChar(\mathbb R)=\varnothing$ (the formula's $(r,r)$ comes from a principal
digit split, and $(\omega,\omega)$ is the case $\theta=\omega$). Neither
source~16 nor source~17 cites it.
\end{note}

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1318-1479
\subsection{How many gaps have each character? (source 18)}
\label{swo:vi:sub:sh-mult}

\merge\ ``The same classification'' and ``the three cases'' below are the
straddling-cylinder classification of the gaps of $P$: a longest straddling
prefix, a limit supremum of straddling lengths below $\mu$, and lengths
cofinal in $\mu$, which are cases~1, 2 and~3 of \cref{swo:gs:lem:cut-trichotomy}
(\cref{swo:sh:thm:exact-gap-spectrum}). Source~18's $d_*$ is the density
$\mu^{<\mu}$ of $P$ (\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}); its prefix variable $q$ is
printed $p'$, since $q=\cf\mu$ here. The theorem is the minimal-slice
analogue of the reals report's count of $2^{\mathfrak c}$ gaps of $\W$
(\code{lwo:sp:thm:gapcount}), which source~18 does not cite.

The same classification gives exact multiplicities, not just a list
of the possible character pairs.  Write
\[
d_*=\mu^{<\mu}=\sup_{\lambda<\mu}\mu^{|\lambda|}.
\]
This is also the cardinality of the tree of injective prefixes of
length less than $\mu$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact multiplicities]\label{swo:sh:thm:gap-multiplicities}
The minimal permutation order $P$ has exactly $2^\mu$ proper
Dedekind gaps.  For a pair $(a,b)$ in the spectrum of
\Cref{swo:sh:thm:exact-gap-spectrum}, let $G_{a,b}$ be the set of
proper gaps of that character.  Then
\[
\boxed{
|G_{a,b}|=\begin{cases}
2^\mu,&(a,b)=(q,q),\\
d_*,&(a,b)\ne(q,q).
\end{cases}}
\]
In particular, the Dedekind completion of $P$, with or without its
two external ends, has cardinality $2^\mu$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, every full nonsurjective injection $f:\mu\to X$ gives a
proper gap of character $(q,q)$.  Fix $x_*\in X$ and consider
injections into $X\setminus\{x_*\}$.  There are $2^\mu$ such
injections: for a lower bound partition this set into $\mu$ disjoint
pairs and choose one entry of each pair according to a binary word;
the upper bound is $\mu^\mu=2^\mu$.

Distinct injections give distinct gaps.  If $f<g$ first differ at
$\alpha$, choose an unused digit strictly between $f(\alpha)$ and
$g(\alpha)$ and extend the common prefix followed by that digit to
a bijection.  This is possible because the intervening interval has
cardinality $\mu$, while fewer than $\mu$ digits have been used.
The resulting member of $P$ lies strictly between $f$ and $g$.
There are therefore at least $2^\mu$ gaps of character
$(q,q)$.

For an upper bound on all gaps, the tree of short prefixes has
cardinality $d_*\le 2^\mu$, an alphabet of size $\mu$ has at most
$2^\mu$ cuts, and there are at most $\mu^\mu=2^\mu$ full injections.
The three cases of the straddling-cylinder classification therefore
give at most $2^\mu$ gaps altogether.  This proves both the total
number and the multiplicity of $(q,q)$.

Next fix an infinite regular $\theta<\mu$.  There are exactly $d_*$
prefixes whose lengths have cofinality $\theta$.  For the nontrivial
lower bound, take any infinite cardinal $\lambda$ with
$\theta\le\lambda<\mu$ and put
\[
\delta=\lambda\cdot\theta.
\]
Then $\delta<\mu$, $|\delta|=\lambda$, and
$\operatorname{cf}\delta=\theta$.  There are $\mu^\lambda$ injective
words of this length.  One way to see the lower bound is to partition
$X$ into $\lambda$ disjoint sets, each of cardinality $\mu$, and
choose the digit at each coordinate from its assigned set.  Taking
the supremum over $\lambda$ gives $d_*$.  The opposite bound is the
cardinality of the whole prefix tree.

Distinct prefixes give distinct lower-boundary cuts.  For
incomparable prefixes, an unused digit strictly between their first
disagreeing digits yields a permutation between their cylinders;
the interval has size $\mu$ and fewer than $\mu$ digits have been used.
If $p'$ properly extends $p$, choose at the first additional coordinate
an unused digit smaller than the corresponding digit of $p'$.
This gives a point of $C_p$ below all of $C_{p'}$; a larger choice gives
a point above all of $C_{p'}$.  Thus the lower boundaries, and also
the upper boundaries, are distinct in this case as well.  Consequently
there are at least $d_*$ gaps of each character $(\theta,r)$ and
$(r,\theta)$.

It remains to bound the number of gaps of any character other than
$(q,q)$.  Full-length omitted branches contribute none of
these.  Limit-cylinder boundaries contribute at most $d_*$ gaps.
For the maximal-prefix case distinguish two possibilities.

If $q=r$, the character being counted is not $(r,r)$.
In the alphabet sign-path description, a gap not of character
$(r,r)$ has one sign set bounded below $\kappa$, so its path is
eventually constant.  There are at most
$\sum_{\delta<\kappa}2^{|\delta|}=\mu$ such paths.  Deleting a small
set only adds $(r,r)$ gaps, and a principal digit cut likewise
lifts to that character.  Thus each maximal prefix contributes at
most $\mu$ gaps of the character under consideration, and all short
prefixes together contribute at most $d_*\cdot\mu=d_*$.

If $q\ne r$, then $\mu>\kappa$: equality would give
$\operatorname{cf}\mu=\operatorname{cf}\kappa$.  Every alphabet cut
is represented by a length-$\kappa$ sign word, so there are at most
$2^\kappa$ of them.  Cuts created by deletions and retained points
add at most $\mu$.  Since $\kappa<\mu$,
\[
2^\kappa\le\mu^\kappa\le d_*.
\]
Again all maximal prefixes together contribute at most $d_*$ gaps.

Every allowed character other than $(q,q)$ has the form
$(\theta,r)$ or $(r,\theta)$ for a regular $\theta<\mu$.
The only apparent exception would be $(r,r)$ with $r=\mu$,
but then $\kappa=\mu$ is regular and $q=r$, so that pair is
precisely the already excluded $(q,q)$.
The prefix-boundary lower bound therefore applies to every remaining
character, completing the multiplicity calculation.

Finally $|P|=2^\mu$, and its completion adds one point for each
proper gap (and optionally its two ends).  The completion cardinality
is consequently $2^\mu$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Consequences and distinctions}

The exact formula makes several distinctions transparent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\mu=\kappa$ is regular, then $r=q=\kappa$ and the
 spectrum is
 \[
 \{(\kappa,\kappa)\}\cup
 \{(\theta,\kappa),(\kappa,\theta):
          \theta<\kappa\text{ infinite regular}\}.
 \]
 In particular, the order's $\eta_\kappa$ property does not prohibit
 asymmetric gaps with one small side.
\item At a singular strong-limit cutoff, $\mu=\kappa$ and
 $r=q=\operatorname{cf}\kappa$.  The asymmetric pairs range
 over every infinite regular $\theta<\kappa$, including regular
 cardinals much larger than $\operatorname{cf}\kappa$.
\item If $\kappa=\omega_1$ and $2^{\aleph_0}=\aleph_2$, the proper
 gap spectrum consists exactly of
 \[
 (\omega,\omega_1),\quad(\omega_1,\omega),\quad
 (\omega_1,\omega_1),\quad(\omega_2,\omega_2).
 \]
 Every point has character $(\omega_2,\omega_2)$, while both global
 cofinalities are $\omega_1$.  Thus local character, global character,
 and gap character cannot be conflated.
\item If $\kappa=\omega$, then $\mu=r=q=\omega$ and every
 proper gap has character $(\omega,\omega)$.
\end{enumerate}

The set of possible character pairs depends only on $\mu$, $r$,
and $q$; its proof retains substantially more information.
Every gap has a precise structural cause: a split between next
digits, a boundary of a limit-length cylinder, or a nonsurjective
branch of full length.  Coinciding character pairs need not have
the same structural cause.  Nor does equality of these spectra by
itself establish order isomorphism.

Natural further problems include classifying the order of the gaps
of each character, studying which of the three mechanisms can be
recognized intrinsically from the pure linear order, and extending
the analysis to strata of nonminimal enumeration length.  In the
last problem a prefix can leave a small residual alphabet near the
end of the domain, so the uniform large-interval argument used here
no longer applies without a separate analysis of the ordinal tail.

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 831-898
\subsection{Saturation and illustrative cases (source 17)}
\begin{definition}\label{swo:gs:n6.4}
For an infinite regular cardinal $\tau$, an order is an $\eta_\tau$ order
when every pair of separated sets $A<B$, each of size below $\tau$, has a
strict interpolant. Empty sides are allowed.
\end{definition}

\begin{corollary}[Exact saturation threshold]\label{swo:gs:cor:saturation}
The order $P$ is an $\eta_r$ order, and is not an $\eta_{r^+}$ order.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $A<B$ have sizes below $r$ and no strict interpolant. If one side
is empty, the other would be cofinal or coinitial in $P$, contradicting
\eqref{swo:gs:eq:invariants}. If both are nonempty, let
\[
 D=\{x:\exists a\in A\ (x\le a)\},\qquad
 U=\{x:\exists b\in B\ (b\le x)\}.
\]
They are disjoint, and no strict interpolant means that they cover $P$.
If $D$ has a greatest element $x$, then $B$ is coinitial immediately above
$x$, contradicting right point character $q\ge r$. If $U$ has a least
element, the dual contradiction uses $A$. If neither has an endpoint,
$(D,U)$ is a gap with both characters below $r$, forbidden by
\eqref{swo:gs:eq:gap-spectrum}.
An $(r,r)$ gap exists; cofinal and coinitial witnesses of size $r$ show
failure of $\eta_{r^+}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The countable birthday cutoff]\label{swo:gs:n6.6}
At $\kappa=\omega$, $X$ is the order of finite sign sequences,
$\mu=r=q=\omega$, and $P$ has countable weight and density, character
$(\omega,\omega)$ at every point, and only $(\omega,\omega)$ gaps.
It is a Baire, zero-dimensional order. These facts do not identify it
with the real line, whose order topology has a completely different
connectedness structure.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Regular size-matched cutoffs]\label{swo:gs:n6.7}
If $\kappa$ is regular and $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, then $\mu=r=q=\kappa$.
The gap spectrum is
\[
 \{(\kappa,\kappa)\}\cup
 \{(\theta,\kappa),(\kappa,\theta):\theta\in\Reg,\ \theta<\kappa\}.
\]
The order is $\eta_\kappa$ but not $\eta_{\kappa^+}$. The small character
on one side of a limit-prefix boundary does not violate $\eta_\kappa$,
because the other side requires $\kappa$ witnesses.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[A singular strong-limit cutoff]\label{swo:gs:n6.8}
At $\kappa=\beth_\omega$, one has $\mu=\kappa$ and $r=q=\omega$.
Every point has countable character, but for every infinite regular
$\theta<\kappa$ there are gaps of character $(\theta,\omega)$ and
$(\omega,\theta)$. Weight, density and cellularity are
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=2^\kappa$. This complements rather than replaces the
source's $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ binary-coding-length theorem
(\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}).

\smallskip\noindent\merge\ (3 October 2026.) Source~17 wrote ``are
$\kappa^{<\kappa}$, with no extra cardinal-arithmetic simplification
asserted''. At a singular strong limit $\kappa$ one has
$2^\kappa\le\kappa^{\cf\kappa}\le\kappa^{<\kappa}\le2^\kappa$, so the value
is $2^\kappa$; source~18 proves this identity in
\cref{swo:sh:cor:new-regular-singular-invariants}, and source~15 obtains the
same value $2^\kappa$ from its block-type theorem
(\cref{swo:cb:new:cor:block-density}).
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[What has not been classified]\label{swo:gs:n6.9}
The preceding formulas concern the minimal type $\mu$. They do not extend
by replacing $\mu$ with an arbitrary ordinal $\alpha$ of cardinality
$\mu$: a prefix of length below $\alpha$ can already use $\mu$ labels,
leaving a radically smaller residual alphabet. Nor do equal values of
these invariants imply order-isomorphism. Both issues remain in the
research program at the end of this article.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Source 15's route to the saturation threshold}\label{swo:cb:new:sec:local-geometry}

\merge\ Source~15's Section~9, ``Exact local characters at every cardinal
cutoff''. Its Lemma~9.1 is recorded in
\cref{swo:cb:new:lem:full-size-intervals}, its Theorem~9.2 in
\cref{swo:cb:new:thm:local-characters} and its Lemma~9.3 in
\cref{swo:cb:new:lem:power-sup-cofinality};
its saturation theorem is proved directly, by a prefix search, not from the
gap spectrum as in \cref{swo:gs:cor:saturation}, and is printed in full. Its
$\delta=\cf\kappa$ is printed $r$. Its first sentence describes this report at
source~15's pin; the point characters it mentions are now
\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}.

% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 1655-1871 (chunk lines 4-19)

The repository leaves the point characters of
$\WO_{2^{<\kappa}}(\No_{<\kappa})$ open when
$2^{<\kappa}$ need not equal $\kappa$.  They can be computed exactly.
The local invariant is controlled by the enumeration length, whereas
the global invariant is controlled by the numerical alphabet.

Throughout this section let
\[
 X=\No_{<\kappa},\qquad \mu=2^{<\kappa},\qquad
 r=\cf\kappa,\qquad L=\WO_\mu(X),
\]
where $\kappa$ is an infinite initial cardinal.  For $f\in L$, write
$\operatorname{cf}^{-}(f)$ for the cofinality of the open initial
segment $L_{<f}$, and $\operatorname{ci}^{+}(f)$ for the coinitiality
of the open final segment $L_{>f}$.
% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 1655-1871 (chunk lines 115-120)

We use the convention that an order is $\eta_\nu$ if every cut
$A<B$ with $|A|+|B|<\nu$ has a strict interpolant, including the
cases where one side is empty.  This does not assert that an arbitrary
cut with larger sides is filled.

% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 1655-1871 (chunk lines 146-218)
\begin{theorem}[Exact saturation threshold for the minimal slice]
\label{swo:cb:new:thm:saturation-threshold}
The order $L=\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ is $\eta_{\cf\kappa}$.
For every infinite cardinal $\nu>\cf\kappa$, it is not $\eta_\nu$.
Equivalently, among infinite cardinal cut-size parameters,
\[
             L\text{ is }\eta_\nu
                \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \nu\leq\cf\kappa.
\]
In particular, at every regular $\kappa$, the minimal slice is
$\eta_\kappa$ without the additional assumption
$2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $A<B$ be subsets of $L$ with $|A|+|B|<r$.
Any family of fewer than $r$ surreal labels has birthdays
bounded below $\kappa$.  Consequently, for any strict numerical cut
formed by such labels, there are two points $u<v$ of $X$ strictly
between its sides.  This follows from the usual surreal cut
construction applied twice; all resulting birthdays stay below
$\kappa$.  The same statement holds with an empty side.
By \Cref{swo:cb:new:lem:full-size-intervals}, the interval $(u,v)$ has
cardinality $\mu$.  Thus a numerical cut of this size admits a
separator outside any prescribed set of fewer than $\mu$ labels.

If one of $A,B$ is empty, choose the first entry strictly above all
first entries on the left or below all first entries on the right,
and complete to a permutation.  Suppose both are nonempty.  For
each $(a,b)\in A\times B$, let $d(a,b)<\mu$ be its first-difference
coordinate.  By \Cref{swo:cb:new:lem:power-sup-cofinality},
\[
        \beta=\sup\{d(a,b)+1:a\in A,\ b\in B\}<\mu.
\]

Build an injective prefix $p$, keeping as active those rows of
$A$ and $B$ that still agree with $p$.  At a stage $\eta$, every
active left value at coordinate $\eta$ is at most every active
right value.  If a value occurs on both sides, it is unique; append
that value to $p$ and keep the rows taking it.  Every discarded left
row is already below all extensions of the new prefix, and every
discarded right row is already above them.  The appended value is
fresh because it belongs to active injective rows.

If the active value sets have no common value, they form a strict
numerical cut.  Choose a separator outside the range of $p$ using
the preceding full-size-interval argument, append it, and stop.
If an active side becomes empty, use the same argument for the
resulting one-sided cut.  At a limit stage take the union of the
prefixes and retain the rows that extend it.  The search cannot
still have active rows on both sides at stage $\beta$: such a pair
would agree at every coordinate below $\beta$, contradicting the definition of
$\beta$.  Therefore it stops with a prefix of length less than
$\mu$.

That prefix has used fewer than $\mu$ labels.  Its remaining labels
and remaining positions both have cardinality $\mu$, and an
exhaustive completion has order type $\mu$.  Every such completion
lies strictly above $A$ and below $B$, proving $\eta_r$.

Finally, \Cref{swo:cb:new:thm:local-characters} gives a global
cofinal subset of size $r$.  Together with an empty right side
it is a cut of total size below every cardinal $\nu>r$, but
it has no upper interpolant.  Hence no such $\eta_\nu$ property
holds.
\end{proof}

These results settle the point-character part, and the maximal uniform
cut-size threshold, of the earlier minimal-slice question.  They do
not compute its full two-sided unrealized-cut spectrum.  In
particular, saturation below $\cf\kappa$ and failure above that
threshold do not specify which individual larger pairs of regular
cofinalities occur as gaps.

\smallskip\noindent\merge\ (3 October 2026.) The ``full two-sided
unrealized-cut spectrum'' that source~15 leaves open is source~17's
\cref{swo:gs:thm:gap-spectrum} (and source~18's
\cref{swo:sh:thm:exact-gap-spectrum}); its multiplicities are
\cref{swo:sh:thm:gap-multiplicities}.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 755-975
\subsection{Source 18's route and extension: characters, interpolation and two regimes}
\label{swo:vi:sub:sh-chars}

\merge\ The rest of source~18's Section~4, printed whole. Its point
characters and interpolation theorem are source~17's
\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants,swo:gs:cor:saturation} and source~15's
\cref{swo:cb:new:thm:saturation-threshold}, proved again with a different
interpolation argument (bounded dropout coordinates); it extends both to the
types $\mu+n$ and to the finite-cofinite prefix orders $Q_n$
(\cref{swo:sh:lem:new-cofinite-prefixes}), and it exhibits a bounded $(r,r)$
obstruction explicitly. Its corollary on two regimes merges with source~17's
examples \cref{swo:gs:n6.7,swo:gs:n6.8}; its second part supplies the value
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=2^\kappa$ used in \cref{swo:gs:n6.8}.

% ---- source 18 resumes (Subsection 4.4)
\subsubsection{Exact point characters and global cofinalities}

For a point $e$ in a linear order, its left character means the
cofinality of $(-\infty,e)$, and its right character means the
coinitiality of $(e,\infty)$.  An immediate predecessor or successor
therefore gives character $1$ on the respective side.

\begin{theorem}[Complete local and global characters at the minimal type]
\label{swo:sh:thm:new-minimal-characters}
Every point of $\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$ has left and right character
$q=\operatorname{cf}(\mu)$, whereas
\[
 \operatorname{cf}(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa}))
 =\operatorname{ci}(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa}))=r.
\]
More generally, $Q_n(\No_{<\kappa})$ has point characters $(q,q)$ for
every finite $n$.  Accordingly, in
$\WO_{\mu+n}(\No_{<\kappa})$:
\begin{itemize}
\item if $n=0$ or $1$, every point has characters $(q,q)$;
\item if $n\geq2$, the first and last points of each finite $n!$-point
      fiber have characters $(q,1)$ and $(1,q)$ respectively;
\item all interior points of a finite fiber have characters $(1,1)$.
\end{itemize}
Interior points exist exactly when $n\geq3$.  The global cofinality and
coinitiality remain $r$ for every finite $n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $p\in Q_n(\No_{<\kappa})$ and $\delta<\mu$.  There are $\mu$ labels
numerically below $p(\delta)$, and equally many above it.  The labels
used before $\delta$, together with the finitely many labels omitted
from $p$, exclude fewer than $\mu$ labels.  Choose a smaller available
label which therefore occurs later in the $\mu$-prefix, and transpose
its occurrence with position $\delta$.  This gives $p^-_\delta<p$
with first difference exactly $\delta$.  An analogous transposition
gives $p^+_\delta>p$.

As $\delta$ runs along an increasing cofinal sequence in $\mu$, the
points $p^-_\delta$ increase cofinally toward $p$, while
$p^+_\delta$ decrease coinitially toward it.  This proves both upper
bounds $q$.  Conversely, fewer than $q$ points below $p$ have
first-disagreement coordinates bounded below $\mu$.  Changing $p$ at
a later coordinate gives a point above all of them and below $p$.
They cannot be cofinal.  The dual argument gives the right lower
bound.  This proves the character statement for $Q_n$.

The map $e\mapsto e(0)$ is onto $\No_{<\kappa}$ and respects comparisons
whenever the first coordinates differ.  A family cofinal in the
enumeration order must therefore have first coordinates cofinal in
$\No_{<\kappa}$; conversely, choose one enumeration over every member of
a numerical cofinal sequence.  This proves the global cofinality
formula, and the coinitiality formula is dual.  Finally,
$\WO_{\mu+n}(\No_{<\kappa})$ is the lexicographic sum over $Q_n$ of
finite ordered fibers of size $n!$.  Immediate neighbors inside a
fiber give character $1$, while approach to the outside of a fiber is
controlled by the character $q$ of the quotient point.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Sharp interpolation at every infinite cardinal cutoff}

We say that $L$ is $\eta_r$ if for all sets $A,B\subseteq L$ with
$|A\cup B|<r$ and $a<b$ for every $a\in A,b\in B$, there is
$z\in L$ satisfying $A<z<B$.  Empty sides are allowed.  The sharpness
result below also uses a bounded cut with both sides nonempty, so it
does not depend on the empty-side convention.

\begin{theorem}[Exact guaranteed interpolation]
\label{swo:sh:thm:new-cutoff-interpolation}
For every infinite initial cardinal $\kappa$, with notation as above,
\[
 \WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\text{ is }\eta_r,
 \qquad r=\operatorname{cf}(\kappa).
\]
It has a bounded unfilled cut of character $(r,r)$, so it is not
$\eta_{r^+}$.  The same interpolation and sharpness assertions hold
for $\WO_{\mu+1}(\No_{<\kappa})$.  For $n\geq2$, adjacent points in
the terminal fibers already preclude $\eta_\omega$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of interpolation]
First $\No_{<\kappa}$ is $\eta_r$.  A family of fewer than $r$ numerical
options has birthdays bounded below $\kappa$, so the set-cut theorem
supplies a separator with birthday less than $\kappa$.  Applying it
twice supplies a nonempty interval of possible separators, still
between the prescribed options.  By
\Cref{swo:sh:lem:new-residual-alphabet}, such an interval has $\mu$
labels.

Let $A<B$ be sets of input enumerations with $|A\cup B|<r$.  We
construct an injective common prefix $p$ until a completion separating
the inputs becomes possible.  At a prefix of length $\delta<\mu$,
the active inputs are
\[
 A_p=\{a\in A:a\restriction\delta=p\},\qquad
 B_p=\{b\in B:b\restriction\delta=p\}.
\]
The invariant is that all discarded lower inputs lie below every
completion of $p$, while all discarded upper inputs lie above every
completion of $p$.  Let $L$ and $U$ be the sets of next entries of the
active lower and upper inputs.  Then every $l\in L$ is at most every
$u\in U$.

If $L\cap U=\varnothing$, the two next-entry sets are strictly
separated (with empty sides permitted).  Choose an interval of
numerical separators between them.  It has $\mu$ labels, whereas
$\operatorname{ran}p$ has size less than $\mu$.  Choose an unused
separator $z$ in it.  Every completion of $p\frown(z)$ lies strictly
between all inputs, and an exhaustive completion of the required type
exists by cardinality.  The construction stops.

Otherwise, $L\cap U$ consists of exactly one label $z$: two different
common labels would contradict $L\leq U$.  This label is unused, since
an active input is injective.  Append it to $p$.  Inputs with a smaller
lower entry or a larger upper entry are discarded in the correct
direction, maintaining the invariant.  At limits take the union of
the prefixes.

Suppose the construction never stopped before $\mu$.  The union is an
injective word $p$ of length $\mu$.  Every input whose $\mu$-prefix
differs from $p$ drops out at its first disagreement.  There are fewer
than $r\leq q=\operatorname{cf}(\mu)$ inputs, so these dropout
coordinates are bounded below $\mu$.  At most one input can have
$\mu$-prefix exactly $p$: for type $\mu$ the whole enumeration is
determined, and for type $\mu+1$ its single omitted label determines
the last entry.  Since $A<B$, that one input cannot lie on both sides.
Beyond the bound on the dropout coordinates, at least one active side
is therefore empty, forcing the stopping case.  This contradiction
proves that the construction stops at a coordinate below $\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the bounded obstruction]
Choose a sign word $s$ of length exactly $\kappa$ such that both its
plus positions and minus positions are cofinal in $\kappa$, and whose
first two signs are $+,-$.  For example, use the parity of the finite
tail of each ordinal coordinate.  Then $0<s<1$, while
$s\notin \No_{<\kappa}$.  Prefixes $s\restriction\gamma$ at cofinally many
positions with $s(\gamma)=+$ lie below $s$ and are cofinal below its
cut in $\No_{<\kappa}$; prefixes at cofinally many minus positions are
coinitial above it.  Choose such two cofinal collections indexed by
$r$.

Every $x\in \No_{<\kappa}$ has its comparison with $s$ decided before
$\kappa$.  A sufficiently long chosen prefix therefore passes $x$ on
the appropriate side.  No element of $\No_{<\kappa}$ separates the two
prefix families.  Neither side can have a smaller cofinal family:
fewer than $r$ birthdays are bounded below $\kappa$, and a longer
appropriate prefix passes every label in that family.  Thus this is
a bounded numerical gap of character $(r,r)$.

Pull the cut back under the first-coordinate map
$e\mapsto e(0)$.  Choose arbitrary completions for the two numerical
cofinal families.  A hypothetical separating enumeration would need
its first entry to lie in the missing numerical cut, which is
impossible.  Conversely those completions are cofinal and coinitial
on their respective sides, and the first-coordinate projection
proves the matching lower bounds.  Enumerations starting with $0$ and
$1$ bound the whole configuration.  This supplies the claimed
bounded $(r,r)$ obstruction.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Regular and singular consequences}

\begin{corollary}[Two especially transparent regimes]
\label{swo:sh:cor:new-regular-singular-invariants}
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\kappa$ is regular and $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, then
\[
 |\WO_\kappa(\No_{<\kappa})|=2^\kappa,\qquad
 \dens=\cell=\kappa,
\]
and every local character and global cofinality or coinitiality is
$\kappa$.  The order is $\eta_\kappa$ and has a bounded unfilled
$(\kappa,\kappa)$-cut.
\item If $\kappa$ is a singular strong-limit cardinal, then
\[
 |\WO_\kappa(\No_{<\kappa})|=2^\kappa,\qquad
 \dens=\cell=2^\kappa,
\]
while every point character and both global cofinalities are
$\operatorname{cf}(\kappa)$.  Its sharp interpolation threshold is
$\eta_{\operatorname{cf}(\kappa)}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the regular case, $\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$.  For a fixed
$\lambda<\kappa$, every function $\lambda\to\kappa$ has bounded
range, and the possibilities for each range bound are at most
$2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$; summing over $\kappa$ range bounds gives
$\kappa^\lambda=\kappa$ for nonzero $\lambda<\kappa$.

In the singular strong-limit case, $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$.  Let
$r=\operatorname{cf}(\kappa)$ and take a cofinal sequence
$(\kappa_\xi)_{\xi<r}$ below $\kappa$.  A subset of $\kappa$ is
determined by its intersections with the $\kappa_\xi$.  Each
intersection has fewer than $\kappa$ possibilities by the strong-limit
hypothesis, so
\[
 2^\kappa\leq\kappa^r
       \leq\kappa^{<\kappa}\leq\kappa^\kappa=2^\kappa.
\]
Thus $\kappa^{<\kappa}=2^\kappa$.  The other conclusions now follow
from the preceding theorems and the baseline cardinality theorem.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{What these results settle, and what they do not.}
The baseline explicitly left density, cellularity and much of the
singular-cutoff interpolation analysis as continuation questions.
The preceding theorems determine density and cellularity for every
fixed enumeration type and every infinite alphabet, and give exact
local/global characters and sharp guaranteed interpolation at every
infinite initial birthday cutoff for the minimal slice.  They do not
identify the order up to isomorphism.  In particular, equality of
these invariants is not an isomorphism theorem, and the old exact
ordinal spectrum remains logically separate from the new topological
information.  The full spectrum of unfilled cut characters requires
an additional analysis of boundaries of transfinite prefix cylinders.

% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 1214-1654

\section{Longer strata: every cardinal-block type (source 15)}
\label{swo:cb:new:sec:block-types}\label{swo:cb:sec:fixed}

\merge\ Source~15's Section~8, printed whole: the only batch-81 treatment of
strata longer than the minimal one. Its $r_\kappa$ is printed $\rho_\kappa$
(by \cref{swo:st:thm:transition}, $\rho_\kappa=\bl(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa}))$), so
that $r$ keeps the meaning $\cf\kappa$; its $D_{\sigma,m}$ is printed
$\B_\sigma\times_\lex m$, so that no letter clashes with its core $\mathcal D$
(\cref{swo:sub:notation81}). The section extends source~12's
\cref{swo:st:thm:transition} ($\gamma=1$, $n=0$) along the targets of
\cref{swo:st:n14.1}. Its entry code (\cref{swo:cb:lem:entrycode}) is source~12's
\cref{swo:st:lem:code} and source~16's mechanism in (S16.2); its cube clause is
\cref{swo:st:lem:strictcube} (reals report \code{lwo:thm:cube-strict}); its row
scheduler (\cref{swo:cb:new:lem:block-scheduler}) is source~12's
\cref{swo:st:lem:scheduler} run blockwise along $\gamma$; its finite-deletion
lemma (\cref{swo:cb:new:lem:finite-deletion}) is \cref{swo:st:lem:cones} with a
finite set removed; its finite-capacity lemma is, by its own account, the
finite-fiber fusion argument of the reals report. Source~15 states its
finite-deletion lemma for singular strong-limit $\kappa$; the hypothesis is not
used in its proof.

% ---- source 15 resumes (Section 8)
The earlier minimal-slice theorem (\cref{swo:st:sec:transition}) extends to all enumeration types whose
remainder on division by the alphabet cardinal is finite.  This includes
the explicitly proposed next cases $\mu\cdot2$ and $\mu\cdot\gamma+n$.
It does not classify an additional infinite remainder smaller than $\mu$.
All products and sums of ordinals in this section are ordinal operations.

Let $\kappa$ be an infinite initial cardinal, and put
\[
 X=\No_{<\kappa},\qquad \mu=|X|=2^{<\kappa},\qquad
 \rho_\kappa=
 \begin{cases}
  \kappa\cdot\kappa,&\kappa\text{ is a singular strong-limit cardinal},\\
  \mu,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
For an ordinal $\alpha$ with $|\alpha|=\mu$, write $\WO_\alpha(X)$ for
the bijections $\alpha\to X$, compared by their first unequal entry in
the numerical order of $X$.  For a positive integer $m$, write
\[
 \B_\sigma\times_{\lex}m=\B_\sigma\times_{\mathrm{lex}}\{0,\ldots,m-1\},
 \qquad \B_\sigma=(2^\sigma,<_{\mathrm{lex}}),
\]
the finite chain $m=\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$ being the last coordinate.

\begin{lemma}[An explicit entry code and the strict cube hierarchy]\label{swo:cb:lem:entrycode}
Replace each minus sign by $00$, each plus by $11$, and terminate with
$01$. The resulting code $c(s)$ is prefix-free and preserves numerical
surreal order. Its length is $2\cdot\bd(s)+2$, less than $\kappa$
for $s\in\No_{<\kappa}$. Furthermore,
\[
 \B_\alpha\emb\B_\beta\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\alpha\leq\beta.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The three blocks $00<01<11$ encode precisely minus, termination, and
plus. Distinct completed codes cannot be proper prefixes: at the first
termination of one of them the other either terminates too or has a
different block. Padding a completed entry therefore does not alter its
comparison with another entry.

For the cube assertion, extension by zeros gives the embedding when
$\alpha\leq\beta$. Suppose $\alpha>\beta$ and an embedding in the other
direction exists. Successively read target bits, retaining at each step
a full source child cylinder whose image lies on one side of the next
target-bit split. Such a child exists: if the lower child does not lie
wholly below the split, every point of the upper child lies above it.
At limits take unions of the chosen prefixes. After $\beta$ steps, all
points extending one source prefix of length $\beta$ have the same whole
target word. There are at least two source extensions since
$\beta<\alpha$, contradicting injectivity. This is the same cylinder
fusion mechanism used in the prior binary-length calculations.
\end{proof}

When $\mu=\kappa$ is singular, $\kappa$ is a strong limit. Otherwise
$2^\rho=\kappa$ for some $\rho<\kappa$ would also give
$2^\lambda=\kappa$ at $\lambda=\max(\rho,\cf\kappa)<\kappa$,
contradicting K\"onig's inequality $\cf(2^\lambda)>\lambda$.
Thus the three cases used below are exhaustive.

\begin{theorem}[Cardinal blocks and a finite terminal permutation]
\label{swo:cb:new:thm:block-types}
For every $0<\gamma<\mu^+$ and every $n<\omega$,
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})
       \equi (\B_{\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma}\times_{\lex}n!).
 \quad}
\]
In particular, if $\kappa$ is a singular strong limit, then
\[
 \WO_{\kappa\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})
       \equi
       \B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa\cdot\gamma}
          \times_{\mathrm{lex}}\{0,\ldots,n!-1\}.
\]
The statement concerns equimorphism; it does not identify these
permutation orders with the generally nondense binary products.
\end{theorem}

We give the scheduler detail before proving the theorem, since a
cardinality-preserving reindexing alone would not preserve lexicographic
order.

\begin{lemma}[Blockwise row scheduling]
\label{swo:cb:new:lem:block-scheduler}
Let $Y$ be a linearly ordered set of infinite cardinality $\kappa$, let
$0<\gamma<\kappa^+$, and put $\lambda=\kappa\cdot\gamma$.
Suppose that $Y$ contains pairwise disjoint subsets $(Y_i)_{i<\lambda}$,
each of cardinality $\kappa$, with an order embedding
$\phi_i:X\to Y_i$, and that
$Z=Y\setminus\bigcup_{i<\lambda}Y_i$ is either empty or has
cardinality $\kappa$.  Then
\[
                   X^\lambda_{\mathrm{lex}}\emb\WO_\lambda(Y).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each $i<\lambda$, fix a bijection $b_i:\kappa\to Y_i$.
For $x\in X$, transpose $\phi_i(x)$ with $b_i(0)$ in that enumeration;
denote the result by $b_{i,x}$.  Its first value is $\phi_i(x)$,
and for equal $x$ the whole row is the same.

Split the variable rows into the consecutive blocks
$\{\kappa\cdot\beta+\xi:\xi<\kappa\}$, $\beta<\gamma$.
Within one block schedule row--column pairs $(\xi,j)\in\kappa^2$
first by $\max(\xi,j)$ and then lexicographically inside each level.
Every pair has fewer than $\kappa$ predecessors: all earlier pairs lie
in a square bounded below $\kappa$, and such a square has cardinality
less than $\kappa$, even when $\kappa$ is singular.  The schedule
therefore has order type $\kappa$.  The first occurrence of row $\xi$
is $(\xi,0)$, and these first occurrences have increasing row indices.

If $Z\ne\varnothing$, include its fixed $\kappa$-long enumeration as a
filler row before the variable rows in the first block.  The row-index
order is $1+\kappa=\kappa$, so the same construction applies.  The
filler row has no input parameter.  Every later block uses only its
$\kappa$ variable rows.

For $x=(x_i)_{i<\lambda}$, output the entries of $b_{i,x_i}$ by these
successive block schedules.  Each block has length $\kappa$ and there
are $\gamma$ blocks, so the output has length exactly $\lambda$.
The disjoint rows and the filler exhaust $Y$ exactly once.

If inputs $x<_{\mathrm{lex}}x'$ first differ at row
$i=\kappa\cdot\beta+\xi$, earlier blocks agree completely.  In block
$\beta$, every output before the first entry of row $i$ comes from a
lower row or from the fixed filler.  Those outputs agree.  The first
unequal output is therefore $\phi_i(x_i)<\phi_i(x'_i)$ at the first
entry of row $i$.  This proves both injectivity and order preservation.
No regularity of $\gamma$ or $\kappa$ was used.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite deletion does not obstruct the singular scheduler]
\label{swo:cb:new:lem:finite-deletion}
If $\kappa$ is a singular strong limit, $X=\No_{<\kappa}$,
$F\subseteq X$ is finite, and $\lambda=\kappa\cdot\gamma$ for
$0<\gamma<\kappa^+$, then
\[
                   X^\lambda_{\mathrm{lex}}
                         \emb\WO_\lambda(X\setminus F).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $\xi<\kappa$, let $C_\xi$ be the sign-sequence cone with prefix
$+^\xi\concat -$.  The cones are disjoint, and prefixing gives an
order isomorphism $X\to C_\xi$.  Indeed,
$\xi+1+\eta<\kappa$ for $\xi,\eta<\kappa$, because $\kappa$ is an
initial cardinal; deletion of the prefix gives the inverse.  Each
cone has cardinality $\kappa$.  At most finitely many cones meet $F$.
Select $\kappa$ unaffected cones and index them by $\lambda$, which
has cardinality $\kappa$.

The complement of those selected cones in $X\setminus F$ has
cardinality $\kappa$: it contains all but finitely many of the
all-plus sequences, one for each length below $\kappa$, and is a
subset of $X$.  The preceding lemma now applies, with that complement
as the filler.  In particular, no unexplained bijection of a grid with
$\lambda$ is being used in place of a lexicographic scheduler.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{swo:cb:new:thm:block-types}]
Write $\lambda=\mu\cdot\gamma$ and $\sigma=\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma$.
We first construct the upper embedding.  Use the repository's
prefix-free, numerical-order-preserving binary code $c(s)$ for each
$s\in X$, with positive length less than $\kappa$.

If $\mu>\kappa$, pad each individual entry code to length $\kappa$.
Prefix-freeness ensures that this preserves all entry comparisons.
A block of $\mu$ entries then consumes ordinal length
$\kappa\cdot\mu=\mu$.  Applying this separately to the $\gamma$
successive blocks gives a prefix code of length
$\mu\cdot\gamma=\sigma$.

If $\mu=\kappa$ and $\kappa$ is regular, concatenate the unpadded
codes in each $\kappa$-entry block.  Each proper partial sum of their
lengths is below $\kappa$ by regularity, while the full sum is
$\kappa$ because all code lengths are positive.  Each block therefore
consumes exactly $\kappa$, again giving length $\sigma$.

In the remaining case $\mu=\kappa$ is singular, it is a strong limit.
Pad each entry code to length $\kappa$.  Each block now consumes
$\kappa\cdot\kappa$, and the complete $\lambda$-entry prefix has
code length $\kappa\cdot\kappa\cdot\gamma=\sigma$.

In all cases, a prefix of length $\lambda$ from a bijection
$\lambda+n\to X$ has a binary code of length exactly $\sigma$.
The remaining $n$ labels form a finite set determined by that prefix.
Append the rank of their terminal permutation in its increasing
lexicographic list of $n!$ permutations.  Different prefixes compare
inside the binary code; equal prefixes have the same remaining labels
and compare by the finite rank.  This gives
$\WO_{\lambda+n}(X)\emb (\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}n!)$.

For the reverse embedding in either nonexceptional case, reserve a
fixed $n$-element set $F\subseteq X$ and partition $X\setminus F$
into pairs indexed by $\lambda$.  Such a partition exists because
$|\lambda|=\mu$.  List each pair in its increasing or decreasing
orientation according to the corresponding bit of a member of
$\B_\lambda$.  Since $\lambda$ is a nonzero limit ordinal,
$2\cdot\lambda=\lambda$.  Append the requested one of the $n!$
permutations of $F$.  Here $\sigma=\lambda$, giving the required
embedding.

It remains to obtain the longer cube in the singular strong-limit
case.  Let $\delta=\cf\kappa<\kappa$, and choose nonzero ordinals
$(\tau_j)_{j<\delta}$ cofinal in $\kappa$, with
$\sum_{j<\delta}\tau_j=\kappa$.  The identities
\[
 \delta\cdot\kappa=\kappa,
 \qquad
 \delta\cdot\lambda=\delta\cdot(\kappa\cdot\gamma)=\lambda
\]
allow the $\lambda$ input coordinates of $X^\lambda$ to be grouped
into $\lambda$ consecutive blocks of $\delta$ coordinates.  In each
block, restrict its $j$th coordinate to surreals whose sign sequences
have length exactly $\tau_j$.  That coordinate is a copy of
$\B_{\tau_j}$, so the entire restricted product is
\[
 \B_{\sum_{i<\lambda}\sum_{j<\delta}\tau_j}
       =\B_{\kappa\cdot\lambda}
       =\B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa\cdot\gamma}
       =\B_\sigma.
\]
By \Cref{swo:cb:new:lem:finite-deletion}, this product embeds into
$\WO_\lambda(X\setminus F)$.  Append any of the $n!$ permutations
of $F$, obtaining $(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}n!)\emb\WO_{\lambda+n}(X)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact coding lengths, embedded cubes, and comparisons}
\label{swo:vi:sub:cb-coding}

The next capacity argument is the finite-fiber fusion argument already
used in the real-alphabet report.  It is included to make the numerical
consequences of the extension explicit.

\begin{lemma}[Finite fibers over a binary cube]
\label{swo:cb:new:lem:finite-capacity}
Let $\sigma,\tau$ be nonzero limit ordinals and let $m,n$ be positive
integers.  Then
\[
 (\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)\emb (\B_{\tau}\times_{\lex}n)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sigma<\tau\ \text{or}\
      (\sigma=\tau\ \text{and}\ m\leq n).
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \bl(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)=\sigma+\lceil\log_2m\rceil,
 \qquad
 \B_\theta\emb (\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \theta\leq\sigma+\lfloor\log_2m\rfloor.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For equal binary lengths, suppose
$f:(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)\emb (\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}n)$.  Recursively choose a source
binary prefix $s_\xi$ and target binary prefix $t_\xi$, both of
length $\xi$, such that
\[
 f\bigl(C_{s_\xi}\times\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\bigr)
     \subseteq C_{t_\xi}\times\{0,\ldots,n-1\}.
\]
At a successor stage the target's next bit cuts the image into two
ordered parts.  Of the source cylinder's two full children, at least
one lies wholly on one side of that cut: if its lower child is not
wholly on the lower side, its upper child is wholly on the upper side.
Keep that child and the corresponding target bit.  At a limit stage
take unions of prefixes.  The invariant persists, and the source
cylinder remains nonempty.  At stage $\sigma$, all $m$ points over
one source binary sequence lie in one target fiber of size $n$.
Thus $m\leq n$.  The converse is immediate.

Coding the finite chain of size $m$ by
$\lceil\log_2m\rceil$ bits gives
$(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)\emb\B_{\sigma+\lceil\log_2m\rceil}$.
No cube shorter than $\sigma$ can contain $(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)$, since it
already contains $\B_\sigma$ and the binary cube hierarchy is
strict.  A target $\B_{\sigma+j}=(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}2^j)$ with $j<\omega$
can contain it only if $m\leq2^j$, by the same fusion argument.
This proves the coding-length formula.

Likewise, $\B_{\sigma+j}$ embeds into $(\B_{\sigma}\times_{\lex}m)$ exactly when
$2^j\leq m$.  Once the first forbidden finite extension has been
excluded, every larger cube is excluded because it contains that
extension.  This proves the complete cube spectrum.

Finally, if $\sigma<\tau$, then
$\sigma+\lceil\log_2m\rceil<\tau$ because $\tau$ is a limit.
Code into that shorter cube and then into the target.  If
$\tau<\sigma$, the target codes into a cube of length
$\tau+\lceil\log_2n\rceil<\sigma$, whereas the source contains
$\B_\sigma$; strictness excludes an embedding.  This proves the
comparison criterion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The complete invariant on these block types]
\label{swo:cb:new:cor:block-invariants}
Under the hypotheses of \Cref{swo:cb:new:thm:block-types}, put
$\sigma=\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma$.  Then
\[
 \bl\bigl(\WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})\bigr)
       =\sigma+\lceil\log_2(n!)\rceil,
\]
and
\[
 \B_\theta\emb\WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \theta\leq\sigma+\lfloor\log_2(n!)\rfloor.
\]
For $0<\gamma,\eta<\mu^+$ and $m,n<\omega$,
\[
 \WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})
      \emb\WO_{\mu\cdot\eta+m}(\No_{<\kappa})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \gamma<\eta\ \text{or}\
       (\gamma=\eta\ \text{and}\ n!\leq m!).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply the finite-capacity lemma and use strict monotonicity in the right
argument of $\gamma\mapsto \rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma$ for a fixed
nonzero left factor.  Here each $\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma$ is a nonzero
limit ordinal.
\end{proof}

For an unconditional singular example, take $\kappa=\beth_\omega$.
The types $\kappa\cdot2$, $\kappa\cdot2+3$, and $\kappa^2$ have
binary coding lengths respectively
\[
 \kappa^2\cdot2,\qquad \kappa^2\cdot2+3,\qquad \kappa^3.
\]
The type $\kappa\cdot2+3$ contains the cube of length
$\kappa^2\cdot2+2$ but not the cube of its own coding length.
These distinctions are invisible to cardinality and to the ordinal
embedding spectrum, which remain $2^\kappa$ and
$\{\beta:\beta<\kappa^+\}$ throughout.

\subsection{Density and cellularity, including the singular minimal slice}
\label{swo:vi:sub:cb-density}

Write $\dens(L)$ for the least cardinality of a dense subset in the order
topology, and $\cell(L)$ for the supremum of cardinalities of families of
pairwise disjoint nonempty open sets.  An additional consequence of the
coding theorem is an exact answer for these two invariants on the same
block types.  In particular, the singular minimal slice has the largest
possible cellularity, a fact not visible from its unchanged ordinal
embedding spectrum.

\begin{corollary}[Density and cellularity of the block types]
\label{swo:cb:new:cor:block-density}
For $L=\WO_{\mu\cdot\gamma+n}(\No_{<\kappa})$ as above,
\[
 \dens(L)=\cell(L)=
 \begin{cases}
 2^{<\mu},&\rho_\kappa=\mu,\ \gamma=1,\ n\leq2,\\
 2^\mu,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Consequently
\[
 d\bigl(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\bigr)
 =c\bigl(\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\bigr)
 =
 \begin{cases}
 2^\kappa,&\kappa\text{ is a singular strong limit},\\
 2^{<\mu},&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
First consider $\rho_\kappa=\mu$, $\gamma=1$, and $n\leq2$.
The finite factor $n!$ has size at most two.  There is an embedding
$f:L\to (\B_{\mu}\times_{\lex}n!)$.  No point of $L$ is isolated.  Indeed, $L$
has no endpoints because the native surreal alphabet has neither a
least nor a greatest element; swapping the first entry with a smaller
or larger label moves either way without changing the enumeration type.
The adjacency criterion for exhaustive permutations applies inside a
fixed type: if the unused remainder at a first difference is infinite,
an ascent in the lower tail or a descent in the upper tail gives an
intermediate permutation of the same type.  Adjacency is therefore
confined to the finite terminal permutation component.  For $n\leq2$
each such component has at most two points, so no point has both an
immediate predecessor and an immediate successor.  Thus no point is
isolated, and every nonempty open set contains at least five points.

For every binary prefix $s$ of length less than $\mu$ whose cylinder
meets the first-coordinate projection of $f[L]$, choose one element
$x_s\in L$ whose image has that prefix.  There are at most
$\sum_{\xi<\mu}2^{|\xi|}=2^{<\mu}$ such choices.  These choices are
dense.  To see this, take five increasingly ordered points
$x_0<\cdots<x_4$ in any given nonempty open interval.  Each binary
fiber has at most two points, so the binary first coordinate of
$f(x_2)$ is strictly between those of $f(x_0)$ and $f(x_4)$.
Choose a prefix long enough to distinguish it from both outer
coordinates.  Its whole binary cylinder lies strictly between them,
and the chosen $x_s$ lies in the original open interval.  Hence
$\dens(L)\leq2^{<\mu}$.

Conversely $L$ contains $\B_\mu$.  For each $\xi<\mu$, its
$2^{|\xi|}$ cylinders of length $\xi$ each contain three ordered
points.  The ambient open intervals in $L$ between the images of
the outer two points are nonempty, since they contain the image of
the middle point, and they are pairwise disjoint.  Therefore
$\cell(L)\geq2^{<\mu}$, proving equality in the first
case since always $\cell(L)\leq \dens(L)$.

In every remaining case, put $\sigma=\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma$.
If $\sigma>\mu$, the embedded cube $\B_\sigma$ has $2^\mu$
distinct cylinders of prefix length $\mu$, each with at least three
points because $\sigma$ is a limit ordinal greater than $\mu$.
The preceding interval construction gives $\cell(L)\geq2^\mu$.
If $\sigma=\mu$ but $n\geq3$, the embedded order $(\B_{\mu}\times_{\lex}n!)$
has a fiber with at least three points over each of its $2^\mu$
binary sequences.  The intervals between the images of its first
and last fiber points are again pairwise disjoint and nonempty.
Thus $\cell(L)\geq2^\mu$ here as well.  Finally
$\dens(L)\leq|L|=2^\mu$, giving the claimed equalities.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The boundary of the result}
\label{swo:vi:sub:cb-boundary}

Write a general admissible type as
$\alpha=\mu\cdot\gamma+\zeta$, $\zeta<\mu$.
The theorem settles the case that $\zeta$ is finite.  When $\zeta$
is infinite, the preceding long prefix leaves a residual numerical
suborder of cardinality $|\zeta|$; that residual order varies with
the prefix.  Neither the size nor the coding behavior of this smaller
alphabet can simply be replaced by those of $\No_{<\kappa}$.
An exact treatment must control the possible residual suborders and
the code lengths available uniformly for their permutations.  Thus
the result gives a precise affirmative continuation of the proposed
cardinal-block program while leaving the smaller infinite-tail
classification explicitly open.

\begin{remark}[\merge; the reals report's display at $X=\No_{<\kappa}$]\label{swo:vi:rem:lwo-display}
The reals report asks under which hypotheses on $X$ the display
$\WO_{\lambda+n}(X)\equi\B_\lambda\times_\lex n!$ still holds (its question
``Other ground orders'', \code{lwo:sec:research}); its batch-80 note records
that \cref{swo:st:thm:transition} gives the case $n=0$, $\lambda=\mu$ for
$X=\No_{<\kappa}$, except at a singular strong limit. For $\lambda=\mu\cdot\gamma$,
$0<\gamma<\mu^+$, and every $n<\omega$, \cref{swo:cb:new:thm:block-types} gives
$\WO_{\lambda+n}(\No_{<\kappa})\equi\B_{\rho_\kappa\cdot\gamma}\times_\lex n!$, and
by \cref{swo:cb:new:lem:finite-capacity} two such products with nonzero limit
lengths are equimorphic only when the lengths are equal. So the display holds
whenever $\kappa$ is not a singular strong limit ($\rho_\kappa=\mu$). At a
singular strong limit ($\mu=\kappa$, $\rho_\kappa=\kappa\cdot\kappa$) it holds
exactly when $\kappa\cdot\kappa\cdot\gamma=\kappa\cdot\gamma$, that is, when
$\gamma=\kappa^\omega\cdot\delta$ for some ordinal $\delta$ (then
$\kappa\cdot\gamma=\gamma$, by Cantor normal form to base $\kappa$); it fails
for $\gamma=1,2,\kappa,\ldots$. The display is therefore not simply false at
singular strong limits. Strata $\lambda$ that are not multiples of $\mu$
(the first is $\mu+\omega$), cutoffs that are not cardinals, and other alphabets
remain open, as source~15 says in \cref{swo:vi:sub:cb-boundary}.
\end{remark}

% ---- hand chunk baselines
\section{Set-sized baselines and warnings in sources 13--18}\label{swo:vi:sec:baselines}

\merge\ Every batch-81 source restates the set-sized baseline of the reals
report and of Part~I (\cref{swo:sw:fixed,swo:sw:setspectrum}): cardinality
$2^\mu$, universality for orders of size at most $\mu$, and the exact ordinal
spectrum $\beta<\mu^+$. They are recorded here once, with the sources' own
provenance statements, followed by the set-level warnings of sources~13, 14
and~17. None of these statements is new.

\begin{note}[Source~16, Theorem~4.1, ``Basic set-alphabet results'']\label{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum}\label{swo:ds:sec:setbaseline}
Source~16's Section~4 fixes ``an infinite linearly ordered set $X$ of
cardinality $\mu$'' and records that exhaustive enumerations are
prefix-incomparable and that an injective prefix of length less than $\mu$
extends to a bijection of every ordinal type $\alpha$ with $\card\alpha=\mu$.
\emph{The orders $\WO(X)$ and $\WO_\mu(X)$ have cardinality $2^\mu$. Every
linear order of cardinality at most $\mu$ embeds into $\WO_\mu(X)$. For every
ordinal $\beta$, $\beta\emb\WO(X)$ if and only if $\beta<\mu^+$, and the same
statement holds with $\WO_\mu(X)$ in place of $\WO(X)$, and for the reverse of
$\beta$.} Its proof is the pair-orientation, initial-segment and
coordinate-stabilization proof of \cref{swo:sw:fixed}. Source~16's status line:
``Theorem~4.1 is background from the ProveIt reals and surreal reports; it is
reproved here to make the later arguments self-contained. In particular,
`all ordinals embed' is false at every fixed set stage.''

% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 438-469 (chunk lines 2-33)
\emph{Birthday cutoffs and a singular distinction} (source~16's
Subsection~4.1).
For $\No_{<\kappa}$, $\kappa$ an infinite cardinal, substitute
$\mu=2^{<\kappa}$ for the alphabet cardinal in
\Cref{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum}. The ordinal embedding cutoff is $\mu^+$,
not automatically $\kappa^+$. The earlier report also proves a sharper
binary-coding distinction:
\[
 \WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\equi
 \begin{cases}
 2^{\kappa\cdot\kappa}_{\lex},
      &\kappa\text{ is a singular strong-limit cardinal},\\
 2^\mu_{\lex},&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\tag{S16.2}\label{swo:ds:eq:singular}
\]
Here $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ is ordinal multiplication. This statement is
equimorphism, meaning embeddings in both directions, not isomorphism.

The upper coding mechanism replaces a negative sign by $00$, a positive
sign by $11$, and termination by $01$. These codes are prefix-free and
preserve numerical comparison. At a regular stage, a sum of fewer than
$\kappa$ code lengths remains below $\kappa$. At a singular strong-limit
stage it can already reach $\kappa$ before all $\kappa$ coordinates have
been processed; padding every entry to length $\kappa$ gives total length
$\kappa\cdot\kappa$. The matching lower bound in the report uses a
cofinal sequence of sign lengths and an order-preserving row scheduler
(\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}).
We do not reclassify the binary coding ranks of all longer strata here.
The topological classification below, by contrast, covers every admissible
enumeration length and every infinite alphabet.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~18, Theorem~3.1, ``Set-sized universality and the exact ordinal spectrum'']\label{swo:sh:thm:set-spectrum}\label{swo:sh:sec:fixed}
Source~18's Section~3 defines $\WO(X)$ and $\WO_\alpha(X)$ as here, notes that
two exhaustive enumerations are never proper prefixes of one another, so that
no termination convention is needed, and that ``labels are essential: two
two-point well-orders can be isomorphic as abstract orders and still have
different first numerical labels''. \emph{Let $X$ be an infinite linear order of
cardinality $\mu$. Then $\card{\WO(X)}=\card{\WO_\mu(X)}=2^\mu$. Every linear
order of cardinality at most $\mu$ embeds into $\WO_\mu(X)$. An ordinal $\beta$
embeds into $\WO(X)$ if and only if $\beta<\mu^+$. The same exact spectrum holds
for reverse ordinals and for every nonempty stratum $\WO_\alpha(X)$.} The proof
is that of \cref{swo:sw:fixed}; source~18 adds that the stabilization argument
``uses the regularity of the successor cardinal $\mu^+$, not regularity of
$\mu$'', and that cardinality $2^\mu$ is a poor guide, since such an order
contains no increasing sequence of type $\mu^+$. Its subsection ``What the
existing binary-rank result adds'' restates \cref{swo:st:thm:transition}
(binary coding length $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ at a singular strong-limit cutoff,
$\mu$ otherwise), calls it ``background rather than a dependency of our new
cut-spectrum proof'', and adds: ``Replacing these ordinal sums by
cardinalities would erase a genuine distinction. Our results below add
topological information not determined by this coding length.''
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Source~15, Lemma~3.2 and Theorem~3.3, ``Set-sized baseline, from the prior reports'']\label{swo:cb:lem:prefixfree}\label{swo:cb:thm:sets}\label{swo:cb:sec:baseline}
Source~15's Lemma~3.2 (``Exhaustiveness removes prefix ambiguity''):
\emph{distinct exhaustive bijective enumerations of the same set have a first
unequal coordinate in both domains; neither is a proper prefix of the other.}
Its Theorem~3.3 is the baseline of \cref{swo:ds:thm:setspectrum} for every
nonempty fixed-type slice, proved as in \cref{swo:sw:fixed}, with the fixed type
$\alpha=\lambda+n$ handled by reserving $n$ final labels and orienting pairs
indexed by $\lambda$. Source~15 says: ``This proof uses no special property of
real or surreal arithmetic. It is reproduced to fix the dependence of the
later theorems; its content is already in the audited reports. If $X$ is
finite of size $n$, the whole order is the finite chain of its $n!$
permutations. The empty carrier has one empty well-order.'' Its subsections on
birthday cutoffs and on set-length words, which close its Section~3, follow.
Its Lemma~3.1 (fresh separators) belongs to the class layer and is recorded
with the other batch-81 restatements of \cref{swo:lem:delete}.
\end{note}

% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 251-365 (chunk lines 77-124)

\subsection{Source 15's birthday cutoffs and set-length words}
\label{swo:vi:sub:cb-base}

\merge\ The last two subsections of source~15's Section~3; its $X_\kappa$ is
printed $\No_{<\kappa}$, and its forward reference to its Section~8 points to
\cref{swo:cb:new:sec:block-types}.

\subsubsection{Birthday cutoffs}

For an infinite initial cardinal $\kappa$, let
\[
 X_\kappa=\No_{<\kappa},\qquad \mu=|X_\kappa|=2^{<\kappa}.
\]
Counting sign levels proves the cardinality formula: the supremum of their
cardinalities is at least $\kappa$, and the union of $\kappa$ levels does
not exceed it. The preceding theorem gives
\[
 |\WO(X_\kappa)|=2^{2^{<\kappa}},\qquad
 \beta\emb\WO(X_\kappa)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ 
 \beta<(2^{<\kappa})^+.
\]
At regular $\kappa$, a cut using fewer than $\kappa$ labels has birthdays
bounded below $\kappa$, and the usual cut construction stays in the
cutoff. Singular $\kappa$ need not have this property. The fixed-type
calculation in \Cref{swo:cb:sec:fixed} makes the distinction visible even
when the cardinalities and ordinal spectra coincide.

\subsubsection{Why all set-length words are a different order}

If $X$ varies, the class of all pairs $(X,R)$ with $X$ a set of surreals
and $R$ a well-order is a legitimate class of sets. Its ordinal
enumerations are injective set-length words. Proper prefixes now occur.
For example $(0)$ is a prefix of $(0,1)$. One may put shorter words first,
shorter words last, or use a termination symbol placed inside the alphabet.
These comparisons agree on exhaustive enumerations of a fixed carrier but
need not agree on variable carriers.

The prior report gives an explicit self-delimiting sign code embedding all
set-length surreal words, with shorter-first order, into $\No$. Its code
also exhibits why this order need not be isomorphic to $\No$: a word of
nonzero limit length is the supremum of its shorter prefixes, whereas a
surreal point has no set-sized cofinal family below it. Restricting to
successor-length words removes that particular obstruction and yields
a class order isomorphic to $\No$ in $\GBC$ (this report's
\cref{swo:part:words}, source~11's Sections~9--11 there).
An embedding theorem therefore must not be strengthened to an isomorphism
theorem merely because both carriers are proper classes.

% ---- source 13 (batch-81 manuscript 01), lines 417-492
\subsection{Source 13's fixed-set section}\label{swo:fs:swf:sec:fixed}

\merge\ Source~13's Section~4, ``The fixed-set theory and birthday
fragments'', printed whole; its theorem, a restatement credited by source~13
itself, is printed as a note, and its $X_\kappa$ is printed $\No_{<\kappa}$.

The set-sized layer is important both in its own right and as a safe
formalization starting point. We record its already-established results
with their provenance rather than repeating the earlier report in full.

\begin{note}[Source~13, Theorem~4.1, ``Fixed-alphabet results from the repository'']\label{swo:fs:swf:thm:fixed}
Let $X$ be an infinite linearly ordered set, $|X|=\mu$, and compare
exhaustive enumerations using its label order. Then
\[
 |\W(X)|=2^\mu,
 \qquad
 \beta\emb\W(X)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \beta<\mu^+,
\]
and the same spectrum holds for reversed ordinals. Every linear order
of cardinality at most $\mu$ embeds in $\W(X)$.
\par\emph{Provenance and proof mechanisms} (source~13).
These are the general-alphabet results of the repository's real
well-ordering report, carried into Section 6 of the surreal report
(\cite{ProveItReals}; \cref{swo:sw:fixed}). The cardinal lower bound codes independent
binary choices by pair reversals in an enumeration; the upper bound
counts well-order relations on $X$. Universality uses binary codes for
initial segments of a small order. The ordinal upper bound is a
prefix-stabilization obstruction, stronger than a cardinality bound.
We invoke that established written theorem here; this paragraph is not
a replacement proof of its stabilization argument.
\end{note}

For an infinite cardinal $\kappa$, let
\[
       \No_{<\kappa},\qquad \mu=2^{<\kappa}.
\]
Counting signs gives $|\No_{<\kappa}|=\mu$: there are $2^{|\alpha|}$ sign
sequences of length $\alpha$, and the union over $\alpha<\kappa$ has
that supremum cardinality. Thus the ordinal spectrum of
$\W(\No_{<\kappa})$ is exactly the ordinals below $(2^{<\kappa})^+$.
This does not say that only ordinals below the birthday cutoff embed.

\subsubsection{A warning about the minimal-length layer}

Write $\B_\theta=(\{0,1\}^\theta,<_{\lex})$, and write $L\equi M$ for
mutual order embeddability. The merged surreal report proves the
following sharper distinction (\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}, Theorem~8.3 of
the report at its pin):
\[
 \W_\mu(X_\kappa)\equi
 \begin{cases}
  \B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa}, &\kappa\text{ singular strong limit},\\
  \B_\mu, &\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \mu=2^{<\kappa}.
\]
The multiplication in $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ is \emph{ordinal}
multiplication. At a singular strong limit, $\mu=\kappa$, but the
lengths $\kappa$ and $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ need not have the same
lexicographic effect. This is an existing result, not an advance of the
present manuscript.

There is no automatic passage from equimorphism to isomorphism, from a
minimal-length stratum to all strata, or from independently chosen
cutoff embeddings to a coherent global map. The last passage requires
agreement on overlaps. These distinctions become especially visible in
the product obstruction below.

\subsubsection{Set-sized approximations to the new cut theorem}

For an arbitrary set alphabet, prefix cylinders can terminate by
exhaustion and can have extrema. The crossing arguments of
source~13's Section~8 (its reconstruction of cuts from prefix cylinders) still provide a useful tree description, but
its clean all-ordinal formulation depends on two genuinely global facts:
every set prefix has a completion, and after deleting its range there
are fresh labels on both sides of any remaining set cut. A bounded
alphabet need not have either property at its boundary. Therefore the
new global theorem is not asserted by simply replacing $\No$ by every
birthday fragment in its statement.

\begin{note}[Source~14, Theorem~2.1, ``Set-carrier baseline, inherited'']\label{swo:tc:thm:setbaseline}
\emph{In $\ZFC$, if $\card X=\mu$ is infinite, then $\card{\WO(X)}=2^\mu$ and
$\beta\emb\WO(X)$ if and only if $\beta<\mu^+$, for ordinals $\beta$. Every
linear order of cardinality at most $\mu$ embeds in $\WO(X)$. Adjacent pairs
are exactly the consecutive permutations of a finite residual alphabet after a
common prefix.} This is \cref{swo:sw:fixed} with source~11's adjacency theorem
(reals report \code{lwo:thm:adjacency}). Source~14 prints it as ``inherited from
the repository, not a new result of this continuation'' and gives no proof:
``The complete proof is in the predecessor report; we do not substitute
counting for that argument.'' Its set-level remark follows.
\end{note}

% ---- source 14 (batch-81 manuscript 02), lines 257-306 (chunk lines 26-55)

\merge\ The rest of source~14's Subsection~2.2, ``Fixed carriers and the
inherited spectra''.

The size assertion is plausible for two different reasons: a relation on
$X$ has at most $2^\mu$ possibilities, while independently choosing the
orientation of $\mu$ disjoint pairs gives $2^\mu$ distinct well-orders.
The ordinal upper bound is a lexicographic obstruction, not a cardinality
bound: $2^\mu$ can be much larger than $\mu^+$. The complete proof is in
the predecessor report; we do not substitute counting for that argument.

For an infinite cardinal $\kappa$, sign sequences give
\[
 |\No_{<\kappa}|=2^{<\kappa}.
\]
Thus \Cref{swo:tc:thm:setbaseline} applies with $\mu=2^{<\kappa}$.
The repository also proves a more refined minimal-slice dichotomy:
with $\mathbb B_\delta=(\{0,1\}^{\delta},<_{\rm lex})$,
\[
 \W_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\equim
 \begin{cases}
   \mathbb B_{\kappa\cdot\kappa},&\kappa\text{ is singular strong limit},\\
   \mathbb B_\mu,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Here $\cdot$ is \emph{ordinal} multiplication and $\equim$ means mutual
order embeddability, not isomorphism. This inherited result should not be
confused with the support-cardinal theorem proved below: birthday bounds
restrict the alphabet, whereas support bounds restrict how much of a
fixed global enumeration is changed.

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 189-217
\subsection{Source 17's labelled well-orders of a fixed set}\label{swo:vi:sub:gs-base}

\merge\ Source~17's Subsection~2.1, from its map of interpretations.
Let $(X,<)$ be a set with a specified linear order. A well-ordering $R$ of
$X$ has a unique exhaustive enumeration
$e_R:\alpha_R\longrightarrow X$, where $\alpha_R$ is its ordinal type.
For two distinct well-orders, neither exhaustive enumeration is a proper
prefix of the other: after listing every label once there is no fresh label
to append. Therefore a first unequal coordinate exists, and we define
\[
 R<_{\lex}S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 e_R(\delta)<e_S(\delta),\qquad
 \delta=\min\{\xi:e_R(\xi)\ne e_S(\xi)\}.
\]
Write $\WO(X)$ for all such well-orders, and $\WO_\alpha(X)$ for the
suborder with enumeration type exactly $\alpha$. Equivalently,
$\WO_\alpha(X)$ consists of bijections $\alpha\to X$.
The notation $\WO_\mu(X)$ below always uses the initial ordinal of the
cardinal $\mu=|X|$.

The source establishes, for infinite $|X|=\mu$,
\[
 |\WO(X)|=2^\mu,
 \qquad
 \beta\text{ embeds in }\WO(X)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \beta<\mu^+,
\]
and universality for linear orders of cardinality at most $\mu$
(\cref{swo:sw:setspectrum}; \cite{ProveItReals}). These are labelled-order results. Quotienting
well-orders by abstract order-isomorphism removes exactly the labels on
which the comparison depends.

supersede it.

\item \emph{Source~11's question on the cut spectrum and point characters of
the minimal slice without $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$} (\cref{swo:n16.7}):

weight and cellularity $\mu^{<\mu}$ (\cref{swo:gs:thm:invariants}), the exact gap
spectrum (\cref{swo:gs:thm:gap-spectrum}), its multiplicities
(\cref{swo:sh:thm:gap-multiplicities}), and the saturation threshold $\eta_r$
(\cref{swo:gs:cor:saturation,swo:cb:new:thm:saturation-threshold,swo:sh:thm:new-cutoff-interpolation}).

\item \emph{The reals report's questions on other alphabets}
(\code{lwo:rk:q:alphabets}, \code{lwo:sp:q:alphabets}): the density and

\part{Words, termination conventions and other comparison orders}\label{swo:part:conventions}

\merge\ (3 October 2026.) The second Part of the batch-81 addition
(\cref{swo:sub:batch81}; notation in \cref{swo:sub:notation81}). Part~II
(\cref{swo:part:words}) compared set-length words with proper prefixes first;
the six batch-81 sources ask what changes when the comparison convention
changes. This Part prints the six sources' maps of the possible
interpretations (\cref{swo:vii:sec:maps}); the isomorphism of the
unrestricted, injective and numerically increasing shorter-first word orders
(\cref{swo:ds:sec:words}); the criterion for every placement of a formal
terminator and the list of all isomorphism types it produces
(\cref{swo:ds:sec:termination}); the exact cut classification of centered
increasing words and of the conventions for the entry zero
(\cref{swo:vii:sec:centered}); and comparison orders other than numerical
enumeration lexicography: inverse ranks, relation tables, restrictions and
normal forms (\cref{swo:cb:sec:alternatives}). \Cref{swo:vii:sec:status}
records which questions the Part answers.

The bases are chosen by generality, as in Part~VI: source~16 for the word
isomorphism and the termination classification (every placement of a
terminator, unrestricted and injective words), source~18 for centered
increasing words and the zero conventions (the only source treating them),
source~15 for the other comparison orders (the only source proving theorems
about them). Source~13's central termination theorem and source~18's
centered theorem are the same special case of source~16's criterion, proved
independently, with the same letter code; they are printed as routes.
Source~18 was written before sources~13--17 arrived. Re-proofs of Part~II
are printed as notes. Source~16 uses the macro names of this report for other
objects (its \verb|\Words| is the class of \emph{all} words, its \verb|\I| the
class injections); here they are printed $\WordsAll$ and $\Jinj$, and
$\Words$ keeps its meaning of injective words (\cref{swo:sub:notation81}).
Labels written for the merge in this Part begin \code{swo:vii:}.

\section{The six sources' maps of interpretations}\label{swo:vii:sec:maps}

\merge\ Each batch-81 source opens with a map of what ``all well-orders of
the surreal numbers'' can mean, as sources~12, 09 and~08 did
(\cref{swo:sec:intros}). The six maps are printed here, each whole, because
their rows on varying carriers, termination, increasing subsets, relation
codes and abstract types are the subject of this Part; the subsections on a
fixed set carrier that sources~14 and~17 put inside their maps are printed in

% ---- source 13 (batch-81 manuscript 01), lines 206-321
\subsection{Source 13: the interpretations that must be separated}\label{swo:fs:sec:interpretations}

\subsubsection{A taxonomy by carrier and representation}

The following table fixes the principal meanings. In every row, numerical
comparison of surreal labels is distinct from the auxiliary well-order
being represented.

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.22\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.38\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.32\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Interpretation & Representation & Logical size and caveat\\
\midrule\endhead
Fixed set $X\subseteq\No$ & Well-order relations on $X$, equivalently
bijections $\alpha\to X$ from set ordinals & A set; every possible
well-order type on $X$ is permitted.\\[4pt]
Fixed length on $X$ & Bijections $\lambda\to X$ & A single stratum;
$|\lambda|=|X|$ is necessary.\\[4pt]
Arbitrary set subsets & Injective words $p:\alpha\to\No$ & An ordinary
class of sets; termination must be specified.\\[4pt]
Words with repetitions & Arbitrary $p:\alpha\to\No$ & An ordinary
class, but not a class of well-orderings of the ranges.\\[4pt]
Inherited numerical order & Subsets on which the restriction of $<$ is
a well-order & Not arbitrary relabelings; increasing enumerations only.\\[4pt]
Set-like whole-class orders & Class bijections $E:\Ord\to\No$ & The
virtual domain $\Hsl$; its elements are classes.\\[4pt]
Unrestricted whole-class orders & Class well-order relations on $\No$ &
The virtual domain $\Hall$; proper-class predecessor segments are allowed.\\[4pt]
Unlabeled order types & Quotients by order-isomorphism & Label-sensitive
lexicographic comparison does not descend.\\[4pt]
Relation-code order & Characteristic bits of a relation, read in a chosen
order of pairs & A different, coding-dependent lexicographic order.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

This covers the standard semantic axes, not every artificial encoding one
could invent. Birthday-first comparison, a fixed global well-order of the
labels in place of their numerical order, or comparison of unquotiented
Conway expressions each changes the problem again.

\subsubsection{Fixed carriers have no end-marker ambiguity}

For a set $X$, put
\[
 \W(X)=\{e:\alpha\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X:
                   \alpha\text{ an ordinal}\},\qquad
 \W_\lambda(X)=\{e\in\W(X):\dom(e)=\lambda\}.
\]
These are sets: if $|X|=\mu$ is infinite, the possible domains are among
the ordinals below $\mu^+$ of cardinality $\mu$.

\begin{lemma}[Prefix-freeness of exhaustive enumerations]\label{swo:fs:lem:prefixfree}
Distinct members of $\W(X)$ are never proper prefixes of one another.
Their lexicographic comparison is determined at a least common coordinate
where their values differ.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
