A prefix that already enumerates all of $X$ cannot be extended injectively
by another member of $X$. If two different enumerations agreed on their
common domain, the shorter would be such a prefix. Otherwise their set
of disagreement coordinates has a least member.
\end{proof}

An auxiliary well-order $R$ on $X$ has one ordinal type and one increasing
enumeration in that order. This is a bijection between relation
representations and the displayed exhaustive words. Lexicographic
comparison uses the \emph{numerical} order of their values, not the order
$R$ itself.

\subsubsection{Varying the range introduces a genuine convention}

An injective word $p:\alpha\to\No$ enumerates a well-order of its range.
Once ranges may differ, $p$ can be a proper prefix of another word. Under
\emph{end-first} dictionary comparison, $p<p\cat x$ for every fresh $x$.
Under end-last comparison the inequality is reversed. A terminal symbol
placed inside an enlarged alphabet gives a third possibility.

Deleting repeated letters from arbitrary words is not a harmless change
of representation. For example, with $a<b$, the two words $(a,a,b)$ and
$(a,b)$ have different dictionary positions but the same first-occurrence
enumeration. No quotient order is supplied merely by performing this
deletion.

\subsubsection{Inherited well-orders and unlabeled quotients}

The numerical order of all surreals is not a well-order: it contains, for
example, the descending sequence $0,-1,-2,\ldots$. Its ordinal subclass,
represented by all-plus signs, is a proper-class increasing well-order.
Every set ordinal is likewise realized as a numerically well-ordered
subset of $\No$.

The class of \emph{set-sized} numerically increasing words is an ordinary
class of set codes. Under end-first comparison it has an empty minimum
and the limit-prefix phenomenon proved in \Cref{swo:fs:sec:words}.
It therefore differs from the surreal line despite containing a copy of
$\No$ through singleton words. The general class-universality argument
below embeds it back into $\No$: equimorphism is not isomorphism.

If labels are forgotten altogether, lexicographic comparison is usually
lost. On a two-element labeled carrier the two enumerations have the
same ordinal type but opposite lexicographic positions. More dramatically,
every individual set-like global well-order of $\No$ has type $\Ord$;
its quotient by abstract order-isomorphism has just one element, although
$\Hsl$ itself is extremely rich.

\subsubsection{The Kanovei--Shelah comparison is related, not identical}

Kanovei and Shelah's definable hyperreal construction uses a linearly
ordered family of maps from the continuum into $\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$
whose ranges are ultrafilters. The maps are compared lexicographically;
injectivity is not the defining requirement \cite{KanoveiShelah}.
This is an important motivation for ordered collections of enumeration
codes. It should not be identified without argument with the exhaustive
well-orderings of one fixed labeled carrier. In particular, replacing
its index by ``all surreal well-orders'' would change both the coding
and the logical size level.

% ---- source 14 (batch-81 manuscript 02), lines 211-256
\subsection{Source 14: which interpretation is intended?}
\label{swo:tc:sec:interpretations}
\subsubsection{A map of the natural meanings}
In the table below, ``ordinary class'' means a collection of sets defined
by a class predicate. ``Virtual'' means that each candidate is itself a
class and the theory quantifies over candidates without forming their
collection as another class.

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.22\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.40\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.29\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Interpretation & Objects and comparison & Size and main warning\\
\midrule\endhead
Fixed set $X\subseteq\No$ & All well-order relations on $X$, compared
by their exhaustive labelled ordinal enumerations. & A set; arbitrary
well-order type of cardinality $|X|$ is allowed.\\
Fixed length $\alpha$ & Bijections $\alpha\to X$, numerical
first-disagreement comparison. & A set; one slice of the preceding order.\\
Birthday fragment & $X=\No_{<\theta}$, with either of the preceding
choices. & A set; the ordinal cutoff and cardinality play different roles.\\
Variable set domain & Pairs $(A,R)$ with $A\subseteq\No$ a set and $R$
a well-order of $A$, or injective set-length surreal words. & An ordinary
proper class; a termination convention is indispensable.\\
Unrestricted words & All functions $\alpha\to\No$ for set ordinals
$\alpha$, allowing repeated values. & An ordinary proper class; these are
not enumerations of well-ordered labelled subsets.\\
Increasing subsets & Subsets or subclasses well-ordered by the
\emph{numerical} order inherited from $\No$. & A restriction on labels,
not an arbitrary imposed well-order.\\
Set-like global order & Class bijections $\Ord\to\No$. & Virtual order
$\Hsl$; each proper initial segment is a set.\\
Arbitrary global order & Class well-order relations on all of $\No$,
including non-set-like ones. & Virtual order $\Hall$; direct labelled
common-prefix comparison is available.\\
Relation-code lex & Characteristic functions of relations, using a fixed
well-ordered schedule of ordered pairs. & A different order; depends on
the schedule and on the bit convention.\\
Order types only & Isomorphism classes after forgetting surreal labels. &
Enumeration lex does not descend. Set-like proper class orders all have
type $\Ord$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

This list covers the main structural ambiguities, rather than pretending
that arbitrary encodings cannot create additional variants. Every
subsequent theorem names the interpretation to which it applies.

\merge\ Source~14's Subsection~2.2 (``Fixed carriers and the inherited
spectra'') is printed in Part~VI (\cref{swo:tc:thm:setbaseline}); its
Subsections~2.3--2.5 follow.

% ---- source 14 (batch-81 manuscript 02), lines 307-365
\subsubsection{Variable domains and the terminal symbol}
An imposed well-order on a set $A\subseteq\No$ is represented uniquely
by an injective word $p:\lambda\to\No$. For varying $A$, a word may be a
proper prefix of another. Three reasonable choices are:
\emph{prefix first}, using $\End<x$ for every numerical letter;
\emph{prefix last}, using $x<\End$; and a marker inserted at a specified
cut in the numerical alphabet. A fourth order is length-first lexicography.
They do not agree, and none becomes a well-order simply because the
word domains are ordinals: the one-letter words already contain the
numerical order of $\No$.

The familiar sign-sequence order of the surreals uses
$-<\End<+$. It is not prefix-first dictionary order. A useful
self-delimiting numerical code replaces $-$ by $00$, $+$ by $11$, and
appends $01$. Comparisons are preserved because
\[
 00<01<11.
\]
The terminal block makes distinct numerical codewords
prefix-incomparable. Concatenating such codes is useful for embeddings,
but does not turn arbitrary repeated-letter words into exhaustive
enumerations. The predecessor report treats the resulting compression
and the special cuts at limit word lengths (\cref{swo:sw:encoding,swo:sw:characters,swo:sw:wordcuts}; Sections~9--11 of this report at the pin).

\subsubsection{Numerically increasing well-orders}
The numerical order of $\No$ is not a well-order; for example,
$0>-1>-2>\cdots$. Hence no numerical order-preserving enumeration can
well-order all of $\No$. This is different from imposing another
well-order on the same carrier.

Proper subclasses can nevertheless be numerically well-ordered and
non-set-like. For instance,
\[
 \left\{-\frac1{\alpha+1}:\alpha\in\Ord\right\}\cup\{0\}
\]
together with numerical order has type $\Ord+1$. The ordinal arithmetic
in the denominator is interpreted by the corresponding surreal ordinal
followed by surreal addition; the displayed values are strictly
increasing and all precede $0$. Thus a numerically bounded subclass can
have a point with a proper class of predecessors. Set-likeness is not a
consequence of numerical boundedness.

\subsubsection{Forgetting labels changes the problem}
For a fixed infinite set of cardinality $\mu$, its possible abstract
well-order types are the ordinals $\alpha$ satisfying
$\mu\leq\alpha<\mu^+$. Ordering these types by ordinal size gives a
well-order of type $\mu^+$, which cannot embed in $\W(X)$ by
\Cref{swo:tc:thm:setbaseline}. Therefore the order on abstract types is
not the induced quotient of enumeration lexicography.

More directly, different enumerations of the same type need not occupy
a convex subset: choose first labels $x<y<z$ and complete the first and
third to type $\mu$, the middle to type $\mu+1$. All three completions
exist, and their lexical positions are decided by those first labels.
For a proper class carrier every set-like well-order has abstract type
$\Ord$, so quotienting that fragment by abstract type collapses it to a
single type. Almost all of the lexicographic information was in the
labels, not in the abstract order type.

% ---- source 15 (batch-81 manuscript 03), lines 159-208
\subsection{Source 15: a map of the interpretations}\label{swo:cb:sec:map}

Every concrete version is determined by six choices: the carrier; whether
orders are labelled or identified up to isomorphism; how an order is coded;
which order compares the code entries; which well-order scans the entries;
and the universe in which all codes are collected. Prefix conventions add
a seventh choice when the coded sequences have variable domains and need
not exhaust a common carrier.

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.34\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Interpretation & Objects and comparison & Main distinction\\
\midrule\endhead
Fixed carrier, all types & All ordinal bijections $e:\alpha\to X$, compared at first unequal labels in $X$ & A set in ZFC; exhaustive codes are prefix-free.\\
Fixed enumeration type & Only $e:\alpha\to X$ for one prescribed $\alpha$ & Same possible ordinal suborders need not mean the same binary coding length.\\
Variable set carrier & Injective set-length words, or all well-ordered sets of surreal labels & Proper prefixes occur; termination must be ordered explicitly.\\
Natural well-ordered subsets & Subsets $A\subseteq\No$ with the inherited numerical order well-founded & No arbitrary rearrangement is part of the data.\\
Set-like class orders & Bijections $\Ord\to\No$; first ordinal difference & All predecessors are sets; a predicate on class functions.\\
All labelled class orders & Arbitrary class well-orders on $\No$; common labelled prefix & May differ only after a proper class of identical earlier labels.\\
Fixed class index type & Bijections $J\to\No$ for one given class well-order $J$ & A chosen type such as $\Ord+\Ord$ restricts the domain.\\
Inverse-rank lexicography & Rank values of labels scanned in a fixed baseline well-order & Uses ordinal values rather than numerical surreal labels; has different endpoints.\\
Relation-table lexicography & Characteristic bits of $R\subseteq\No^2$ in a fixed well-ordered scan & Depends on scan and bit orientation; differs already on three points.\\
Isomorphism types & Quotient well-orders by abstract order isomorphism & Native enumeration lex does not descend to this quotient.\\
Birthday/simplicity variants & Birthday ranks, sign strings, or simplicity predecessors & Birthday ties need a separate well-order; simplicity is only partial.\\
Definable or computable codes & A specified syntactic class with an evaluator & Restricts the available objects; syntax alone does not supply universal truth.\\
Higher-universe totality & All relevant relations become sets in a larger universe & Results depend on the class realization and external well-foundedness.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

This table is exhaustive at the level of the structural choices just
listed, not a claim to enumerate every artificially possible encoding.
Once arbitrary encodings and arbitrary scans are allowed, one can manufacture
many further linearizations. The mathematically meaningful task is to say
which properties are intrinsic to the chosen order and which depend on
its presentation.

\subsubsection{The historical Kanovei--Shelah construction}

The original construction uses
\[
 A=\{a:\mathfrak c\to\mathcal P(\mathbb N):\ran(a)
       \text{ is an ultrafilter on }\mathbb N\},
\]
ordered lexicographically in the initial ordinal $\mathfrak c$ coordinates,
with $\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$ itself ordered through characteristic functions.
Maps may repeat entries, and different maps may represent the same
ultrafilter. This is an important precedent for ordering presentations
without selecting a preferred presentation. It is not literally an order
of all well-orders of $\mathbb R$ or of $\No$ \cite[Section~1]{KanoveiShelah}.

% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 188-276
\subsection{Source 16: a systematic map of the interpretations}\label{swo:ds:sec:interpretations}

Three choices account for most natural readings: the carrier to be
well-ordered, whether numerical labels are retained, and the coding used for
comparison. A fourth choice, the treatment of termination, becomes necessary
when carriers or word lengths vary. There cannot be a literal list of every
possible encoding: composing any encoding with an arbitrary permutation can
manufacture another order. The following table covers the principal
mathematical interpretations and explains how the remaining variants arise.

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.23\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Interpretation & Objects and comparison & Size and main issue\\
\midrule\endhead
Fixed set of labels $X\subseteq\No$ & Bijections $\alpha\to X$; first unequal
entry compared numerically & A set in ZFC; the order on enumerations is
usually far from a well-order.\\[5pt]
Fixed enumeration type & Only bijections $\alpha\to X$, with $\alpha$ fixed &
Changing $\alpha$ changes topology and may change embedding invariants.\\[5pt]
Variable set carrier & Injective set-length words in $\No$ & An ordinary
class of set codes; proper-prefix comparison must be specified.\\[5pt]
Numerically well-ordered subsets & Increasing enumerations in the numerical
order & No arbitrary auxiliary well-order remains on each carrier; proper
class subsets can still be non-set-like.\\[5pt]
Set-like global well-orders & Class bijections $\Ord\to\No$ & A predicate on
class variables; not an ordinary class of such graphs.\\[5pt]
Arbitrary global class well-orders & Class relations on all of $\No$;
compare after the common labelled prefix & Common prefixes can be proper
classes; ordinal-coordinate comparison alone is insufficient.\\[5pt]
Relation-table encoding & Binary membership tables for order relations,
read along an auxiliary well-order of pairs & Gives a different,
coding-dependent lexicographic order.\\[5pt]
Abstract order types & Forget surreal labels and identify isomorphic
well-orders & Numerical first-disagreement comparison no longer descends to
the quotient.\\[5pt]
Larger-universe interpretation & Regard internal classes as sets in an
explicit containing universe & Makes entire collections available, while
changing the meaning of ``set'', ``class'', and ``all''.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\subsubsection{Fixed labels versus abstract types}

For a linearly ordered set $(X,<)$, write
\[
 \WO_\alpha(X)=\{e:\alpha\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X\},\qquad
 \WO(X)=\bigcup_{|\alpha|=|X|}\WO_\alpha(X).
\]
A bijection is identified with the well-order it induces on $X$. Distinct
exhaustive enumerations cannot be proper initial segments of one another:
the shorter enumeration has already used every label, leaving no fresh next
entry for the longer one. Therefore every distinct pair has a first unequal
entry, with no termination convention needed.

If labels are discarded, the order types are simply the ordinals $\alpha$
of cardinality $|X|$. Their ordinary ordinal comparison is meaningful, but
it is not numerical lexicography. Even two permutations of a finite set have
the same abstract order type and different lexicographic positions.
For arbitrary class well-orders, a single class of all abstract ``class
ordinals'' is not supplied by GB or KM. Direct labelled comparison will
avoid this issue entirely.

\subsubsection{Relation tables genuinely change the answer}

Choose an auxiliary well-order $j:\delta\to X\times X$ and encode a relation
$R$ by the binary word $\chi_R(j(\xi))$. The earliest differing bit defines
a linear comparison, but its answer depends on $j$ and the bit convention.
For example, on $X=\{0,1,2\}$ the enumeration $(0,2,1)$ precedes $(1,0,2)$
numerically. In row-major relation-table order, their first differing entry
is the pair $(0,1)$: its bits are respectively $1$ and $0$. The table
comparison therefore gives the opposite inequality. A class version needs
a specified class ordering of the pairs and appropriately stratified
quantification; the word ``lexicographic'' alone does not choose one.

\subsubsection{The Kanovei--Shelah connection}

The motivating analogy should not be mistaken for an identification.
Kanovei and Shelah use the index
\[
 A=\{a:\mathfrak c\to\mathcal P(\mathbb N):
             \ran(a)\text{ is an ultrafilter on }\mathbb N\},
\]
ordered at the first differing coordinate by binary characteristic-function
order on $\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$. Repetitions are permitted, and the displayed
definition admits ultrafilters generally. The point is a definable order on
redundant codes, used in an ultrapower construction. It is not literally the
space of all bijective enumerations or all well-orders of $\mathbb R$
\cite[\S2 in the published version; \S1 in the preprint]{KanoveiShelah}.

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 180-188
\subsection{Source 17: what can ``all well-orders of surreal numbers'' mean?}
\label{swo:gs:sec:meanings}

We use the sign-sequence presentation and numerical order of surreal
numbers; standard background is given in \cite{Conway,Gonshor}.
A well-ordering relation and the numerical order of its labels are distinct.
An enumeration of a well-order need not be numerically increasing. This
single distinction already produces two different research programs.

\merge\ Source~17's Subsection~2.1 (``Labelled well-orders of a fixed set'')
is printed in Part~VI (\cref{swo:vi:sub:gs-base}). Its displayed word code
keeps its number as (S17.1).

% ---- source 17 (batch-81 manuscript 05), lines 218-324
\subsubsection{Set-length words and varying supports}
Every well-order of a set-sized subset of $\No$ is represented by an
injective word $w:\alpha\to\No$. Now one word can be a proper prefix of
another. We use the \emph{shorter-prefix-first} convention unless specified
otherwise. The resulting carrier is an ordinary class of sets. Allowing
repeated entries gives a larger class of words, but such a word no longer
represents a well-order of its range without an additional occurrence
labelling.

There is a useful explicit compression, also present in the source.
Represent $x\in\No$ by its sign sequence. Replace a minus sign by $--$,
a plus sign by $++$, and append the terminal block $-+$. Call the resulting
prefix-free code $c(x)$. The comparison
\[
 --\ <\ -+\ <\ ++
\]
encodes the surreal convention ``minus $<$ termination $<$ plus''.
For any set-length word put
\[\tag{S17.1}\label{swo:gs:eq:wordcode}
 \Phi(w)=\mathop{\mathbin{\cat}}_{i<\alpha}\bigl(+\cat c(w(i))\bigr).
\]
At a first differing entry the prefix-free coefficient codes determine the
comparison; at a proper word prefix the next sign is $+$, so the longer word
is larger. Hence $\Phi$ is an order embedding into $\No$, with birthday
\[
 \bd\Phi(w)=\sum_{i<\alpha}\bigl(1+2\cdot\bd(w(i))+2\bigr).
\]
Products and sums in this formula are ordinal operations. All lengths are
sets. The empty word is least, so the entire word order is not isomorphic
to the endpoint-free surreal line, despite embeddings in both directions.
Using $-$ instead of the leading $+$ in each entry block gives the
longer-prefix-first convention and makes the empty word greatest.

Length-first comparison, often called shortlex, is another legitimate
linear order, but is not this dictionary order. It should not be silently
substituted in an embedding or topology theorem.

\subsubsection{Numerically well-ordered subsets}
A different interpretation asks for subsets $A\subseteq\No$ whose
\emph{inherited numerical order} is a well-order. Their set-sized
increasing enumerations form a suborder of the injective word order.
They include every singleton, so their lexicographic order contains a copy
of $(\No,<)$ and is not a well-order. They inherit the embedding
\eqref{swo:gs:eq:wordcode}. Every set-sized increasing word admits a longer
increasing extension, because a set of surreal numbers has a strict upper
bound.

There is no numerically increasing exhaustive well-order of the whole
surreal line: the sequence $0,-1,-2,\ldots$ already witnesses the failure of
well-foundedness of the numerical order. This does not prohibit an
arbitrary labelled class well-order of $\No$.

Proper-class \emph{subsets} with their inherited numerical order must
also be distinguished from the entire numerical line. They can be
non-set-like well-orders. For example,
\[
 \left\{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}:\alpha\in\Ord\right\}\cup\{0\}
\]
is increasingly well-ordered in type $\Ord+1$. The reciprocal is surreal
field reciprocal, and $\alpha$ denotes its ordinal surreal value. The
set of predecessors of $0$ in this class order is proper. A collection
of all such class subsets again lives at the higher logical level.
For varying supports, proper-prefix comparisons require an explicit
termination convention, just as for set words. Our main raw comparison
keeps the carrier fixed and therefore has no termination ambiguity.

\subsubsection{Global enumerations and unrestricted relations}
A \emph{set-like} class well-order is one in which each element has a set
of predecessors. A set-like well-order of the proper class $\No$ has order
type $\Ord$, and is equivalently a class bijection
\[
 E:\Ord\longrightarrow\No.
\]
Write $\Hsl$ for this predicate on class relations, and $\Jinj$ for the analogous
predicate of injective, not necessarily exhaustive, $\Ord$-words.
These symbols are not names of ordinary GB classes whose elements are
class functions.

Write $\Hall$ for arbitrary class well-orders of $\No$. Orders of type
$\Ord+1$, $\Ord+\Ord$, and many other non-set-like types belong here.
For example, enumerate $\No\setminus\{a,b\}$ first and append $a,b$.
Interchanging those last two labels creates a first disagreement after a
proper-class prefix, not at a set ordinal.

One can also fix a particular class well-order $J$ and compare bijections
$J\to\No$ at their least differing $J$-position. This is legitimate when
least elements of nonempty admitted subclasses of $J$ are part of the
hypothesis. It is a fixed-domain alternative, not a universal set ordinal
index for every class well-order.

\subsubsection{Relation codes, isomorphism types, and ordinal-valued rankings}
Fix a class bijection $d:\Ord\to\No\times\No$. A class relation $R$ has
binary code $\xi\mapsto\mathbf1_R(d(\xi))$, which can be compared
lexicographically. This yields a valid order on the admitted well-order
relations, but depends on $d$ and the bit convention. It is generally not
the native labelled-enumeration order. Even on $\{0,1\}$, listing coordinate
$(0,1)$ first and using $0<1$ compares the two well-orders in the reverse
of their first-label comparison.

Abstract isomorphism types form yet another subject. For set well-orders
they are ordinals, naturally ordered by initial-segment embeddability.
The native lexicographic order does not descend to this quotient: the two
enumerations $(a,b)$ and $(b,a)$ have the same abstract type but opposite
label order when $a<b$. A rank function, a birthday-first code, or a chosen
well-order of relation codes introduces additional structure rather than
recovering a canonical lexicographic comparison from the abstract types.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 256-301
\subsection{Source 18: interpretations and foundational conventions}
\label{swo:sh:sec:interpretations}

\subsubsection{A map of the natural interpretations}

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Object & What a point represents & Size and comparison\\
\midrule
\endhead
$\WO(X)$, $X\subset\No$ a set & A labelled well-order of the fixed carrier
$X$, in any possible ordinal type & A set; compare unique exhaustive
ordinal enumerations\\[4pt]
$\WO_\alpha(X)$ & A well-order whose type is the specified ordinal
$\alpha$ & A stratum of $\WO(X)$; nonempty exactly when
$|\alpha|=|X|$\\[4pt]
Injective set words & A well-order of a variable set-sized subset of
$\No$ & A genuine proper class of sets; a termination convention is needed\\[4pt]
Increasing set words & A subset well-ordered by the numerical surreal
order, with its unique increasing enumeration & A subclass of the previous
word class; no arbitrary reordering of a fixed subset\\[4pt]
$\Hsl(\No)$ & A set-like global well-order, equivalently a class bijection
$\Ord\to\No$ & A predicate on class relations; distinct enumerations have
a set ordinal first difference\\[4pt]
$\Hall(\No)$ & Any class well-order of the entire surreal carrier & A
predicate on class relations; the common labelled prefix can be proper\\[4pt]
Inherited class well-orders & A subclass on which numerical $<$ is a
well-order & A different restriction: $\No$ itself is not numerically
well-ordered\\[4pt]
Order types modulo isomorphism & Labels are forgotten & Numerical
lexicography does not descend to this quotient\\[4pt]
Characteristic-relation codes & A well-order relation encoded by its
membership bits in a chosen order of pairs & A valid auxiliary lex order,
usually different from enumeration comparison\\[4pt]
Containing-universe objects & All orders admitted by a specified smaller
set/class universe & Sets in an external universe; ``all'' is relative to
the chosen class realization\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

There are indefinitely many artificial encodings. The table covers the
natural mathematical choices by varying carrier, enumeration type,
admissible order, label retention, comparison rule, and size level. An
arbitrary encoding cannot be expected to preserve the results for another
row.

\merge\ Source~18's Subsections~2.2 and~2.3 (sign sequences with its lemma
on surreal order tools, and set and class theories) are foundations, not
interpretations, and are not printed in this Part. Its Subsection~2.4
follows.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 391-403
\subsubsection{Why the Kanovei--Shelah connection needs qualification}

Kanovei and Shelah's published construction \cite[\S2]{KanoveiShelah}
orders maps $a:\mathfrak c\to\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$ whose ranges are
ultrafilters, using characteristic-function lexicography on
$\mathcal P(\mathbb N)$. Repetitions are allowed in those maps and in
the ultrafilter presentation. Their index is therefore not literally
$\WO(\mathbb R)$ or a permutation order of the reals. The useful
connection is the method of linearly ordering many codes without
choosing one privileged code. The present article studies different,
precisely specified orders inspired by that method. This correction
was already explicit in the repository's reals report \cite{ProveItReals}.

\begin{note}[\merge; what the six maps share]\label{swo:vii:note:maps}
All six separate the same carriers as the batch-80 sources
(\cref{swo:sec:objects,swo:sk:sec:question}): a fixed set with all types or
one type, varying set carriers (injective words, with a termination
convention), unrestricted words, numerically well-ordered subsets, set-like
and arbitrary class well-orders, relation codes and abstract types.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Termination.} Sources~13, 14, 16 and~17 name the three placements
of a terminator (first, last, inside the alphabet) and length-first order as
a fourth choice; source~14's warning that the Conway sign order uses
$-<\End<+$ and is not prefix-first dictionary order is the convention printed in
\cref{swo:sec:prelim}.
\item \emph{Increasing subclasses.} The example
$\{-1/(\alpha+1):\alpha\in\Ord\}\cup\{0\}$ of numerical type $\Ord+1$ is
source~12's (\cref{swo:st:sec:subclasses}); sources~14, 15, 16, 17 and~18
repeat it.
\item \emph{Relation codes.} The two- and three-point examples of
sources~14, 16, 17 and~18 make the point of source~12's shipped example and
of \cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode}; source~15 proves theorems about these
orders (\cref{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints,swo:cb:cor:tablepatterns}).
\item \emph{Abstract types.} That enumeration lexicography does not descend to
abstract order types (sources~13, 14, 15, 16, 17) is printed with source~15's
non-convexity example in \cref{swo:cb:sec:alternatives}; source~14's argument
that the type order $\mu^+$ cannot embed uses \cref{swo:tc:thm:setbaseline}.
\item \emph{Kanovei--Shelah.} The Kanovei--Shelah subsections of sources~13, 15, 16 and~18
printed here (sources~14 and~17 discuss it elsewhere in their texts) make
source~08's point (\cref{swo:sk:sec:KS}): the index there is a
lexicographically ordered family of redundant codes, not a space of
well-orders. Source~16 cites Section~2 of the published version and Section~1
of the preprint; source~15 cites ``Section~1''.
\item \emph{Prefix-freeness.} Source~13's \cref{swo:fs:lem:prefixfree} is the
prefix-freeness of exhaustive enumerations used in \cref{swo:sw:fixed}.
\end{itemize}
\end{note}

% ---- hand chunk words
% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 735-830
\section{Shorter-first words: three classes, one prefix order (source 16; routes of sources 13, 14 and 18)}\label{swo:ds:sec:words}

\merge\ Source~16's Section~7, printed whole; its $\mathcal T$,
$\mathcal T^{\mathrm{inj}}$, $\mathcal T^{\mathrm{inc}}$ are printed $\WordsAll$,
$\Words$, $\WordsInc$ (\cref{swo:sub:notation81}), and its equations keep
their numbers as (S16.$n$). Its references to its own fresh-separator lemma,
universality proposition, set-cut display and raw-comparison section are
printed as references to \cref{swo:lem:delete,swo:prop:universal,swo:n3.1}
and \cref{swo:cf:lex}, which state the same facts. The word orders of Part~II
(\cref{swo:part:words}) are the cases $\WordsAll$ and $\Words$; what this
section adds is the increasing class $\WordsInc$ and the isomorphism of the
three (\cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism}). Source~18 proves the isomorphism
independently, with the uniformity of the back-and-forth made explicit
(\cref{swo:vii:sub:sh-tree}); source~13's and source~18's word sections and
source~14's explicit codes follow as notes and routes.

Let $\WordsAll$ be the class of all set functions $f:\alpha\to\No$ with
$\alpha$ an ordinal. Let $\Words$ be its injective subclass, and
$\WordsInc$ its numerically strictly increasing subclass. Members of all
three are sets. In $\Words$ the word imposes a well-order on its image;
in $\WordsInc$ that well-order is the restriction of the numerical order.
Initially give all three the convention that a proper prefix is smaller
than its extension.

The empty word is then a minimum. More substantially, if $f$ has nonzero
limit length $\lambda$, its proper prefixes are cofinal below it, giving
\[
 \cf(\WordsAll_{<f})=\cf(\lambda).
\tag{S16.5}\label{swo:ds:eq:wordcharacter}
\]
At successor-length words there is no set cofinal family below the point,
and above any word there is no set coinitial family. For the unrestricted
and injective cases these facts appear in the earlier report; they follow
by choosing a fresh next letter between the set of active coordinates and
the coordinate of the given word. Equation~\eqref{swo:ds:eq:wordcharacter} already
prevents an isomorphism with $\No$, even if the empty word is removed:
a surreal cannot be the supremum of a set lying strictly below it, by
the set-cut property (\cref{swo:n3.1}).

\begin{theorem}[Three interpretations with the same prefix order]
\label{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism}
In GBC,
\[
 \WordsAll\cong \Words\cong \WordsInc
\]
under shorter-prefix-first comparison. There are isomorphisms preserving
word length, the prefix relation, unions of set-length prefix chains, and
every singleton word. The same assertion holds for the convention that
proper prefixes are larger than their extensions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For an admissible output prefix $q$, its possible next letters form,
respectively,
\[
 \No,\qquad \No\setminus\ran(q),\qquad
 U(q)=\{x\in\No:\forall y\in\ran(q)\ (y<x)\}.
\]
Each is a proper class with the set-cut property. For the second use
the fresh-separator lemma (\cref{swo:lem:delete}). For the third, given sets $A<B$ in $U(q)$,
fill the cut $A\cup\ran(q)<B$; this also proves the property with an
empty side. Uniform back-and-forth as in
class universality (\cref{swo:prop:universal}) gives, for every set parameter $q$, an
order isomorphism $h_q$ from $\No$ onto its available-letter class.
Take $h_\varnothing$ to be the identity. A single class relation
$H(q,x,y)$ defines all these maps: a pair belongs when it appears in the
uniquely specified set-length back-and-forth history. No choice of a
proper class for each index is hidden here.

For a free word $f:\alpha\to\No$, define its output by set recursion:
\[
 q(i)=h_{q\res i}(f(i))\qquad(i<\alpha).
\]
The output is injective or increasing according to the target rule.
Conversely, an admissible output $q$ uniquely recovers
$f(i)=h_{q\res i}^{-1}(q(i))$. These operations are inverse and preserve
prefixes and lengths. At a first differing coordinate, both inputs use
the same strictly increasing local map, so their comparison is preserved.
The endpoint termination rule depends only on the prefix relation, which
is also preserved. The recursive definition makes compatibility with
unions immediate, and the choice at the empty prefix fixes singletons.
\end{proof}

This is an isomorphism theorem, not a literal identification of the objects.
Longer labels generally change. In particular, the theorem does not turn
an arbitrary chosen well-order of a subset into the numerical order on
the \emph{same} subset. It shows that the bare lexicographic order does not
recover that conceptual distinction.

\subsection{Increasing proper classes are a further case}

The preceding theorem concerns set-length words. A numerically well-ordered
proper subclass can itself be non-set-like. For example, the increasing
class
\[
 \left\{-\frac1{\xi+1}:\xi\in\Ord\right\}\cup\{0\}
\]
has order type $\Ord+1$; the point $0$ has a proper class of predecessors.
The simpler subclass of nonnegative ordinal surreals has type $\Ord$.
In GBC, class universality (\cref{swo:prop:universal}) embeds every class well-order
into the numerical surreal line, so every such abstract order can occur
on a numerically well-ordered subclass. No increasing enumeration can
exhaust $\No$: it has a first value and omits every smaller surreal.

If distinct proper class carriers are allowed, comparison must also say
what happens when one whole labelled class order is an initial segment of
another. The direct common-prefix method of Part~III (\cref{swo:cf:lex}), with
an explicitly placed termination symbol, supplies the corresponding
variant. It is a different problem from comparing well-orders on the same
fixed carrier.

\begin{note}[\merge; what transfers from Part~II]\label{swo:vii:note:partII}
Display (S16.5) and the two sentences after it are \cref{swo:sw:character}
(sources~11 and~09: \cref{swo:ec:prop:prefix-cf}). Because the isomorphisms of
\cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism} preserve length and the prefix relation,
every statement of Part~II about $\WordsAll$ or $\Words$ that is phrased by
lengths and prefixes holds in $\GBC$ for $\WordsInc$: the exact local
characters (\cref{swo:sw:character}), the word-cut classification
(\cref{swo:sw:cutclassification}) and the successor-word corollary
(\cref{swo:sw:successors}). Source~18 makes this remark for the cut
classification (\cref{swo:sh:sec:dictionary-words}); its direct proof of the
characters of $\WordsInc$ needs no global choice
(\cref{swo:sh:prop:dictionary-characters}). Part~II's explicit code
(\cref{swo:sw:code}) restricts to $\WordsInc$ without choice. The example of
source~16's Subsection~7.1 is source~12's (\cref{swo:st:sec:subclasses}).
\end{note}

% ---- source 13 (batch-81 manuscript 01), lines 493-533
\subsection{Source 13: set-length words and their first obstruction}\label{swo:fs:sec:words}

\merge\ Source~13's Section~5, printed whole. Its proposition is
\cref{swo:sw:character}~(1) of Part~II (and source~09's
\cref{swo:ec:prop:prefix-cf}) and is printed as a note.

Let $\WordsAll$ be the ordinary class of all set-length words over $\No$, and
let $\I\subseteq \WordsAll$ be the injective words. The latter is exactly the
class of well-orderings of arbitrary set-sized subsets, represented by
their increasing enumeration in the auxiliary order. In this section the
terminal symbol precedes every label.

\begin{note}[Source~13, Proposition~5.1, ``Limit-prefix character'']\label{swo:fs:prop:wordlimit}
\emph{
Suppose $p$ has nonzero limit length $\lambda$. In either $\WordsAll$ or $\I$
when $p$ is injective, the proper prefixes of $p$ are cofinal below $p$.
The cofinality of the strict lower side at $p$ is exactly $\cf(\lambda)$.}
This is \cref{swo:sw:character}~(1), with the same proof.
\end{note}
\begin{proof}[Source~13's proof]
If $q<p$, then either $q$ is a proper prefix of $p$, or its comparison
with $p$ is decided by a smaller letter at some coordinate
$\xi<\lambda$. In either case a sufficiently long proper prefix of $p$
lies above $q$ and below $p$. Restricting to a cofinal sequence of
lengths gives the upper bound $\cf(\lambda)$.

For the converse, assign to each $q<p$ a bound $\xi_q<\lambda$ beyond
which proper prefixes of $p$ lie above $q$. A family of cardinality less
than $\cf(\lambda)$ has a common such bound. A later proper prefix then
witnesses its failure to be cofinal.
\end{proof}

Every point of $\No$ has no set-sized cofinal family strictly below it:
a proposed family together with that point is a set cut and has a
strict interpolant. Thus the displayed word orders are not isomorphic
to $\No$, even after the empty minimum is deleted. They nevertheless
embed into $\No$ by class universality (\cref{swo:prop:universal}), and $\No$ embeds
back through singleton words. The earlier report gives explicit
sign-coding embeddings and a more detailed end-first analysis
(\cref{swo:sw:encoding,swo:sw:characters,swo:sw:wordcuts}; Sections~9--11 of this report at the pin).

The same limit-prefix proof applies to numerically increasing set
words, since every proper prefix remains increasing. By reversing the
position of termination, one obtains the corresponding small
\emph{right}-hand character. \Cref{swo:fs:sec:central} proves that a
central termination convention has a fundamentally different outcome.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1480-1573
\subsection{Source 18: set-length dictionary words and numerical increase}
\label{swo:sh:sec:dictionary-words}

Let $\WordsAll$ be the class of all functions $f:\alpha\to\No$
with $\alpha$ a set ordinal, and let $\Words$ and $\WordsInc$
be its injective and strictly numerically increasing subclasses. These
are ordinary classes: their members are set graphs. In $\Words$ a
point represents a well-order of its own range; in $\WordsInc$ that
well-order is the inherited numerical order. No point exhausts $\No$.

First use ordinary dictionary comparison: compare first unequal
entries, and put a proper prefix before every proper extension. This
convention is sometimes described by a formal terminator below all
surreal letters. Such a terminator is not a surreal number.

\begin{note}[Source~18, Proposition~6.1, ``Explicit order coding; repository result'']\label{swo:sh:prop:dictionary-code}
\emph{
There is an explicit order embedding $\WordsAll\emb\No$, requiring
no Global Choice. Singleton words embed $\No$ into each of
$\WordsAll,\Words,\WordsInc$. Thus all three word orders and
$\No$ are equimorphic, but the word orders are not isomorphic to
$\No$.} This is \cref{swo:sw:code}, with the same letter code
$K(x)=C(x)=(+)\cat D(s_x)\cat(+,-)$; source~18 labels it a repository result.
\end{note}
\begin{proof}[Source~18's proof]
Duplicate each sign in the sign expansion $s_x$ and write the result
as $D(s_x)$. The code
\[
 K(x)=(+)\cat D(s_x)\cat(+,-)
\]
is prefix-free and numerically order-preserving. A terminated source
word is correctly compared with a longer one because its delimiter
satisfies $(-,-)<(+,-)<(+,+)$. Concatenate the $K(f(i))$ in ordinal
domain order. Replacement and ordinal summation give a set sign word.
At the first unequal entry prefix-freeness preserves the comparison;
at the end of a proper prefix the next block begins with $+$, so the
shorter word is smaller. The code length is exactly
\[
 \sum_{i<\alpha}\bigl(1+2\cdot\bd(f(i))+2\bigr).
\]
This proves the embedding. The nonisomorphism follows from the
next proposition and holds even after removing the empty word.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[Source~18, Proposition~6.2, ``The prefix-supremum obstruction'']\label{swo:sh:prop:dictionary-characters}
\emph{
The empty word is the minimum of each dictionary word order. For any
word $f$ of nonzero limit length $\alpha$, its proper prefixes are
cofinal below it, and the left character of $f$ is $\cf\alpha$.
At every successor-length word the left segment has no set cofinal
subset. At every word the right segment has no set coinitial subset.
These assertions hold in $\WordsAll$, $\Words$, and $\WordsInc$.} For
$\WordsAll$ and $\Words$ this is \cref{swo:sw:character}. The case
$\WordsInc$ also follows in $\GBC$ from \cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism},
which preserves lengths and prefixes; source~18's proof below needs no global
choice.
\end{note}
\begin{proof}[Source~18's proof]
At a limit length, a smaller word is either a proper prefix or differs
at a coordinate below $\alpha$. A sufficiently long proper prefix
exceeds it. A cofinal set of prefix lengths therefore gives the upper
bound $\cf\alpha$; fewer than that many comparison coordinates are
bounded and cannot be cofinal. At successor length, interpolate at
the last entry above all relevant lower next values and below that
entry. In the injective case avoid earlier labels; in the increasing
case also stay above all earlier labels. These are set constraints
and the surreal cut property satisfies them. To defeat a proposed
set coinitial family above $f$, append a new value below all of its
relevant next values. In the increasing case impose the additional
lower bound $\ran f$. Every family member extending $f$ already
satisfies that bound, so a separator still exists.
\end{proof}

In particular, an increasing word of length $\omega$ is the supremum
of its finite prefixes. No surreal number is the supremum of a set
strictly below it, since the surreal set-cut property inserts a
smaller upper bound. This invariant distinguishes the word orders
from $\No$; mutual embeddings do not preserve suprema in the ambient
order.

The repository proves a sharper dictionary cut criterion for arbitrary
and injective words (\cref{swo:sw:cutclassification}): a set cut $A<B$ fails to have
an interpolant exactly when $B$ contains the empty word, or when
$B$ has a minimum of nonzero limit length and $A$ is cofinal below
that minimum. A common-prefix search proves this: unequal next-value
sets can be separated, while a forced common entry is appended; the
search is bounded by the input word lengths. Its only terminal failure
is a forced upper minimum reached at a limit. Restricting to nonzero
successor-length words removes both failures and gives a copy of
$\No$ in GBC. The prefix-tree isomorphism proved below transfers this
criterion to increasing dictionary words as well.

The following sections give the genuinely different outcomes obtained
by placing termination near zero. They treat zero exclusion, zero
padding, and a distinct terminator separately; these choices cannot
be conflated.

% ---- source 14 (batch-81 manuscript 02), lines 1417-1464
\subsection{Source 14: an explicit word embedding with a termination convention}
\label{swo:tc:sec:embeddings}
\merge\ The first subsection of source~14's Section~11; it gives a second
explicit code for the prefix-first word order (a second route to
\cref{swo:sw:code}) and a code for the prefix-last order. The rest of that
section (binary class words, the no-coding theorem and the Kanovei--Shelah
remark) is not printed in this Part; its relation-code subsection is in
\cref{swo:vii:sub:relcodes}.
The ordinary class of all set-length surreal words is much smaller in
logical sort than the virtual collection $\Hsl$. An explicit coding makes
its embeddability into $\No$ transparent and illustrates why the terminal
symbol matters. The underlying compression idea is inherited from the
predecessor (\cref{swo:sw:encoding}; Section~9 of this report at the pin); we spell out a version that
also permits repeated letters.

For a surreal $x$, let $c(x)$ be its binary sign code: replace each
minus by $00$, each plus by $11$, and append $01$. The three blocks
encode $-<\End<+$. Different complete codes are prefix-incomparable,
and their first-difference order is the numerical order of their labels.
For a word $p:\lambda\to\No$, concatenate the codes $c(p(\alpha))$
in ordinal order. Now replace each binary $0$ by $01$ and each binary
$1$ by $11$, and append $00$ to terminate the entire word. Finally read
$0,1$ as surreal signs $-,+$.

\begin{proposition}[Source~14, Proposition~11.1, ``Prefix-first word coding'']\label{swo:tc:n11.1}
This construction is an order embedding of the class of all set-length
surreal words, with prefix-first lexicography, into $\No$. Its output
birthday is
\[
 \sum_{\alpha<\lambda}\bigl(4\cdot\bd(p(\alpha))+4\bigr)+2,
\]
where every sum and multiplication is ordinal arithmetic.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
All lengths and concatenations are sets. At the first differing label,
the prefix-free numerical codes determine the correct binary comparison;
the outer replacement preserves it. If one whole word is a proper
prefix of the other, its outer terminal block $00$ is compared with
one of the data blocks $01,11$, making the shorter word smaller.
Complete outer codes are prefix-incomparable, so their comparison as
surreal sign sequences never reaches an ambiguity about termination.
The length of a label code is $2\cdot\bd(x)+2$ and outer replacement
doubles that length on the left. Summing those lengths and adding the
last two signs gives the displayed formula.
\end{proof}

For prefix-last order, use outer data blocks $00,10$ and terminal
block $11$ instead. The same proof applies. The embeddings do not claim
that these word orders are isomorphic to the entire surreal line: for
example the empty word is the least point in the prefix-first order.
Nor do they turn an $\Ord$-length class word into a set-length surreal.
The birthday formula is explicitly a \emph{set} ordinal expression.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 2042-2149
\subsection{Source 18's route: shorter-first increasing words are not a new order type in GBC}\label{swo:vii:sub:sh-tree}

\merge\ The first part of source~18's Section~10: a second proof of
\cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism}, written without knowledge of source~16.
Its proof makes explicit that the family of local isomorphisms is one class
given by one elementary formula from a set-like global well-order, and its
statement adds that zero may be excluded from any of the alphabets. The
corollary of that section, on cones of centered increasing words, is printed
with them (\cref{swo:sh:wv:cone-types}).
\label{swo:sh:wv:prefix-isomorphism}

The preceding centered results should not be transferred to the
shorter-first convention.  For shorter-first words a stronger
identification is possible, despite the constraints on coordinates.

\begin{theorem}[A prefix-preserving tree isomorphism]
\label{swo:sh:wv:tree-isomorphism}
In \(\mathrm{GBC}\), the classes of arbitrary, injective, and
strictly increasing set-length surreal words, all with shorter
prefixes first, are pairwise order-isomorphic.  Isomorphisms can
be chosen to preserve every word length and the prefix relation.
The conclusion also holds if zero is excluded from any of the
alphabets.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a permitted target prefix \(p\), its admissible next-letter
class is respectively
\[
 \No,\qquad
 \No\setminus\operatorname{ran}(p),\qquad
 D_p=\{x\in\No:\operatorname{ran}(p)<x\}.
\]
If zero is excluded, delete it as well.  Each class is proper and
fills every set cut.  For the increasing case, simply add
\(\operatorname{ran}(p)\) to the lower side of the proposed cut;
every upper-side letter already exceeds that range.  For the
injective case use set avoidance.

Fix a set-like global well-order \(\prec\).  There is a single
uniformly defined family of order isomorphisms
\[
                    \theta_p:\No\longrightarrow D_p,
\]
where \(D_p\) now denotes the relevant admissible class in any of
the cases.  Here is a construction that makes the uniformity and
the choice strength explicit.  Restrict \(\prec\) to \(\No\)
and to \(D_p\); these give ordinal enumerations, definably in
the set parameter \(p\).  Run the deterministic back-and-forth
that, at each stage, treats the least unmatched source point and
then the least unmatched target point, always choosing the
\(\prec\)-least unused filler of the required cut.  Every partial
map and every cut at an ordinal stage is a set.  For each set
ordinal, ordinary set recursion defines the unique initial
construction, with \(p\) and \(\prec\) as parameters.  The
assertion that a pair occurs in some such initial construction
is one elementary formula with these parameters.  Elementary
class comprehension therefore gives all the \(\theta_p\)'s as
one class relation.

In particular, we have not selected an arbitrary class
isomorphism independently for each of a proper class of prefixes.
No extra class-choice principle, and no recursion with
proper-class-valued stages, has been used.

For an arbitrary source word \(f:\alpha\to\No\), construct a
target word by set recursion.  Given its prefix \(p\) at position
\(\xi\), put the next target entry equal to
\(\theta_p(f(\xi))\); take unions at limits.  The output is a
permitted word of the same length.  Conversely, from any permitted
target word \(g\) recover
\[
              f(\xi)=\theta_{g\restriction\xi}^{-1}(g(\xi)).
\]
These procedures are inverse.  They preserve prefixes, and the
first unequal entries are transformed by the same increasing
map \(\theta_p\).  Thus they preserve the shorter-first
lexicographic order.

% ---- hand chunk termination
% ---- source 16 (batch-81 manuscript 04), lines 831-1063
\section{Termination conventions: the criterion and all isomorphism types (source 16; routes of sources 13 and 18)}\label{swo:ds:sec:termination}

\merge\ Source~16's Section~8, printed whole, as the base of this section:
it decides, for every placement of a formal terminator in the alphabet
$\No$, whether the unrestricted and the injective word orders fill every set
cut (\cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator}), and lists every isomorphism type that
occurs (\cref{swo:ds:thm:alltermination}). Its termination types
$\mathcal T_{\min}$, $T_\kappa$ are printed $\mathbf T_{\min}$,
$\mathbf T_\kappa$; its references to its own universality proposition and
set-cut display are printed as references to \cref{swo:prop:universal} and
\cref{swo:n3.1}. Source~13 (\cref{swo:fs:sec:central}) and source~18
(\cref{swo:vii:sub:sh-centered}) prove the central case independently, with the
same letter code (\cref{swo:vii:note:codes}).

Choose an initial/final partition $\No=L_\varepsilon+R_\varepsilon$ and
adjoin a formal symbol $\varepsilon$, distinct from every surreal, with
\[
 L_\varepsilon<\varepsilon<R_\varepsilon.
\]
Compare words at the first differing coordinate, treating a terminated
word as having the symbol $\varepsilon$ there. This includes prefix-first
and prefix-last as the two extreme placements. The Conway convention for
sign expansions, $-<\varepsilon<+$, uses a two-letter alphabet; it must
not be confused with prefix-first dictionary order over all surreals.

\begin{theorem}[Termination criterion]\label{swo:ds:thm:terminator}
Let $\mathcal V$ be either $\WordsAll$ or $\Words$. Its termination order
has a strict interpolant for every separated pair of sets, including
empty sides, if and only if:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $L_\varepsilon$ and $R_\varepsilon$ are nonempty;
\item $L_\varepsilon$ has a greatest member or has no set cofinal subset;
\item $R_\varepsilon$ has a least member or has no set coinitial subset.
\end{enumerate}
The equivalence uses GB and the surreal set-cut property. In GBC these
conditions are also equivalent to this word order being isomorphic to
$\No$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If either side of the threshold is empty, the empty word is an endpoint.
If $L_\varepsilon$ has no maximum but a set cofinal subset $C$, the
singleton words $(c)$, $c\in C$, lie below the empty word and have no
strict interpolant below it. Any proposed interpolant has a first letter
$x\in L_\varepsilon$, and some $c\in C$ is larger than $x$, placing
$(c)$ above it. The right-side argument is dual. This proves necessity.

For sufficiency, start with a separated set cut $A<B$ of words. Follow a
common prefix $p$, keeping active precisely the input words extending
it, including equality. Every discarded lower word is below every
extension of $p$, and every discarded upper word is above every such
extension. At the next coordinate let $U,V$ be the sets of active lower
and upper symbols, using $\varepsilon$ for a word ending at $p$.
Every $u\in U$ is at most every $v\in V$.

If $U$ and $V$ meet, their common member is unique. It cannot be
$\varepsilon$, which would put the same word $p$ on both sides. Append
the common surreal letter and continue. In the injective case a witness
among the active words ensures freshness. Take unions at limits. The
search cannot reach an ordinal larger than every input word length, so
eventually $U<V$ are disjoint.

If neither set contains $\varepsilon$, choose a fresh surreal strictly
between them and append it. Suppose $\varepsilon\in U$, so
$V\subseteq R_\varepsilon$. If a numerical member of $U$ already lies
in $R_\varepsilon$, ordinary fresh separation works. Otherwise all
numerical lower symbols lie in $L_\varepsilon$.
If $R_\varepsilon$ has no set coinitial subset, choose
$r\in R_\varepsilon$ below the whole set $V$, and then a fresh letter
strictly between $r$ and $V$. If $R_\varepsilon$ has a least member
$r$ not in $V$, the same interval above $r$ works.

The remaining case is that $r=\min R_\varepsilon$ belongs to $V$.
Append $r$, which is fresh in the injective case because an active upper
word supplies it. All lower comparisons are now decided. For the upper
words continuing through $r$, collect their next numerical letters and
choose a fresh $d$ below this set and below some fixed member of
$L_\varepsilon$. The extension $p\cat(r,d)$ is below all those upper
words, including any terminating just after $r$, and above every lower
word. Thus two letters handle the possible adjacency between the
termination symbol and a numerical letter. The case
$\varepsilon\in V$ is the reversed construction using
$\max L_\varepsilon$ when needed.

This proves interpolation using only a bounded set recursion. The word
carrier is proper, since it contains all singleton surreals. In GBC,
class universality (\cref{swo:prop:universal}) gives the claimed isomorphism with $\No$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Concrete consequences and explicit encodings}

Write $0^-$ for the formal symbol immediately below numerical zero, and
$0^+$ for the one immediately above zero. All labels, including zero,
remain available. For $0^-$ the sides are $x<0$ and $x\ge0$; for $0^+$
they are $x\le0$ and $x>0$. \Cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator} gives
\[
 \WordsAll_{0^-}\cong \Words_{0^-}\cong\No,
 \qquad
 \WordsAll_{0^+}\cong \Words_{0^+}\cong\No
 \quad\text{in GBC}.
\tag{S16.6}\label{swo:ds:eq:centered}
\]
This includes the empty word and all limit-length words.

There are also explicit embeddings that do not use global choice. For a
sign sequence $s_x$, let $D(s_x)$ duplicate each sign, and put
\[
 C_-(x)=D(s_x)\cat(+,-),\qquad
 C_+(x)=D(s_x)\cat(-,+).
\]
Both letter codes are prefix-free and preserve numerical comparison,
because either terminal block lies strictly between $(-,-)$ and $(+,+)$.
The first sign of $C_-(x)$ is negative exactly when $x<0$, and that of
$C_+(x)$ exactly when $x\le0$. Concatenating the codes of the entries
therefore embeds the corresponding termination order into $\No$.
The encoded birthday is
\[
 \sum_{i<\alpha}\bigl(2\cdot\bd(f(i))+2\bigr).
\tag{S16.7}\label{swo:ds:eq:centeredbirthday}
\]
The operations are ordinal operations; the $2$ is on the left.
At regular infinite birthday cutoffs, words of length below the cutoff
whose entries are below it remain below it under this encoding.
Singular cutoffs do not satisfy that closure assertion.

For prefix-first words, add an initial $+$ to each letter code. This
makes every continued block begin above termination and again gives an
explicit embedding into $\No$. Singleton words embed $\No$ back. Thus
equimorphism persists even where local characters prevent isomorphism.

An interior threshold alone does not guarantee saturation. Place it
between the surreals bounded above by some natural number and those
larger than every natural number. The singleton natural-number words are
cofinal below the empty word, so the termination order fails the criterion.
Furthermore, \eqref{swo:ds:eq:centered} does not extend to increasing words:
a positive increasing word of length $\omega$ still has its finite
prefixes cofinal below it. Its permissible continuation letters are all
positive. Hence the centered order on $\WordsInc$ is not $\eta_{\Ord}$.
The isomorphism of \Cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism} specifically uses
an endpoint termination convention.

\subsection{Complete isomorphism classification of the termination family}

There is a stronger classification for unrestricted and injective words.
For an infinite regular cardinal $\kappa$, place the formal terminator
at the threshold
\[
 L_\kappa=\{x:\exists\alpha<\kappa\ (x<\alpha)\},\qquad
 R_\kappa=\{x:\forall\alpha<\kappa\ (\alpha<x)\},
\]
and call the resulting unrestricted word order $\mathbf T_\kappa$. The two
classes partition $\No$: the regular infinite cardinal $\kappa$ is a
limit ordinal. Its left side has cofinality $\kappa$, while its right
side has no set coinitial family, since the set of ordinals below
$\kappa$ can be separated from any proposed such family by
the set-cut property (\cref{swo:n3.1}).

\begin{theorem}[All termination types]\label{swo:ds:thm:alltermination}
In GBC, the isomorphism types obtained from every formal termination
cut, for either unrestricted or injective set words, are exactly
\[
 \mathbf T_{\min},\quad\mathbf T_{\min}^{\mathrm{op}},\quad\No,\quad
 \mathbf T_\kappa,\quad \mathbf T_\kappa^{\mathrm{op}}
 \quad(\kappa\text{ infinite regular}).
\tag{S16.8}\label{swo:ds:eq:alltermination}
\]
There are no repetitions in this list. Here $\mathbf T_{\min}$ uses
shorter-prefix-first comparison. At every point of $\mathbf T_\kappa$ the
left cofinality is $\kappa$ and there is no set coinitial family on the
right; its dual has the reversed profile.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The endpoint placements give the first two types, by
\Cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism} and numerical negation. For an
interior placement, \Cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator} gives $\No$ unless
one threshold side has no endpoint and a set cofinal or coinitial
family. Both sides cannot fail this way: the two sets would contradict
the set-cut property (\cref{swo:n3.1}). The one small character is an infinite regular
cardinal, say $\kappa$.

For a maximum-free left threshold of cofinality $\kappa$, extensions
$p\cat(c)$ with $c$ in a cofinal subset of the left threshold are
cofinal below any word $p$. In the injective case one may move these
letters through intervening nonempty intervals to avoid $\ran(p)$.
Conversely, the next letters of a set cofinal below $p$, among its
extending members, must be cofinal in that threshold. This proves the
left character $\kappa$. The right threshold has no set coinitial
family, and the same next-letter argument excludes one above $p$.
If a threshold side has an endpoint instead, two-letter extensions as
in \Cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator} exclude small local character on that
side. These observations also distinguish the profiles in the list.

Two maximum-free threshold cuts with left cofinality $\kappa$ are
conjugate by a numerical automorphism of $\No$. Choose increasing
cofinal sequences of length $\kappa$ on their left sides, match them,
and extend this set partial isomorphism by back-and-forth. Each left
side is determined as the initial closure of its sequence, so the
automorphism carries the whole threshold cut to the other one.
Applying it entrywise identifies the unrestricted word orders, and
likewise the injective ones. The right-sided case is dual.

To identify unrestricted and injective words in this remaining case,
fix the cofinal left sequence. For every set $S$, choose uniformly a
cofinal sequence of the same type in $L_\varepsilon\setminus S$,
using fresh points in intervening surreal intervals. Start
back-and-forth with the matching of these two set sequences. It gives
a uniform isomorphism $h_S:\No\to\No\setminus S$ preserving both
threshold sides. Taking $h_\varnothing$ as the identity, the recursion
\[
 q(i)=h_{\ran(q\res i)}(f(i))
\]
is a length- and prefix-preserving isomorphism from free words to
injective words, now preserving comparison with the middle terminator
as well. All preparatory sequences and stage histories are sets.
The saturated cases were already identified with $\No$.

Finally endpoints distinguish the first two types. The surreal type
has no small character on either side of any point, whereas the
$\mathbf T_\kappa$ types have the displayed one-sided character. Those
characters distinguish the different regular cardinals and their
duals. This proves exhaustiveness and absence of repetitions.
\end{proof}

Every termination order in this list is nevertheless equimorphic with
$\No$, even without global choice. For an arbitrary threshold, the
increasing map $\phi(x)=x/(1+|x|)$ can be rescaled separately on its
two sides into $(-1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. This preserves all letter
comparisons and puts termination at zero. The duplicated-sign codes
above then give an explicit word embedding into $\No$; singleton
words supply an embedding in the opposite direction. Thus equimorphism
leaves a proper class of different isomorphism types undistinguished.
This classification excludes numerically increasing words with an
interior terminator, whose behavior depends on the prefix as explained
above.

\subsection{Length-first ordering is another independent choice}

In shortlex order one compares the ordinal lengths first, and compares
entries only when those lengths agree. It is the ordered sum of the
fixed-length layers over $\Ord$, rather than one of the termination
orders above. Its empty word is a minimum, and two words can be ordered
by their lengths even when they disagree numerically at their first
entry. This variant is rigorously definable on set codes but should be
named explicitly; none of the global-exhaustive results implicitly uses it.

\begin{note}[\merge; one letter code, five spellings]\label{swo:vii:note:codes}
With $D(s)$ the sign sequence $s$ with every sign doubled, the letter codes
of the six sources and of Part~II are the following; each is prefix-free and
order-preserving because its terminal block lies strictly between $(-,-)$ and
$(+,+)$.
\begin{itemize}
\item $D(s_x)\cat(+,-)$: source~16's $C_-$ (S16.7), source~13's $c(x)$
(\cref{swo:fs:prop:centralcode}) and source~18's centered $C(x)$
(\cref{swo:sh:wv:centered-code}); birthday $2\cdot\bd(x)+2$ per letter, as in
(S16.7). Source~16's $C_+$ is $D(s_x)\cat(-,+)$.
\item $(+)\cat D(s_x)\cat(+,-)$: Part~II's prefix-first code (\cref{swo:sw:code}),
source~16's ``add an initial $+$'' and source~18's $K(x)$
(\cref{swo:sh:prop:dictionary-code}); birthday $1+2\cdot\bd(x)+2$ per letter.
Source~17's code (S17.1) is $(+)\cat D(s_x)\cat(-,+)$, with the same birthday.
\item $00,11,01$ for $-,+$ and termination, read as signs: source~14's inner
code (\cref{swo:tc:n11.1} and its Subsection~2.3) and source~15's entry code
(\cref{swo:cb:lem:entrycode}); this is source~16's $C_+$. Source~14 doubles it
once more for the outer word, which gives $4\cdot\bd(x)+4$ per letter and $2$
for the end.
\end{itemize}
Source~16 does not cite Part~II for its prefix-first code. No source claims
that any of these codes is optimal; source~11's question on optimal birthday
bounds (\cref{swo:n16.6}) stays open (\cref{swo:vii:sec:status}).
\end{note}

% ---- source 13 (batch-81 manuscript 01), lines 1389-1540
\subsection{Source 13's route: central termination restores a surreal word order}\label{swo:fs:sec:central}

\merge\ Source~13's Section~12, printed whole as a route to the central case
of \cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator}. Its order $\WordsAll_0$ is the termination
order of the alphabet $\No\setminus\{0\}$ with the terminator in the place of
the reserved zero, i.e.\ with threshold sides the negative and the positive
surreals; neither side has an endpoint or a set cofinal (resp.\ coinitial)
subset, so \cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator}, transported along an isomorphism
$\No\setminus\{0\}\cong\No$ in $\GBC$, gives \cref{swo:fs:thm:central}. The
proofs are the same common-prefix search. Its code (\cref{swo:fs:prop:centralcode})
is source~16's code $C_-$ (S16.7), with the same birthday. Source~13's own
caveat stands: it does not classify all placements of a marker; source~16
does (\cref{swo:ds:thm:alltermination}). Its references to its fresh-separator
lemma, class universality and core interpolation are printed as references to
\cref{swo:lem:delete,swo:prop:universal,swo:gl:interpolation}.

The end-first convention of \Cref{swo:fs:sec:words} treats every
extension as larger than its prefix, irrespective of the sign of the
new label. We now keep a genuine middle termination value. To avoid
collapsing distinct words, that value must not also be an ordinary
letter.

\subsubsection{The precise alternative}

Let $A=\No\setminus\{0\}$. A word $p:\alpha\to A$ is padded by the
surreal number $0$ at every coordinate at or beyond $\alpha$. Compare
two different words at the first difference of these padded functions,
using numerical comparison in $\No$. Call this \emph{central
termination}, and denote the arbitrary-word and injective-word orders
by $\WordsAll_0$ and $\I_0$, respectively.

For example,
\[
       (-1)<()<(1),\qquad
       p\cat(-1)<p<p\cat(1),
\]
whenever the displayed appended letters are allowed. The empty word
is now an interior point, not an endpoint. For injective words, fresh
negative and positive letters can always be used in place of $-1,1$.

Distinct words really are distinguished by padding: at the end of a
proper prefix, the longer word has a nonzero letter. If $0$ were also
allowed as a letter, $p$ and $p\cat(0)$ would have identical padded
functions. The resulting rule would fail even to define an
antisymmetric order on raw words. Reserving the marker is essential.

\begin{theorem}[Central-word isomorphism]\label{swo:fs:thm:central}
In $\GBC$,
\[
                          \WordsAll_0\cong\No,
                  \qquad \I_0\cong\No.
\]
Both isomorphisms concern ordinary classes of set codes. They do not
identify the word structure, concatenation, or birthdays with surreal
arithmetic.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
It suffices to prove set-cut saturation. Let a set of lower words be
strictly below a set of upper words. Choose an ordinal $\delta$
strictly above all their lengths. We use an active-prefix argument
similar to the interpolation theorem of the core (source~13's Theorem~7.2, \cref{swo:gl:interpolation}), but now an active word
that ends exactly at the current prefix contributes the value $0$.

At a current nonzero-letter prefix $p$ of length $\beta$, an active
bound agrees with $p$ and has length at least $\beta$. Let $L_\beta$
and $U_\beta$ be its next letters, or $0$ for a bound ending there.
Every cross lower value is at most every upper value. If there is a
shared value $t$, it is unique. It cannot be $0$: a lower word and an
upper word both ending at $p$ would be identical, contradicting the
strict cut. Append $t$ and retain the active rows with next value $t$.
In the injective case $t$ is fresh, because it belongs to an active
injective word extending $p$.

If no value is shared, choose a strict separator $x$ between the two
sets, avoiding $0$, and also avoiding $\ran(p)$ in the injective
case. The fresh-separator lemma (\cref{swo:lem:delete}) supplies it. The \emph{terminated}
word $p\cat x$ now lies strictly between every original bound. Every
comparison is already decided at this new coordinate or an earlier
one, so its termination afterwards is immaterial.

At limits, take the union of the earlier prefixes. The procedure
cannot continue indefinitely: after all input lengths have been
passed, no bound can remain active. More specifically, a shared
nonzero letter extends some active word and so cannot occur at a
stage $\beta\geq\delta$. The construction stops at a set stage.
It also works with an empty side or with both sides empty. This proves
set-cut saturation of each order. Their elements are sets; apply
class universality (\cref{swo:prop:universal}) to obtain the two isomorphisms.
\end{proof}

This answers the termination-convention direction for an important
interior convention. It is not a classification of all possible
placements of a marker in every class alphabet. By translating labels,
one may reserve any specified surreal $c$ instead of $0$. Since
$\No\setminus\{0\}\cong\No$, the construction can also be transported
to an abstract alphabet order-isomorphic to the full surreal line.
Such a transport is not the identity on numerical labels and should
not be hidden in the notation.

\subsubsection{An explicit sign-code embedding}

The isomorphism proof uses class back-and-forth. There is also a direct
order embedding, useful for calculations and finite tests.

Given a surreal letter $x$ with sign sequence $s_x$ of length $\alpha$,
replace each $-$ by $--$ and each $+$ by $++$, then append the pair
$+-$. Write the resulting sign block as
\[
             c(x)=\operatorname{double}(s_x)\cat(+,-).
\]
The family of these blocks is prefix-free. If two original sign
sequences first differ by actual signs, their doubled versions decide
the comparison in the same direction. If one sequence ends first,
its terminal pair $+-$ lies lexicographically between the two possible
continuation pairs $--$ and $++$. Thus the same statement holds when
the original comparison used termination.

\begin{proposition}[Direct central-word coding]\label{swo:fs:prop:centralcode}
For a set word $p:\lambda\to\No\setminus\{0\}$, concatenate the
blocks $c(p(i))$ in order and regard the result as a surreal sign
sequence. This defines an order embedding $C:\WordsAll_0\emb\No$, and
hence also an embedding of $\I_0$, with
\[
          \bd(C(p))=
          \sum_{i<\lambda}\bigl(2\cdot\bd(p(i))+2\bigr).
\]
All sums and products in this formula are ordinal operations.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every block has positive set-ordinal length and the concatenation has
set-ordinal length, so it is a surreal. At a first unequal letter,
prefix-freeness and order preservation of the block code decide the
same comparison before either block is exhausted.

If one outer word is a proper prefix of the other, its code terminates
at the beginning of the next block. Because the next letter is
nonzero, the first sign of its block has the sign of that letter.
Comparison of termination with this sign has exactly the central
termination direction. Thus all comparisons are strict and preserved.
The birthday formula is the definition of ordinal concatenation.
\end{proof}

No claim of surjectivity is made for this explicit map. The order
isomorphism in \Cref{swo:fs:thm:central} is a separate conclusion.
The code is a central-termination adaptation of the prefix-free
sign-block methods used in the earlier set-word analysis
of Part~II (\cref{swo:sw:code}); the accompanying finite tests check it by
independent dyadic evaluation.

\subsubsection{Why the obstruction disappears}

At a limit-length central word $p$, the proper prefixes are not
uniformly below it: their comparison depends on the next nonzero
letter of $p$. More importantly, a set of lower approximants can be
separated from $p$ by a fresh next value at the decisive active stage.
The set-cut proof supplies this separator regardless of a particular
sign pattern. Hence the small cofinality invariant of
\Cref{swo:fs:prop:wordlimit} no longer obstructs an isomorphism
with $\No$.

The two constructions therefore illustrate a general warning:
lexicographic comparison of variable-length words is not a minor
technical variation of fixed-domain lexicography. A termination
convention can change endpoints, point characters, and the entire
isomorphism type.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1574-1688
\subsection{Source 18's route: centered termination changes the order}\label{swo:vii:sub:sh-centered}

\merge\ Source~18's Section~7, printed whole as a third proof of the central
case. Its classes $\mathcal C$, $\mathcal C_{\mathrm{inj}}$,
$\mathcal C_{\mathrm{inc}}$ are printed $\WordsAll_c$, $\Words_c$, $\WordsInc_c$
(\cref{swo:sub:notation81}). Its centered order is source~13's $\WordsAll_0$
(the terminator compared as zero with the next entry), and its code $C(x)$
is source~13's $c(x)$ and source~16's $C_-(x)$. Nothing in it is new against
sources~13 and~16 except the remark that singleton words also embed $\No$
into $\WordsInc_c$, which the next section uses. Source~18 was written
before sources~13--17 arrived.
\label{swo:sh:wv:centered}

For a set word \(p\), write \(\ell(p)\) for its ordinal length and
\(p\mathbin{\frown}\langle x\rangle\) for ordinal concatenation.
The surreal set-cut property used throughout this section is the following:
if \(L<R\) are sets of surreals, possibly empty, there is a surreal
strictly between them.  The interpolant can also be required to avoid
any prescribed set.  Indeed, first find a nonempty surreal interval
between the two sides, and then use the set-cut property inside that
interval to escape the forbidden set.  No global choice is needed
for these interpolation statements.

Put \(D=\No\setminus\{0\}\).  Let \(\WordsAll_c\) be all set-length
words over \(D\), and let \(\Words_c\) and
\(\WordsInc_c\) be its injective and strictly numerically
increasing subclasses.  Include the empty word in all three classes.
Compare unequal words at their first unequal entry.  If one word is a
proper prefix of the other, compare its termination symbol \(\dagger\)
with the next entry of the longer word, using
\[
             x<\dagger\quad(x<0),\qquad
             \dagger<x\quad(x>0).
\]
Thus \(\dagger\) occupies the missing numerical position of zero.
This is a strict linear order, called the centered order below.
The definition is not the shorter-first convention.

\begin{theorem}[Centered arbitrary and injective words]
\label{swo:sh:wv:centered-eta}
Both \(\WordsAll_c\) and \(\Words_c\) fill every
set cut.  Consequently, in \(\mathrm{GBC}\), both are
order-isomorphic to \(\No\).  They have no endpoints; neither side
of any point has a set cofinal or a set coinitial subset, respectively.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a prefix \(p\), its cone consists of all words extending \(p\),
including \(p\) itself.  A cone is convex.  A word outside it is
uniformly below the whole cone or uniformly above the whole cone:
the comparison has already been decided before \(p\) ends.

Given a set cut \(A<B\), start with the empty prefix.  Maintain the
invariant that discarded members of \(A\) are below its whole cone,
and discarded members of \(B\) are above its whole cone.  At a current
prefix \(p\) of length \(\delta\), assign next tokens to the active
words: an actual next entry to a proper extension, and the numerical
token \(0\) to \(p\) itself.  Let \(L_p,R_p\) be the two sets of
tokens.  Every member of \(L_p\) is at most every member of \(R_p\).
Their intersection therefore has at most one member.

If the token sets are disjoint, choose
\[
          L_p<c<R_p,\qquad
          c\notin\{0\}\cup\operatorname{ran}(p).
\]
Then \(p\mathbin{\frown}\langle c\rangle\) interpolates the original
cut.  Avoiding the range is needed only for the injective version.
If the token sets meet, their common token cannot be zero: zero would
mean that the same word \(p\) belonged to both sides.  Append their
common nonzero entry and continue.  The new prefix is injective when
required, since it is an actual prefix of a witnessing injective word.
At a limit stage take the union.  The cone invariant persists.

Choose an ordinal strictly above every length occurring in
\(A\cup B\).  The search cannot keep finding common actual entries
that long.  It therefore terminates with a successor-length
interpolant.  Empty sides cause no difficulty.  Taking one side to be
a singleton gives the asserted local character properties.

In \(\mathrm{GBC}\), restrict a fixed set-like global well-order to
each of the two proper classes and to \(\No\).  An ordinal
back-and-forth constructs an isomorphism: at each stage its partial
map is a set, and the new point is chosen in the cut imposed by that
set.  Freshness follows from the set-avoidance version of
interpolation.  This is a recursion with set-sized partial maps,
not an appeal to choice for arbitrary families of proper classes.
\end{proof}

The contrast with shorter-first words is substantive.  At a limit
word, the centered convention still permits extensions on both sides.
These extensions prevent the proper prefixes from creating a
one-sided set-cofinal obstruction.

\begin{proposition}[An explicit centered sign embedding]
\label{swo:sh:wv:centered-code}
There is an explicit order embedding of \(\WordsAll_c\) into \(\No\),
without global choice.  Conversely, \(\No\) embeds into each of
\(\WordsAll_c\), \(\Words_c\), and
\(\WordsInc_c\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a nonzero surreal \(x\), let \(s_x\) be its nonempty sign expansion.
Duplicate each sign, writing \(D(s_x)\), and put
\[
       C(x)=D(s_x)\mathbin{\frown}(+,-).
\]
This is a prefix-free order-preserving code.  When one original sign
expansion ends, the delimiter \((+,-)\) is compared with the next
doubled block; the inequalities
\[
                  (-,-)<(+,-)<(+,+)
\]
give exactly the surreal comparison at termination.  The first sign
of \(C(x)\) is the sign of \(x\).
For a word \(f\), concatenate the blocks \(C(f(i))\) in ordinal
domain order.  Replacement makes the resulting sign expansion a set.
First disagreements are preserved by prefix freedom; proper-prefix
comparisons are preserved by the first sign of the next block.
This proves the first assertion.
