For the reverse embedding send \(0\) to the empty word and send
\(x\ne0\) to \(\langle x\rangle\).
\end{proof}

% ---- hand chunk centered increasing
\section{Centered increasing words and the entry zero (source 18)}\label{swo:vii:sec:centered}

\merge\ Source~18's Sections~8, 9 and~11 and the corollary of its Section~10,
printed whole; no other source treats these orders. Source~16 observed the
first obstruction (end of its Subsection~8.1: a positive increasing word of
length $\omega$ is the supremum of its finite prefixes, so the centered order
on $\WordsInc$ is not $\eta_{\Ord}$) and asked for the classification of
increasing words with an interior terminator (its question ``Centered
increasing words''); source~18 gives it for the threshold at zero
(\cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts}), and settles the three ways of treating an
actual entry zero (\cref{swo:sh:wv:zero-quotient,swo:sh:wv:adjacent-terminator-all,swo:sh:wv:zero-adjacency}).
Its proofs use the common-prefix search of \cref{swo:sw:cutclassification}
with the termination token evaluated as zero.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1689-1838
\subsection{Centered increasing words: an exact cut classification}
\label{swo:sh:wv:increasing}

Strict numerical increase changes which continuations remain
available.  An all-negative prefix admits fresh negative and positive
continuations.  Once a positive entry has occurred, every valid
continuation is positive.  This distinction gives the entire
obstruction below.

\begin{theorem}[Local characters of centered increasing words]
\label{swo:sh:wv:inc-characters}
Let \(f\in\WordsInc_c\) have length \(\alpha\).
If \(\alpha\) is a nonzero limit and \(f\) has a positive entry, then
\[
 \operatorname{cf}\bigl((\WordsInc_c)_{<f}\bigr)
                  =\operatorname{cf}(\alpha).
\]
At every other point the open initial segment has no set cofinal
subset.  At every point the open final segment has no set coinitial
subset.  The order has no endpoints and is dense.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(f\) has a positive entry and has limit length, its sufficiently
long proper prefixes form an increasing cofinal family below it.
Every smaller word either terminates before \(f\), or first differs
at a smaller entry before \(\alpha\); it cannot properly extend
\(f\), since all such extensions are larger.  It is consequently
below every sufficiently long prefix.  A cofinal sequence of prefix
lengths proves the upper bound \(\operatorname{cf}(\alpha)\).
Conversely, for each smaller word choose a prefix exceeding it.
Fewer than \(\operatorname{cf}(\alpha)\) chosen lengths are bounded,
so that many words cannot be cofinal.

Suppose next that every entry of \(f\) is negative; this includes
the empty word.  Given a set \(A<f\), collect the next entries of
those members of \(A\) that properly extend \(f\).  They are all
negative.  Choose a negative \(d\) above this set and above
\(\operatorname{ran}(f)\).  The set-cut property permits this even
when the range of \(f\) has no largest member.  Then
\[
              A<f\mathbin{\frown}\langle d\rangle<f.
\]
Members of \(A\) that do not extend \(f\) first differ earlier
with a smaller entry.  They are also below this new word.

If \(f=p\mathbin{\frown}\langle x\rangle\) and \(x>0\), a smaller
word sharing \(p\) either stops there or has next entry below \(x\).
For a set of such words, choose \(d<x\) above all their next entries,
above zero, and above \(\operatorname{ran}(p)\).
The word \(p\mathbin{\frown}\langle d\rangle\) is a strictly larger
lower bound for \(f\) than any member of the given set.
This handles the remaining successor case.

Finally, given a set \(B>f\), the next entries of members that extend
\(f\) are positive.  Choose a positive \(d\), above the range of
\(f\), but below all those entries.  Then
\[
                    f<f\mathbin{\frown}\langle d\rangle<B.
\]
All other members of \(B\) are above the whole cone of \(f\).
This proves the right-hand assertion and, with empty sets, the
absence of endpoints.

For density, if two words first differ, choose a new coordinate
strictly between their different entries.  It is automatically
above the common prefix.  If the comparison is a proper-prefix
comparison with next entry \(x>0\), choose a coordinate strictly
between the set \(\{0\}\cup\operatorname{ran}(p)\) and \(x\).
For a next entry \(x<0\), choose one strictly between
\(\{x\}\cup\operatorname{ran}(p)\) and zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All set-cut obstructions]
\label{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts}
A set cut \(A<B\) in \(\WordsInc_c\) has no
interpolant if and only if \(B\) has a minimum \(b\) such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\ell(b)\) is a nonzero limit ordinal;
\item \(b\) has a positive entry;
\item \(A\) is cofinal in the open initial segment below \(b\).
\end{enumerate}
In every other case an interpolant of nonzero successor length
can be chosen.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The stated obstruction plainly prevents interpolation.  For the
converse use the common-prefix search from
\Cref{swo:sh:wv:centered-eta}, evaluating the termination token as
zero.  Common actual coordinates can still be appended, since each
is witnessed by an increasing word.

At a stage where the next-token sets are disjoint, a permissible
new coordinate must satisfy
\[
       L_p\cup\operatorname{ran}(p)<c<R_p,\qquad c\ne0.
\]
Every actual token on the right exceeds the whole range of \(p\).
Thus these are consistent strict surreal constraints unless zero
is a right token and \(p\) already has a positive entry.
When zero is a right token and all entries of \(p\) are negative,
the constraints are still consistent and a negative interpolating
coordinate can be chosen.  Empty right sides are harmless.

The sole exceptional event is therefore reaching \(p\in B\)
after \(p\) has acquired a positive entry.  Every proper extension
of \(p\) is larger, so the active lower side is then empty and
\(p=\min B\).  This event cannot occur at a successor length:
the last common coordinate had a witness in \(A\) extending that
successor prefix, which would be at least \(p\), contradicting
\(A<B\).  It also cannot occur at length zero.

It can occur at a limit length \(\alpha\).  For every sufficiently
large \(\xi<\alpha\), a lower-side witness supplied the coordinate
\(p(\xi)\).  That witness extends
\(p\restriction(\xi+1)\), is at least this prefix, and remains below
\(p\).  The sufficiently long prefixes are cofinal below \(p\)
by \Cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-characters}; hence \(A\) is cofinal there.
As before, a bound on all input word lengths ensures that the search
terminates using only set recursion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{swo:sh:wv:inc-corollary}
The centered increasing-word order is equimorphic with \(\No\),
but is not isomorphic to \(\No\), even in \(\mathrm{GBC}\).
Its successor-length suborder fills every set cut and is isomorphic
to \(\No\) in \(\mathrm{GBC}\).  The larger suborder obtained by
adjoining the empty word and every all-negative limit word also
fills every set cut and has the same isomorphism conclusion.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the explicit embeddings of \Cref{swo:sh:wv:centered-code}.
The increasing word \(\langle1,2,3,\ldots\rangle\) is the supremum
of its finite prefixes in this word order.  Such a nontrivial set
supremum cannot occur in \(\No\), by its set-cut property.
For either stated suborder, an upper set cannot contain the
exceptional minimum in \Cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts}, and the proof
returns a successor word in the suborder itself.  Apply class
back-and-forth in \(\mathrm{GBC}\).
\end{proof}

Every nonempty open interval in the centered increasing-word order
contains an order copy of \(\No\).  The density proof can choose a
whole surreal interval of permissible new coordinates, rather than
one coordinate.  This supplies the copy.  Thus rich interval
universality coexists with the precise limit-word obstructions.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 1839-2041
\subsection{What if the entry zero is allowed?}
\label{swo:sh:wv:zero}

Saying only that termination lies ``between negatives and positives''
does not settle its relation to an actual entry equal to zero.
There are at least three different mathematical constructions.

\subsubsection{Zero padding and its quotient}

One may pad each set word by zeros at every later ordinal coordinate.
First-difference comparison then gives a total preorder, not a strict
linear order on the original words.  For instance the empty word
and \(\langle0\rangle\) become equal.  In general put
\[
 \sigma(f)=\sup\{\xi+1:\xi<\ell(f),\ f(\xi)\ne0\},\qquad
 \rho(f)=f\restriction\sigma(f).
\]
Two padded words are equivalent exactly when their reduced words
\(\rho(f)\) agree.  A class of canonical set representatives is
therefore available: trim the entire terminal zero tail.  A
limit-length reduced word has nonzero entries cofinal in its length.
Reduction preserves injectivity and strict numerical increase.

\begin{proposition}[The zero-padding quotient]
\label{swo:sh:wv:zero-quotient}
For all set words, and separately for injective set words, the
zero-padding quotient fills every set cut and is isomorphic to
\(\No\) in \(\mathrm{GBC}\).
For strictly increasing set words, the quotient has exactly the
cut obstruction in \Cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts}, applied to the reduced
representatives.  Its exceptional points are precisely the reduced
limit words having a positive entry.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Work with reduced representatives.  A set of input representatives
has lengths bounded by a set ordinal \(\mu\).  Pad them by zeros
through coordinate \(\mu\) and perform the numerical common-prefix
search.  This search now allows a common coordinate equal to zero.

In the injective case, the prefix produced by the search remains
injective.  A common nonzero coordinate is an actual coordinate of
every witness.  If zero is common, at least one witness has an actual
zero at that coordinate: otherwise a witness on each side would
have terminated already and, agreeing at all previous coordinates,
would represent the same padded word.  An actual witness certifies
that the enlarged prefix is injective.  At a separation step choose
a nonzero coordinate outside the range of the prefix, using the
surreal set-cut property.  This returns a reduced injective word.
At coordinate \(\mu\), two distinct input classes cannot both remain
active, so termination is guaranteed.  This proves the first claim.

For increasing words the same witness argument makes every common
prefix strictly increasing.  At a separating step add its range
to the lower numerical constraints.  The only potential failure
is an upper padded-zero token together with a nonnegative entry in
the current prefix.

If the prefix has a positive entry, an increasing extension cannot
consist of an extra padded zero.  The active upper word must therefore
be exactly this reduced prefix.  As in the proof of
\Cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts}, a successor stopping stage contradicts
the preceding lower-side witness, while a limit stopping stage gives
the stated cofinal-prefix obstruction.

The remaining apparent failure would be a prefix
\(p=q\mathbin{\frown}\langle0\rangle\), where \(q\) is all-negative,
and an active upper representative equal to \(q\).  This stage
cannot be reached.  The common zero that produced it would require
a lower witness.  If that witness has terminated, it represents
\(q\) itself; if it has an actual zero, its reduced increasing
continuation has a positive entry and lies above \(q\).  Both
possibilities contradict the cut.  There are no further failures.
The converse obstruction is again supplied by cofinal proper
prefixes of a positive limit word.
\end{proof}

For labeled well-orders the quotient is a change of subject that
must be stated explicitly.  It identifies distinct codes with
different underlying sets: an all-negative increasing word and
that word followed by zero are different labeled well-orders,
although the padding convention makes them equivalent.

\subsubsection{A distinct terminator immediately below or above zero}

Alternatively, retain every word as a distinct object and use one
of the two enlarged alphabets
\[
 \No_{<0}<\dagger_-<0<\No_{>0},\qquad
 \No_{<0}<0<\dagger_+<\No_{>0}.
\]
The indicated neighboring symbols are adjacent in the enlarged
alphabet.  These are genuine linear orders on the original words.

\begin{proposition}
\label{swo:sh:wv:adjacent-terminator-all}
For arbitrary set words, and separately for injective set words,
both distinct-terminator conventions fill every set cut.  Each
resulting class order is isomorphic to \(\No\) in \(\mathrm{GBC}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the common-prefix search with tokens in the enlarged alphabet.
At a strict token separation an unused surreal can normally be
inserted.  The only exception is its displayed adjacent pair.

For \(\dagger_-<0\), the exceptional separation has the current
prefix \(p\) on the lower side, and upper active words with next
entry zero.  No lower word has next entry zero, since that would
have caused a common-coordinate step.  Append zero and then append
a sufficiently small negative, unused coordinate.  The resulting
word is above the original lower side and below every upper word
in the zero cone, including its root if that root occurs.
For \(0<\dagger_+\), use the reverse construction: append zero and
then a sufficiently large positive, unused coordinate.
The existence of a zero cone certifies that zero has not already
been used when injectivity is required.  All choices avoid the
remaining set of used entries.  This resolves the only additional
cases, and the length bound still makes the search a set recursion.
\end{proof}

Increasing words behave differently.  The following general fact
isolates the reason, independently of any surreal coding.

\begin{lemma}[Adjacency test for increasing words]
\label{swo:sh:wv:adjacency-test}
Let \(D\) be a linearly ordered alphabet with a distinct termination
symbol \(\dagger\).  Suppose every allowed increasing set word has
a further admissible entry above \(\dagger\).  In the corresponding
order of all increasing set words, \(f<g\) are adjacent if and only if
\[
 g=f\mathbin{\frown}\langle c\rangle,
 \qquad
 c=\min\{x\in D:\operatorname{ran}(f)<x,\ \dagger<x\}.
\]
The minimum in this assertion must exist.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(f\) is not a prefix of \(g\), append a positive-side
admissible entry to \(f\).  The new word is above \(f\), and its
comparison with \(g\) was already settled before the appended
entry; hence it remains below \(g\).  Such a pair is not adjacent.

If \(f\) is a proper prefix of \(g\), its first new entry \(c\)
must be above \(\dagger\).  All subsequent entries exceed \(c\),
so if \(g\) has any further entries, the one-letter extension
\(f\mathbin{\frown}\langle c\rangle\) lies strictly between.
For this one-letter extension to be adjacent, no admissible entry
strictly between \(\dagger\) and \(c\) can exist.  Conversely,
if \(c\) is the stated minimum, every word in between would have
to extend \(f\); its first new entry could neither be below \(c\)
nor above it, and a proper continuation after \(c\) would be larger
than \(g\).  No such word exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exactly where the zero jump appears]
\label{swo:sh:wv:zero-adjacency}
For increasing surreal words with \(\dagger_-\), the adjacent
pairs are exactly
\[
          p\ <\ p\mathbin{\frown}\langle0\rangle
          \quad\text{with every entry of \(p\) negative}.
\]
The empty prefix is allowed.  With \(\dagger_+\), or with zero
excluded and centered termination, there are no adjacent pairs.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Every set of surreal entries has a strictly larger positive surreal,
so the lemma applies.  With \(\dagger_-\), an all-negative prefix
has zero as its least positive-side admissible entry.  Once a
nonnegative entry is present, the admissible upper class has no
least member, by the surreal set-cut property.  With
\(\dagger_+\), its positive-side admissible class consists of
positive surreals above the prefix and has no least member.
The same argument applies when zero is excluded.
\end{proof}

Thus a convention that differs only in the placement of actual
zero can create adjacent pairs even among finite increasing words.
The absence of an eligible letter between termination and the zero
entry is decisive: increasing words cannot use the negative
two-letter detour from \Cref{swo:sh:wv:adjacent-terminator-all}.

For completeness, the same common-prefix argument gives the full
set-cut classification for increasing words with the distinct
terminators.  With \(\dagger_+\), only the positive limit-word
obstruction of \Cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts} remains.  With
\(\dagger_-\), one must additionally allow the adjacent-pair
obstruction
\[
 \max A=p,\qquad \min B=p\mathbin{\frown}\langle0\rangle,
 \qquad \operatorname{ran}(p)<0.
\]
Indeed, at the exceptional token gap the word
\(p\mathbin{\frown}\langle0\rangle\) itself interpolates unless
it is the upper minimum and \(p\) is the lower maximum.  All
other separating-coordinate steps work as before.  A positive
limit stopping stage is unchanged.  For \(\dagger_+\), the
zero-then-positive detour is increasing and always resolves the
adjacent token gap.

\begin{note}[\merge; two of the zero conventions are source~16's]\label{swo:vii:note:adjacent}
For arbitrary and injective words, the conventions $\dagger_-$ and $\dagger_+$
of \cref{swo:sh:wv:adjacent-terminator-all} are source~16's $0^-$ and $0^+$,
and that proposition is (S16.6), a case of \cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator}; both
proofs resolve the adjacent pair by the same two-letter detour. New in
source~18 are the zero-padding quotient (\cref{swo:sh:wv:zero-quotient}) and,
for increasing words, the exact adjacent pairs
(\cref{swo:sh:wv:adjacency-test,swo:sh:wv:zero-adjacency}).
\end{note}

\subsection{Cones of centered increasing words (source 18, Corollary 10.2)}

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 2042-2149 (chunk lines 80-116)
\end{proof}

This theorem sharpens equimorphism: under the displayed choice
assumption the three shorter-first word orders are actually the
same order type.  It does not identify that type with \(\No\).
Their positive-length limit prefixes still have the familiar
small left cofinalities and nontrivial set suprema.

\begin{corollary}[Two kinds of centered increasing cones]
\label{swo:sh:wv:cone-types}
Work in \(\mathrm{GBC}\), with zero excluded and centered termination.
The cone above an all-negative increasing prefix \(p\), including
its root \(p\), is isomorphic to the entire centered increasing-word
order \(\WordsInc_c\).  If \(p\) has a positive entry,
its cone is isomorphic to the shorter-first arbitrary-word order,
including that order's minimum.  These isomorphisms can preserve
the rooted prefix tree and the lengths of tails after the root.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
At an all-negative prefix, the two admissible classes
\[
 \{x:\operatorname{ran}(p)<x<0\},\qquad \{x:0<x\}
\]
are both proper and fill every set cut relative to themselves.
Once a positive coordinate is chosen, the negative admissible
class disappears, and the positive admissible class continues to
fill all set cuts.  Use the uniform construction in the proof of
\Cref{swo:sh:wv:tree-isomorphism} separately on the negative and
positive parts whenever both are present.  The source and target
therefore pass from the all-negative case to the positive case
at the same coordinate, and both entry and termination comparisons
are preserved.  This proves the first assertion.  In the positive
case every later entry is above termination, so the rooted cone
uses precisely shorter-first comparison.  The one-part version
of the same construction proves the second assertion.
\end{proof}

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 2150-2179
\subsection{Regular set stages}
\label{swo:sh:wv:regular-stages}

The cut arguments also give a set-sized version.  Let \(\kappa\)
be regular infinite, restrict entries to \(\No_{<\kappa}\), and
restrict word lengths to be below \(\kappa\).  With zero excluded
and centered termination, the arbitrary and injective word orders
are \(\eta_\kappa\).  Their increasing-word successor suborders
are \(\eta_\kappa\) as well.  The proof of every relevant cut
statement uses fewer than \(\kappa\) input words, has its prefix
length bounded below \(\kappa\), and adds fewer than \(\kappa\)
coordinate constraints or forbidden entries.  Regularity and
the \(\eta_\kappa\) property of \(\No_{<\kappa}\) supply the
last coordinate.  If \(2^{<\kappa}=\kappa\), these orders have
cardinality \(\kappa\) and are isomorphic to \(\No_{<\kappa}\)
by ordinary set back-and-forth.

The complete increasing-word order retains every positive
limit-length obstruction that is present below the cutoff.
When \(\kappa=\omega\) no nonzero limit word length is allowed,
so this particular obstruction disappears.  These statements
should not be extrapolated to singular cutoffs or to unrestricted
word lengths over a fixed set alphabet: a prefix can then exhaust
the available one-sided room.  For example, over the nonzero
dyadic rationals, an increasing negative word
\(\langle-1,-1/2,-1/4,\ldots\rangle\) has no further negative
entry above its whole range, whereas every set of negative
surreals does have such a continuation in the full surreal class.

\begin{note}[\merge; regular stages in Part~II]\label{swo:vii:note:regular}
For shorter-first words the regular-stage statements are Part~II's

\section{Other comparison orders (source 15; remarks of sources 13, 14, 16, 17 and 18)}\label{swo:cb:sec:alternatives}

\merge\ Source~15's Section~10, printed whole as the base: three
lexicographies on three labels, inverse-rank lexicography
(\cref{swo:cb:thm:inverserank}), relation tables and the transfer of their
endpoints (\cref{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints,swo:cb:cor:tablepatterns}), the
failure of restriction-monotone linearizations (\cref{swo:cb:thm:restriction}),
the isomorphism quotient, the normal-form embedding
(\cref{swo:cb:prop:hahn}) and the birthday and simplicity remarks. Its
macros $\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{sl}}$, $\mathfrak W_{\mathrm{all}}$, $\mathcal D$
print as in this report; its $\operatorname{rk}$, $\operatorname{bl}$ are
this report's $\rk$ and $\bl$. The relation-code remarks of the other sources
follow in \cref{swo:vii:sub:relcodes}.

\subsection{Three lexicographies disagree on three labels}

On $0<1<2$, an enumeration is a permutation word. Its inverse-rank
vector lists the positions of labels $0,1,2$. For table lexicography
scan the directed pairs in this order:
\[
 (0,1),(0,2),(1,0),(1,2),(2,0),(2,1),
\]
and use bit $1$ when the first label precedes the second.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule Enumeration & Inverse ranks & Relation table\\\midrule
$012$ & $012$ & $110100$\\
$021$ & $021$ & $110001$\\
$102$ & $102$ & $011100$\\
$120$ & $201$ & $001110$\\
$201$ & $120$ & $100011$\\
$210$ & $210$ & $001011$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The increasing orders on the same six objects are respectively
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
\text{enumeration:}&012,021,102,120,201,210,\\
\text{inverse rank:}&012,021,102,201,120,210,\\
\text{table:}&210,120,102,201,021,012.
\end{array}
\]
The table comparison is not just the reverse of enumeration comparison.
As abstract finite orders, all three are of course the same six-point
chain. The difference here concerns which labelled object precedes which;
the next results distinguish abstract infinite order types as well.

\subsection{Inverse-rank lexicography}

Fix a baseline $q:\Ord\to\No$. For a set-like well-order $R$, let
$r_R:\No\to\Ord$ be its rank map. Compare the permutations
\[
 p_R=r_R\circ q:\Ord\longrightarrow\Ord
\]
lexicographically, using the ordinal order on the values. Conversely,
every class permutation $p$ corresponds to the enumeration $q\circ p^{-1}$.
This comparison uses ordinal ranks of fixed labels, rather than numerical
labels at fixed positions. For a non-set-like order an ordinal rank does
not exist at every label, so this version needs a further coding choice
before it can be applied to all raw orders.

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint and character of inverse ranks]\label{swo:cb:thm:inverserank}
The inverse-rank order of set-like global well-orders has a least element,
the baseline order, and no greatest element. It is dense. For every nonzero
limit ordinal $\lambda$, it has a point $M_\lambda$ with left cofinality
$\cf(\lambda)$. No point has a set coinitial family strictly above it.
In particular this order is not isomorphic to native enumeration lexicography.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The identity permutation is least. If $p$ first differs from the identity
at $\alpha$, all smaller ordinals have already appeared, so
$p(\alpha)>\alpha$. On every tail of a permutation there is an ascending
pair: otherwise a countable subsequence would descend through the ordinals.
Swapping that ascending pair makes the permutation lexicographically larger.
If $p<q$, perform such a swap strictly after their first disagreement;
the result lies strictly between them. This also proves no greatest point.
For a set family of points above $p$, bound all their first disagreements
with $p$ and perform an ascending swap later; the result is above $p$ and
below the entire family.

Let $M_\alpha$ begin with $\alpha$ and then list the remaining ordinals
in increasing order. This is a permutation of type $\Ord$ and is the
least permutation with first value $\alpha$, since it always chooses
the least unused ordinal thereafter. For limit $\lambda$,
\[
 M_\lambda=\sup_{\alpha<\lambda}M_\alpha.
\]
Indeed, a point below $M_\lambda$ has first value below $\lambda$;
some larger $\alpha<\lambda$ therefore gives a point $M_\alpha$
above it. A cofinal family below $M_\lambda$ must have first values
cofinal in $\lambda$, proving the exact character. Native enumeration
lex has no least element and no set cofinal family below any point.
\end{proof}

For a set carrier $X$ of cardinality $\mu$, scanning the labels by a
bijection $q:\mu\to X$ gives a least inverse-rank code among all
well-orders, again the $q$ order. On a fixed-type slice of type $\alpha$,
a least inverse-rank code exists exactly when $\alpha=\mu$: a least
rank vector can have no inversion, since swapping an inverted pair lowers
it, and an increasing bijection of its ranks forces precisely that type.
Thus even fixed-type endpoint behavior depends on the representation.

\subsection{Relation tables and a theorem about all their endpoints}

Choose a well-ordered scan of $X\times X$. For a proper class carrier
assume the scan is set-like, so every proper initial part of the scan is
a set. The table of a strict relation $R$ is its characteristic function,
compared with $0<1$. The native numerical order on $\No^2$ cannot itself
serve as a scan with a universal first-difference principle.

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint transfer for relation tables]\label{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints}
For a set-like scan, a well-order table is least among all well-order
tables if and only if it is least among all strict linear-order tables.
The dual statement holds for greatest elements. Both assertions also
hold with the class of set-like well-orders in place of all well-orders.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $R$ is a claimed minimum and a linear order $T$ has a smaller
table. At the first differing entry $(x,y)$, one has $xRy$ and $yTx$.
The earlier bits impose a set $P$ of directed order constraints: a true
off-diagonal bit gives its indicated direction, and a false one gives
the reversed direction. Both $R$ and $T$ satisfy these constraints.

Let $D$ be the downward closure of $y$ under finite $P$-paths, including
$y$. It is a set, contained in $\{y\}$ and the vertices of the earlier
scan. Since $T$ satisfies $P$ and $yTx$, one has $x\notin D$.
Move $D$ to the front of $R$, preserving the old order within $D$
and within its complement. The resulting relation $R'$ is a well-order.
It satisfies every earlier constraint because $D$ is $P$-downward
closed. It puts $y$ before $x$, so its first changed table bit is
smaller, a contradiction. If $R$ was set-like, $R'$ is still set-like:
new predecessor classes are contained in an old predecessor set together
with $D$. The proof for a maximum moves the appropriate opposite endpoint
at the first larger bit. The reverse implications are immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exactly three possible endpoint patterns]\label{swo:cb:cor:tablepatterns}
On an infinite carrier, table lexicography of well-orders has at most one
endpoint. By choosing the scan, each of the following can occur: a minimum
only, a maximum only, or neither. The same patterns occur for set-like
class well-orders of $\No$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Reversing a linear order complements all off-diagonal bits, so it reverses
the table comparison. If a minimum and maximum well-order both existed,
\Cref{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints} would make them endpoints among all linear
orders, and hence reversals of one another. An order and its reversal
cannot both be well-orders on an infinite carrier: the first $\omega$
points of one form a descending sequence in the other.

For any given linear order $T$, scan each unordered pair in a two-entry
block, putting its $T$-false direction first and its $T$-true direction
second. Scan the blocks set-likely. Then $T$ is the least linear-order
table: a different order changes the first entry of its earliest changed
block from $0$ to $1$. Reversing the two entries makes $T$ greatest.
Take $T$ to be a well-order for the one-endpoint cases, and a linear order
such that neither it nor its reverse is well-founded for the no-endpoint
case. Such an order exists on every infinite carrier; on $\No$ its
native order suffices. Endpoint transfer finishes the argument.
\end{proof}

A scan built around a non-set-like $R$ makes $R$ the minimum among all
well-orders but leaves no minimum among set-like well-orders. The greatest
linear-order table is then $R^{\mathrm{op}}$, not a well-order, so the
set-like subcollection has neither endpoint. Therefore a relation table
may erase the natural numerical comparison while retaining or losing
particular well-order types according to the chosen scan.

\begin{example}[Failure of a numerical pair scan]\label{swo:cb:n10.4}
On the integer labels, compare the enumerations
\[
 0,-1,1,-2,2,-3,3,\ldots,
 \qquad
 0,1,-1,2,-2,3,-3,\ldots,
\]
then append the same set-like enumeration of the remaining surreals.
The resulting set-like orders disagree on the directed pairs
$(-n,n)$ and $(n,-n)$, $n\geq1$. Their numerical-lexicographic
disagreement set in $\No^2$ has no least pair, because its first
coordinates include arbitrarily negative integers. A genuine well-ordered
scan avoids this problem; the numerical order alone does not.
\end{example}

\subsection{Restrictions, extensions, and relabelling}

Restriction to a smaller carrier can reverse enumeration lexicography:
$021<102$ on $0<1<2$, but on $\{1,2\}$ the restrictions are $21>12$.
The obstruction is stronger than a bad choice of lexicographic convention.

\begin{theorem}[No total linearization respects every restriction]\label{swo:cb:thm:restriction}
There is no total order on the six well-orders of a three-element carrier
making all three restrictions to two-element carriers monotone, no matter
which of the two orders is chosen on each two-element well-order space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Encode each pair restriction by $0$ or $1$ according to its chosen order.
The three restrictions determine the original relation, so the six
well-orders map injectively to $\{0,1\}^3$. If all restrictions were
monotone, the six objects in increasing order would give a coordinatewise
increasing chain of six distinct Boolean vectors. Every strict step raises
the sum of the coordinates, whose possible values are $0,1,2,3$.
Such a chain has at most four points, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

Thus arbitrary restrictions do not form a monotone system automatically.
The theorem does not rule out a carefully chosen nested filtration or
restricted family. In the other direction, fixing a well-order $Q$ of
$X\setminus Y$ makes $R\mapsto R+Q$ an order embedding from the native
well-order space of $Y$ into that of $X$. First disagreement occurs before
the fixed tail. Prepending a common labelled prefix works as well.

No linearization can be invariant under every relabelling of a carrier
with at least two labels. A transposition acts as a nontrivial involution
on its well-orders, since well-orders are rigid. But an increasing
involution of a linear order must be the identity: $x<f(x)$ would imply
$f(x)<f(f(x))=x$. Some auxiliary label structure is indispensable.
Increasing automorphisms of the numerical surreal line preserve native
enumeration lex; a table scan or baseline must instead be transported
along with the labels.

\subsection{The quotient by order isomorphism is a new construction}

On the numerical carrier $\mathbb N$, let
\[
 R=(0,1,2,\ldots),\quad
 S=(1,2,3,\ldots,0),\quad
 T=(2,0,1,3,4,\ldots).
\]
Their types are $\omega,\omega+1,\omega$, respectively, and their first
labels give $R<S<T$. Thus the equivalence class of type $\omega$ is
not convex. A linear-order quotient requires convex classes, so labelled
lexicography does not descend to abstract order types.

One may separately order the possible set types by ordinal comparison.
For $|X|=\mu$ infinite they are precisely $\mu\leq\alpha<\mu^+$;
in that comparison their order type is $\mu^+$. The original labelled
order embeds no ordinal $\mu^+$, by \Cref{swo:cb:thm:sets}. This is a
particularly sharp sign that the quotient-type order is a different object.
For set-like well-orders of all $\No$, quotienting by class-order
isomorphism collapses everything to the one type $\Ord$. Arbitrary
non-set-like class types require their own collection and comparison
framework; raw labelled lexicography provides neither automatically.

\subsection{Numerically well-ordered subsets and a normal-form embedding}

Let $A,B\subseteq\No$ be sets or classes well-ordered by the inherited
numerical order. Their union is well-ordered: a nonempty subclass has its
least member among the minima of its intersections with $A$ and $B$.
Consequently $\min(A\mathbin\triangle B)$ exists whenever $A\ne B$.
This makes an intrinsic characteristic comparison possible without a
global scan of the entire numerical line:
\[
 A<_{\chi}B\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       \min(A\mathbin\triangle B)\in A.
\]
It uses membership bits with $1<0$. Transitivity is first-difference
transitivity on the well-ordered union of three carriers. For increasing
enumerations it agrees with numerical first difference and \emph{longer
prefixes first}. The empty carrier is greatest. The shorter-first
convention agrees on genuine unequal next labels but reverses proper-prefix
pairs and makes the empty carrier least.

\begin{proposition}[Coefficient-one normal forms realize set supports]\label{swo:cb:prop:hahn}
For a set-sized naturally well-ordered subset $A\subseteq\No$, the map
\[
 H(A)=-\sum_{a\in A}\omega^{-a}
\]
is a well-defined surreal, and $A<_{\chi}B$ if and only if $H(A)<H(B)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The exponent support $\{-a:a\in A\}$ is reverse well-ordered and is
a set, so the displayed Conway normal form is legitimate. At the least
$a$ in the symmetric difference, the largest differing exponent is
$-a$. The set containing $a$ has coefficient $-1$ there rather than
$0$, and therefore gives the smaller surreal. Uniqueness of normal
form gives injectivity \cite{Conway,Gonshor}.
\end{proof}

This particular construction does not admit proper-class support: the
normal form of one surreal has set support. Naturally well-ordered
proper subclasses nevertheless exist with every class well-order type
by surreal universality. For example
\[
 \left\{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}:\alpha\in\Ord\right\}\cup\{0\}
\]
has natural type $\Ord+1$. Its final point has a proper class of
earlier points. The reciprocal and addition in this example are surreal
operations on the embedded ordinal labels.

At a set limit-length increasing word, shorter-first order makes the
word the supremum of its proper prefixes; longer-first order makes it
their infimum. The associated one-sided cofinality is the cofinality
of the length. These features expose the termination convention in the
order itself, even when both conventions admit embeddings into $\No$.

\subsection{Birthday and simplicity do not secretly provide a scan}

Simplicity, interpreted as initial sign-sequence extension, is a partial
tree order. The one-sign strings $+$ and $-$ are incomparable by it.
Numerical sign comparison instead uses the middle termination symbol
$-<\text{undefined}<+$ and gives the surreal line, not a well-order.

Even birthday-first ordering with numerical tie-breaking fails. The
strings
\[
 s_n=-^n\concat+^\omega\qquad(n<\omega)
\]
all have birthday $\omega$, since $n+\omega=\omega$, and satisfy
$s_{n+1}<s_n$. Thus the birthday-$\omega$ level already contains a
descending sequence under numerical tie-breaking. A genuine
birthday-compatible global well-order requires well-ordering each level
with additional coherent choice data. That choice must be stated; it is
not supplied by birthdays or simplicity alone.

\begin{note}[\merge; repository counterparts of source~15's Section~10]\label{swo:vii:note:alt}
Relation-table lexicography is source~08's relation-code order
(\cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode}: equimorphic with $\B_{\Ord}$ for every
coordinate enumeration) and source~12's remark with its three-point check
(\cref{swo:st:sec:subclasses}). New in source~15 are the endpoint transfer
(\cref{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints}) and the three endpoint patterns
(\cref{swo:cb:cor:tablepatterns}), which show that the endpoints of a
relation-code order are artifacts of the scan; inverse-rank lexicography
(\cref{swo:cb:thm:inverserank}); the restriction theorem
(\cref{swo:cb:thm:restriction}); and the normal-form embedding
(\cref{swo:cb:prop:hahn}). The birthday tie-break example is source~12's
(``Birthday is not a canonical global well-order'', after
\cref{swo:st:thm:choice}), and source~18 repeats it
(\cref{swo:vii:sub:relcodes}). The non-convexity of type classes is made by
sources~13, 14, 16 and~17 in their maps (\cref{swo:vii:sec:maps}).
\end{note}

\subsection{Relation-code remarks of sources 13, 14 and 18}\label{swo:vii:sub:relcodes}

\merge\ Three further remarks on relation codes, each printed whole:
source~13's (from its foundations section), source~14's (from its Section~11)
and source~18's (from its section on global well-orders). Source~16's is in
its map (\cref{swo:ds:sec:interpretations}), source~17's in its map
(\cref{swo:gs:sec:meanings}). They make one point, that relation-code
lexicography depends on the scan and the bit convention and is not
enumeration lexicography; they prove no theorem about it beyond
\cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode} and \cref{swo:cb:sec:alternatives}.

% ---- source 13 (batch-81 manuscript 01), lines 1661-1678
\subsubsection{Source 13: relation-bit lexicography is a different order}

Fix a set-like global enumeration of the ordered pairs of surreal
labels. Read the characteristic bits of a raw relation in this order
and compare at the first different bit. This is a legitimate
code-dependent class lexicography. It is not in general the labeled
common-prefix comparison of \cref{swo:cf:lex}: the first edge
in a chosen code order need not reveal the first disagreement of the
enumerated labels.

The preceding report analyzes this relation-code alternative and its
binary-word equimorphism (\cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode}; Section~17 of this report at the pin). A choice of
pair enumeration is indispensable. Replacing the label order by
birthday order or comparing raw Conway construction trees would impose
still other auxiliary data. Such choices can be studied rigorously,
but no one of them should be advertised as \emph{the} lexicographic
order without identifying the coding convention.

% ---- source 14 (batch-81 manuscript 02), lines 1516-1531
\subsubsection{Source 14: relation-code lexicography really is another order}
One may instead fix a class bijection scheduling $\No\times\No$ by
$\Ord$, and compare characteristic functions of well-order relations
at their first differing scheduled pair. This defines a linear
comparison on the admitted relations but depends on the schedule and
on the convention $0<1$.

The difference is visible with three labels. Let numerical order be
$0<1<2$, and let the enumerations be $e=(0,1,2)$ and $f=(1,0,2)$.
Enumeration lex gives $e<f$. If the relation-coordinate schedule begins
with $(0,1)$, their strict-relation bits there are $1$ and $0$, so the
relation-code comparison gives $e>f$. Starting instead with $(1,0)$
reverses those bits and that conclusion. This counterexample does not
involve any proper-class complication. Results proved for numerical
first-disagreement order cannot simply be transported to relation codes.

% ---- source 18 (batch-81 manuscript 06), lines 2351-2381
\subsubsection{Source 18: inherited numerical well-orders and relation codes}

Numerical $<$ on $\No$ is not a well-order; the negative integers
already descend. Nevertheless, many subclasses are numerically
well-ordered. The ordinals give type $\Ord$, and
\[
 \{-1/(\alpha+1):\alpha\in\Ord\}\cup\{0\}
\]
has numerical order type $\Ord+1$. Under Global Choice, class
universality gives a numerically ordered copy of every supplied class
well-order. This concerns the range of an embedding in the surreal line,
not a well-order of every surreal label.

One can instead order well-order \emph{relations} by characteristic
bits. Choose a set-like class enumeration of $X\times X$, and compare
the bits of two relations at their first unequal pair. The resulting
comparison is well-defined, but is a different auxiliary order. Even
on $\{0,1\}$ the enumerations $(0,1)<(1,0)$ compare in the opposite
direction if the first tested pair is $(0,1)$ and $0<1$ for relation
bits. The enumeration rule tests first labels, while the relation rule
tests a chosen predicate membership. Its dependence on the chosen pair
order should be recorded explicitly.

Finally, ordering birthday first and then ordering sign expansions
lexicographically does not produce a global well-order. The fixed
birthday-$\omega$ slice already contains descending sequences. A
genuine birthday-first global well-order must use an auxiliary
well-order within each birthday level, not its numerical sign order.

\section{What this Part answers}\label{swo:vii:sec:status}

\merge\ Dated notes, 3 October 2026, in the form of \cref{swo:vi:sec:status};
where the question index of \cref{swo:sec:questions} (dated 2 October 2026)
says otherwise, these notes supersede it.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Source~09's direction ``Other termination conventions for set
words''} (\cref{swo:ec:sec:research}): \textbf{answered} for unrestricted and
injective words. Exactly the placements of a formal terminator satisfying the
three conditions of \cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator} give the set-cut property,
hence (in $\GBC$) an order isomorphic to $\No$; every placement gives one of
the types of \cref{swo:ds:thm:alltermination}; the distinguished internal value
is \cref{swo:fs:thm:central,swo:sh:wv:centered-eta}, and the prefix-free codes
are listed in \cref{swo:vii:note:codes}. For increasing words with the
terminator at zero see~(b).
\item \emph{Source~08's question~14, increasing-subset and alternate-termination
orders} (\cref{swo:sk:sec:research}): \textbf{answered} for prefix-small,
prefix-large and centered conventions on unrestricted and injective words
(\cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator,swo:ds:thm:alltermination}), for shorter-first
increasing words (isomorphic to the other two classes in $\GBC$,
\cref{swo:ds:thm:wordisomorphism}), and for centered increasing words up to
their isomorphism type: local characters, all set-cut obstructions,
equimorphism without isomorphism (\cref{swo:sh:wv:inc-characters,swo:sh:wv:inc-cuts,swo:sh:wv:inc-corollary}).
Open: the isomorphism type of the centered increasing order (source~18's own
question) and increasing words with an interior terminator at a threshold
other than zero.
\item \emph{Source~13's question ``A classification of termination cuts''} (for
a class alphabet isomorphic to $\No$): answered by source~16
(\cref{swo:ds:thm:terminator,swo:ds:thm:alltermination}), transported along an
isomorphism of the alphabet with $\No$ in $\GBC$.
\item \emph{Source~16's question ``Centered increasing words''}: answered by
source~18 for the threshold at zero (with zero excluded, padded, or adjacent
to a distinct terminator: \cref{swo:vii:sec:centered}); open for other
interior thresholds.
\item \emph{Source~08's question~9 and source~09's direction ``Non-set-like
well-order relations''} (relation-code orders; \cref{swo:sk:sec:research,swo:ec:sec:research}):
answered in part. Which endpoints a relation-code order of well-orders has is
an artifact of the scan: a minimum only, a maximum only, or neither, each by a
suitable scan, and never both on an infinite carrier
(\cref{swo:cb:thm:tableendpoints,swo:cb:cor:tablepatterns}). The isomorphism
question for two scans stays open.
\item \emph{Source~11's question on optimal birthday bounds} (\cref{swo:n16.6}):
not answered. Sources~13, 14, 16, 17 and~18 give explicit codes with birthdays
$\sum(2\cdot\bd+2)$, $\sum(4\cdot\bd+4)+2$ and $\sum(1+2\cdot\bd+2)$
(\cref{swo:vii:note:codes}); none is claimed optimal.
\end{enumerate}
Sources~16 and~18 also ask how much of the word isomorphisms survives without
a global well-order; source~18's proof (\cref{swo:vii:sub:sh-tree}) shows that
one set-like global well-order suffices as a parameter, so the question is
whether that parameter can be removed. It is open.

\subsection{Source 11's research questions and topics}\label{swo:sec:research}

The questions below are proposed extensions of the present
development. Except where a source is explicitly named, they
are not asserted to be established open problems in the
literature. Several ask for a stronger classification than
the proved results supply.

\subsubsection{A precise description of the represented cuts}

Fix the dense ordinary class \(\Pbd\cong\No\). Every
set-like global enumeration determines a lower cut of
\(\Pbd\), and so does every injective nonexhaustive
\(\Ord\)-word. The omitted-range example shows that the
first collection does not fill every cut generated by the
second.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.1}
Characterize, using the prefix tree and its numerical
labels, exactly which class cuts of \(\Pbd\) arise from
injective \(\Ord\)-words, and which of these words are
exhaustive.
\end{question}

A useful first target is a necessary-and-sufficient
compatibility condition on the intersections of a cut with
prefix cylinders. The challenge is to separate missing
labels, impossible repeated labels, and cuts that escape
every coherent branch. This should first be stated for a
fixed uniform class cut, before quantifying over an entire
hyperclass of cuts.

\subsubsection{The embedding relationship between the two global orders}

The set-like fragment and binary class words are
equimorphic, while the unrestricted order has additional
convex fibers. Theorem~\ref{swo:thm:universe-nonembedding}
rules out a reverse embedding in the full universe
interpretation. Theorem~\ref{swo:thm:km-nonembedding} also
rules out a definable uniform reverse embedding in
\(\KM+\GC\).

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.2}
What is the weakest comprehension scheme over \(\GBC\)
that proves the uniform nonembedding result? Can the
inverse-evaluation step be replaced by an elementary
argument, or is a genuine strengthening necessary?
\end{question}

A precise project should specify the syntactic complexity
of the proposed transformation and of the comprehension
used to invert its code assignment. In the bounded
setting one can instead compare the density and
cellularity of all well-orders of \(\No_{<\kappa}\)
with their minimum-length stratum, especially when
\(\kappa\) is not inaccessible. The full-universe case
is already settled above; the remaining questions
concern strength and wider hypotheses.

\subsubsection{Iterated residual decomposition}

One extraction of the canonical initial \(\Ord\)-part
requires only elementary class comprehension. A residual
proper class has another such part, suggesting a hierarchy
of successive blocks.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.3}
What is the exact recursion strength needed to organize
iterated residual decompositions uniformly, and what
class order types can occur as their block-index orders?
\end{question}

An initial project could analyze finitely many blocks and
specified set-length constructions, recording where the
recursion state becomes a class. The general case should
be compared with known \(\ETR\) results on class
well-order comparability, rather than assumed to follow
from the one-step theorem.

\subsubsection{Recovering the prefix structure from the bare order}

The definitions exhibit a labeled tree of prefixes.
An automorphism of the bare lexicographic order need not
obviously preserve its displayed labels or cylinders.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.4}
Which prefix cones, canonical-core fibers, and finite
permutation blocks are definable from the linear order
alone? What extra predicates make the reconstruction
unique?
\end{question}

Adjacency directly detects finite stretches, but a
singleton fiber can be exhaustive or can omit one label.
Thus local block size alone cannot recover set-likeness.
An analysis of the automorphism group may clarify what
the order remembers about the original enumeration.

\subsubsection{The bounded-support choice of baseline}

Every baseline enumeration gives an ordinary class
\(\Pbd\) abstractly isomorphic to \(\No\). If two
baselines differ on a proper class of positions, their
bounded-support subfamilies can be disjoint.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.5}
How do the two dense classes sit inside the same
\(\Hsl(\No)\), and when does an automorphism of that
higher-order order carry one onto the other?
\end{question}

The abstract isomorphism of the two dense orders does
not automatically extend to all global enumerations.
The represented-cut problem is likely relevant. A
restricted first case is a baseline change obtained by
an order automorphism of the numerical surreal line.

\subsubsection{Sharper birthday bounds and minimal codes}

The explicit code gives a usable bound
\[
 \bd(E(f))=\sum_{i<\dom f}
              \bigl(1+2\cdot\bd(f(i))+2\bigr).
\]
It is designed for a transparent proof, not asserted
to be optimal.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.6}
Which birthday bounds are optimal for uniform embeddings
of injective surreal words? Can shorter prefix-free
coefficient codes preserve useful simplicity relations?
\end{question}

Regular cutoffs permit same-stage compression; singular
cutoffs show why a cardinal bound alone is insufficient.
A sharpened result should specify whether it preserves
only numerical order, prefix extension, simplicity, or
all of these.

\subsubsection{Saturation of more general bounded alphabets}

If \(X\) is an \(\eta_\kappa\) order of cardinality
\(\kappa\), its minimal-length permutation order inherits
\(\eta_\kappa\). Birthday fragments often have cardinality
\(2^{<\kappa}\), which may exceed \(\kappa\).

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.7}
Determine the exact cut spectrum and point characters of
\(\WO_\lambda(\No_{<\kappa})\), where
\(\lambda=2^{<\kappa}\), without assuming
\(2^{<\kappa}=\kappa\).
\end{question}

The issue combines the size of a used prefix with the
number and birthdays of possible numerical separators.
The real-alphabet formulas in the precursor report
cannot settle this case by substitution.

\subsubsection{Class extensions and available global orders}

Two models may have the same sets and hence the same
individual surreal numbers, but different available
classes and different global well-orders.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.8}
Which features of the lexicographic theory are invariant
under extensions of the class part, and how does the
represented-cut collection change?
\end{question}

Uniform formulas such as first divergence remain the
same for the existing relations. Existence of additional
enumerations changes the larger totality. This offers a
way to distinguish properties of the labeled surreal
order from properties of the surrounding class theory.

\subsubsection{A verified first milestone for ProveIt}

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.9}
Can the explicit word compression and the direct
predecessor comparison be formalized first, with a small,
reusable interface independent of surreal arithmetic?
\end{question}

These are concrete, finite-dependency targets:
ordinal concatenation, numerical first difference,
injectivity, and well-order predecessor sets. The next
milestone is the general successor-cardinal obstruction,
followed by bounded-support completion and the
small-family interpolation theorem. This ordering keeps
the central algorithms separate from a formalization of
an entire higher-order set theory.

\subsubsection{Uniform mathematical constructions indexed by well-orders}

The Kanovei--Shelah method suggests using a definable
linear order of codes to index constructions without
selecting one privileged code. The surreal setting
raises additional smallness conditions.

\begin{question}\label{swo:n16.10}
Which finite-support or bounded-support constructions
can be indexed by a uniform class family of global
well-orders, and which require a genuine higher-order
index object?
\end{question}

A first investigation should specify its support sets,
evaluation maps, and quantifier domains before adding
algebra or model theory. The diagonal theorem prevents
an exhaustive class-code shortcut, but leaves many
useful uniform families available.

\subsection{Source 12's research questions and prioritized program}
\label{swo:st:sec:questions}

The following are proposed continuations. Except where an older question
is explicitly attributed, they are not claimed to be recognized open
problems in the literature.

\begin{question}[All fixed enumeration types]\label{swo:st:n14.1}
Determine $\bl(\WO_\alpha(\No_{<\kappa}))$ for every ordinal $\alpha$ of
cardinality $2^{<\kappa}$, not only the minimal type. Can the answer be
expressed by a precise ordinal-sum invariant of admissible entry-code
lengths, together with the finite terminal permutation factor?
\end{question}
The singular theorem identifies the first obstruction that any such
formula must capture. A useful initial target is
$\alpha=\mu\cdot2$ and then $\mu\cdot\gamma+n$ for finite $n$.

\begin{question}[Noncardinal birthday bounds]\label{swo:st:n14.2}
For an arbitrary infinite ordinal $\theta$, classify the minimal
permutation order of $\No_{<\theta}$. Which features of the ordinal tail of
$\theta$, beyond $|\theta|$ and $|\No_{<\theta}|$, survive in its coding
rank and local character?
\end{question}
The cone argument for an initial cardinal relies on closure under
$i+1+\alpha$ below the cutoff. At a noncardinal cutoff that closure can
fail and should be replaced by a stratified family of cones.

\begin{question}[Isomorphism, not merely equimorphism]\label{swo:st:n14.3}
Classify the order topology and isomorphism invariants of
$\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})$. At regular cutoffs, and at singular strong limits,
what are its density, cellularity, cut-character spectra, and degrees of
homogeneity? Which distinguish it from other orders of the same binary
rank?
\end{question}
The binary cube itself cannot be the literal answer: it has endpoints
and adjacent pairs, while the surreal permutation order has different
local behavior. A dense suborder equimorphic with a cube can retain
substantial information not measured by rank.

\begin{question}[The hierarchy of proper-class blocks]\label{swo:st:n14.4}
Study the subpredicates of $\Hall(\No)$ with types
$\Ord\cdot\gamma+\alpha$ for fixed set ordinals $\gamma,\alpha$.
Give explicit, size-safe embedding and nonembedding theorems between
these subpredicates and the set-like order.
\end{question}
The inaccessible-model calculation provides concrete bounded targets and
shows that the full order is strictly more complex than the set-like
order there. Extending binary fusion to uniform transformations on
class predicates may require stronger comprehension; that dependence
should be proved, not assumed away.

\begin{remark}[Source 12's question ``The exact weak-choice strength'', answered]\label{swo:st:n14.5}
\emph{Source 12's text:} Remove set-level AC from the base theory in \cref{swo:st:thm:choice}. What
principle is equivalent to a class well-order of the sign-sequence
surreals then? Compare it with coherent well-ordering of power sets of
ordinals and with uniform ordinal presentations of transitive closures.

\merge\ \emph{Answered (2 October 2026).} Source 11's \cref{swo:cf:choice} proves the
equivalence over GB with no set-level choice: a class well-order of the
sign-sequence surreals is equivalent to global choice. Its proof is
precisely a uniform well-ordering of the power sets $V_{\beta+1}$,
obtained by set-valued recursion from the given well-order on the sign
sequences of each length $\theta$; see \cref{swo:rem:answered-choice}.
(Corrected on 3 October 2026 after the Hilbert's-tenth review
\path{review_surreal_synthesis_0be9b9134.md}: ``uniform'' replaces
``coherent'', since these orders need not extend one another;
\cref{swo:rem:answered-choice} gives a finite example.)
\end{remark}
This is separate from the question whether surreal universality for
class linear orders itself implies global choice, raised by Hamkins
\cite{HamkinsUnivChoice}. The present equivalence concerns a well-order of
all surreal labels and does not settle that universality question.

\begin{question}[Elementary common-prefix theory]\label{swo:st:n14.6}
Formalize \cref{swo:st:thm:fulllex} over a precisely chosen weak class theory.
How little comprehension and class well-foundedness are needed? Can one
characterize the same relation directly by a formula avoiding explicit
mention of the common-prefix class?
\end{question}
The definition already expands to elementary quantifiers. A mechanized
proof would make the separation from abstract class comparability
particularly transparent and yield a reusable theorem for other carriers.

\begin{question}[Automorphisms and finite condensation]\label{swo:st:n14.7}
Determine natural automorphism groups of the full lexicographic order,
and of the order after collapsing finite residual blocks. Which
operations arise from numerical automorphisms or permutations of the
underlying surreal carrier, and which genuinely act on enumeration
structure?
\end{question}
Finite-tail blocks are now characterized intrinsically by adjacency.
That makes condensation a plausible first invariant, but no complete
classification of its quotient is claimed here.

\begin{question}[Dependence on the class realization]\label{swo:st:n14.8}
Fix a transitive set universe $M$ and vary its admissible class collection
$\mathcal X$. Which external order invariants of the admitted global
well-orders detect this change? Can the order reconstruct useful
information about available truth predicates or class choice functions?
\end{question}
The diagonal obstruction persists internally, while external cardinality
can vary. This makes the distinction between full and Henkin semantics a
source of mathematical questions rather than merely a formal warning.

\begin{question}[Compatibility between birthday cutoffs]\label{swo:st:n14.9}
Construct and analyze coherent embeddings from the well-order spaces at
smaller birthday cutoffs into those at larger cutoffs. Which choices of
reserve labels and tail enumerations are functorial, and which preserve
finite condensation, coding ranks, or canonical cylinders?
\end{question}
Naively extending every enumeration by an arbitrary tail can change
minimal length, and fixed choices need not be compatible across three
cutoffs. A coherence theorem would make universe-lifting arguments much
more useful in a proof assistant.

\subsection{Source 09's research directions}
\label{swo:ec:sec:research}
The following are proposed problems emerging from the development, not
a claim that all are already catalogued open problems. Each is stated
with a foundational level so that its intended meaning is clear.

\subsubsection{Formal proof of the surreal core theorem}
\textbf{Framework:} universe-indexed Lean with explicit smallness and a
baseline parameter. Complete the prefix-extension and interpolation
modules, then prove the back-and-forth isomorphism. The difficult part
is expected to be the bookkeeping of small histories and variable
ordinal prefixes, not the finite first-disagreement arguments. A useful
first milestone is the inaccessible or regular-cardinal set-sized
version, parameterized by an abstract saturated alphabet.

\subsubsection{Exact axiomatic strength of the global conclusions}
\textbf{Framework:} $\GB$ with ordinary set choice but without global
choice. \Cref{swo:ec:prop:baseline-choice} settles the baseline question: a
set-like global well-order of $\No$ is equivalent to global choice.
Determine the exact strength of alternative universality and homogeneity
assertions that do not already assume such a baseline. In particular,
which conclusions can be stated and proved for individually definable
class orders without supplying a single uniform global enumeration?
Distinguish uniform existence assertions from a schema treating one
fixed definition at a time; the latter is not automatically a uniform
class construction.

\subsubsection{Which cuts of the core represent exhaustive branches?}
\textbf{Framework:} class cut parameters over $\Psupp(b)$ or an explicitly
higher-sorted completion. Every global enumeration determines a cut
in the dense core, and density makes this representation injective.
Characterize its image intrinsically. The missing-branch construction
shows that injectivity of a class word is insufficient: exhaustiveness
must be detected. A promising formulation separates coherence of set
prefixes, realizability of each next-coordinate cut, and eventual
coverage of every surreal value.

\subsubsection{Automorphisms beyond set-sized partial maps}
\textbf{Framework:} $\GBC$ for the core; higher-sort or universe-bounded
versions for the full comparison object. Set-sized partial
isomorphisms extend in the core, but proper-class partial maps need not.
Classify which preserve enough prefix or support structure to extend.
Also distinguish automorphisms of the bare order from those preserving
the preferred filtration by support bounds. These are different
classification problems even though the bare core is always $\No$.

\subsubsection{Non-set-like well-order relations}
\textbf{Framework:} $\GBC$ or $\GBC+\ETR$, with a fixed relation-code
coordinate order. Study the alternative binary comparison of all global
well-order relations. Which order properties depend on the coordinate
enumeration? Does it contain a natural set-coded dense core, and when
are its cuts represented by actual class well-orders? No conclusion
about that different comparison follows merely from the enumeration
results proved here.

\subsubsection{Sharp birthday and support bounds}
\textbf{Framework:} $\ZFC$ on bounded fragments. Given bounds on the
birthdays and first-disagreement coordinates of a set family, determine
optimal bounds for an interpolating prefix and its supported completion.
The present proof bounds the forced prefix length by
$\sup\{\Delta(E_i,F_j)+1:i\in I,j\in J\}+1$ and uses an oversized
cardinal for support. That bound is
safe but not generally minimal. This direction could yield concrete
complexity estimates for symbolic surreal order calculations.

\subsubsection{What survives away from inaccessible truncations?}
\textbf{Framework:} $\ZFC$ with specified cardinal arithmetic. Replace
the inaccessible $\kappa$ by a regular cardinal and a set alphabet with
appropriate cut saturation. Separate the roles of regularity,
$\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, and closure under the required cut constructor.
At singular cardinals, the first-disagreement bound can reach the
ambient length even for relatively small families; identify the
resulting interpolation spectrum and construct sharp counterexamples.

\subsubsection{Other termination conventions for set words}
\textbf{Framework:} $\GBC$ or bounded $\ZFC$. Our dictionary order makes
proper prefixes smaller and therefore creates the limit-prefix
suprema of \cref{swo:ec:prop:prefix-cf}. Study a distinguished internal
termination value, restricted alphabets, or prefix-free codes of set
well-orders. Determine exactly which variants have the set-cut property
and hence are isomorphic to $\No$. The convention must be part of the
statement, not an implicit analogy with surreal signs.

\subsubsection{Uniform infinitary constructions indexed by redundant codes}
\textbf{Framework:} bounded universes first. Kanovei--Shelah-style
redundant indexing suggests avoiding a choice of one representative
for each well-order or auxiliary object. Investigate constructions
using the support core or bounded strata as indices, while checking
that the resulting products, functions, and quotient relations remain
at the declared level. The diagonal theorem forbids a uniform
same-level list of all global enumerations, but does not preclude
purpose-built redundant families adequate for a particular theorem.

\subsection{Source 08's research questions and topics}\label{swo:sk:sec:research}

The following are directions not resolved by the present manuscript.
They are not blanket assertions that no relevant answer exists in the
literature. Each should begin with a targeted literature search and a
precise choice of size level.

\subsubsection{Set-sized enumeration orders}

\paragraph{1. Isomorphism classification beyond the common spectrum.}
The ordinal spectrum and cardinality of $\W(X)$ depend only on $|X|$,
while its local cylinder structure remembers the numerical order of
residual alphabets. Which additional invariants of $X$ can be recovered
from $\W(X)$? Determine sufficient and necessary conditions for
$\W(X)\cong\W(Y)$, rather than merely embeddings in both directions.
Dense alphabets, well-orders, and mixed sums provide natural test cases.

\paragraph{2. Exact invariants at regular cutoffs (\merge\ partly answered; see the note after this list).}
For regular $\kappa$ with $2^{<\kappa}=\kappa$, compute the density,
cellularity, point characters, and cut cofinalities of
$\W_\kappa(\No_{<\kappa})$. The binary equimorphism in \eqref{swo:sk:eq:t6} does not by
itself determine its order-topological isomorphism type. Investigate
whether the support skeleton construction has a natural $\kappa$-sized
dense realization inside this set order under weaker assumptions than
strong inaccessibility.

\paragraph{3. Singular birthdays and code lengths (\merge\ answered in part; see the note after this list).}
At singular $\kappa$, exactly which cuts of size below $\kappa$ fail to
be realized in $\No_{<\kappa}$ or in its fixed-length enumeration
order? Determine the least binary coding lengths for these orders and
how they depend on cofinality and cardinal arithmetic. The three
specific uses of regularity in \cref{swo:sk:thm:cutoff} identify where a new
argument is needed.

\paragraph{4. Prescribed noninitial lengths.}
For $\alpha$ of cardinality $\kappa$, classify
$\W_\alpha(\No_{<\lambda})$. Finite tails are understood through
\cref{swo:sk:thm:adjacent}, but noninitial limit lengths may preserve finer
lexicographic complexity. Compare this problem with the fixed-type
analysis proposed in the continuum manuscript \cite{Batch76Source02}, checking
its hypotheses and proofs independently for nonseparable alphabets.

\subsubsection{The skeleton and the virtual ambient order}

\paragraph{5. Definable choices of the surreal isomorphism.}
The back-and-forth isomorphism $\Psupp(b)\cong\No$ depends on witness
choices. With a specified global well-order, can it be chosen to
respect useful support or birthday bounds? What additional
normalization gives uniqueness, if any? Distinguish definability from
parameters, parameter-free definability, and invariance under a change
of baseline.

\paragraph{6. Automorphisms and extension from a skeleton.}
An order automorphism $\varphi$ of $\No$ acts on $\Hsl$ by
$e\mapsto\varphi\circ e$ and sends $O_b$ onto $O_{\varphi\circ b}$.
Which automorphisms of one skeleton extend to uniform automorphisms of
the full virtual order? Can order alone recognize eventual-agreement
orbits, or are these additional structure supplied by the coordinate
presentation?

\paragraph{7. Full virtual lexicographic products.}
For an ordinary class carrier, every set-indexed lexicographic power
of $\No$ embeds into a skeleton. The \emph{full virtual} product
$\Hsl\times_{\lex}\Hsl$ is a different object. Does it admit a uniform
order embedding into $\Hsl$? More generally, determine an absorption
spectrum for uniform virtual powers. Simply concatenating two
$\Ord$-long words has domain $\Ord+\Ord$, not a set-like $\Ord$
domain, so the set-sized concatenation argument is unavailable.

\paragraph{8. Higher-size cuts and completion.}
The set-cut theorem deliberately leaves out cuts specified by proper
class families of enumeration codes or by higher collections of
classes. Classify which such cuts are realized once a precise
higher-type language is chosen. A proposed Dedekind completion must
identify the type of its cut objects and cannot be treated as another
ordinary class without a size proof.

\paragraph{9. Classification of relation-code orders.}
For two global coordinate enumerations $q,q'$ of $\No^2$, the
relation-code orders have the same binary equimorphism type. Are there
useful hypotheses yielding an isomorphism? Determine which invariants
are artifacts of the coordinate order and which reflect genuine
properties of class well-orders.

\subsubsection{Foundations and formalization}

\paragraph{10. Minimal axioms, stated theorem by theorem.}
Separate the strength of a baseline $\Ord\cong\No$, uniform prefix
completion, orbit cut filling, and the global back-and-forth theorem.
Over variants of $\GB$ with set Choice, which global selection
principles are necessary for their uniform formulations? The present
proofs establish sufficiency of $\GBC$, not an exact reverse-mathematical
classification.

\paragraph{11. Comparison of non-set-like class well-orders (\merge\ answered; see the note after this list).}
Formulate an explicit alignment or comparison principle for arbitrary
class well-orders and locate its recursion requirements. Which
restricted domains, such as finite sums of $\Ord$ and set ordinals,
admit direct elementary algorithms? A theorem about all class domains
should not be extrapolated from these examples without proof.

\paragraph{12. Dependence on the class collection.}
For fixed $M$ and $\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal D$, compare the virtual
orders of global enumerations in $(M,\mathcal C)$ and
$(M,\mathcal D)$. The same set-coded skeleton relative to a shared
baseline remains available, but new class rows may appear. Study which
higher-size cut properties, operator embeddings, and definability
properties change.

\paragraph{13. Quantitative and certified prefix extension.}
Formalize the support bound in \cref{swo:sk:lem:extension}, the forced-prefix
length in \cref{swo:sk:thm:orbitcut}, and the birthday of its stopping entry.
This would make the central theorem a sequence of explicit bounded
lemmas rather than one monolithic existence proof. It is a strong
first target for the repository's existing small-cut interface.

\paragraph{14. Increasing-subset and alternate-termination orders.}
Study lexicographic orders of numerically increasing set sequences,
as opposed to arbitrary enumerations, and compare prefix-small,
prefix-large, and centered-termination conventions. The limit-prefix
counterexample shows that these choices affect local cofinalities even
when the alphabet is $\No$.

\paragraph{15. An order-theoretic interface for class nonstandard models.}
Determine which part of a Kanovei--Shelah-style indexing construction
can use a set-coded skeleton and which genuinely needs a larger
collection. Keep the order embedding problem separate from the
existence of a class ultrafilter, satisfaction relation, or transfer
principle. A successful construction should include an explicit
uniform-family and smallness audit at every step.

Question~11 is answered: the labelled comparisons of \cref{swo:cf:lex} and
\cref{swo:st:thm:fulllex} compare arbitrary class well-orders of a labelled
class with elementary comprehension, without $\ETR$ (see the note after
\cref{swo:sk:prop:relationcode}). Question~3 is answered in part: the least
binary coding length of the fixed-length enumeration order at a singular
cutoff is $\kappa\cdot\kappa$ at a strong limit and $2^{<\kappa}$ otherwise
(\cref{swo:st:thm:transition}); which cuts of size below $\kappa$ fail to be
realized remains open. Question~2 is answered in part by
\cref{swo:sw:permutationeta} (the order is $\eta_\kappa$, and all its global
and local cofinalities and coinitialities equal $\kappa$). Question~4 is
source~12's \cref{swo:st:n14.1}. The others remain open.
