\section{The sources' conclusions}\label{swo:sec:conclusions}

\merge\ Source~11 has no conclusion section; its summary is
\cref{swo:sec:status}. The conclusions of sources~12, 09 and~08 are printed
whole.

% ---- source 12, lines 1551-1577
\subsection{Source 12's conclusion}

There is a rigorous full lexicographic order of all global surreal
well-orders, provided it is understood as a relation on class objects,
not as an ordinary class containing those objects. Its comparison needs
no universal enumeration of abstract class order types: the largest
common \emph{labelled} prefix is elementary and sufficient.

The set-like suborder has a particularly uniform geometry. It fills
set-indexed cuts, contains every class linear order even locally, and is
equimorphic with binary classes on $\Ord$. The unrestricted order adds
new proper-class prefix phenomena, including finite adjacent blocks.
The diagonal theorem prevents either construction from being exhausted
by a same-level class of set codes.

At set-sized birthday bounds the theory becomes quantitative. The full
well-order space of a carrier of cardinality $\mu$ has exact ordinal
spectrum below $\mu^+$ and binary rank $\mu^+$. The minimal surreal slice
has rank $\mu$, except at singular strong-limit cutoffs, where it has
rank $\kappa\cdot\kappa$. This is an order-theoretic effect of cofinal
blocks of code lengths, invisible to cardinal counting alone.

The inaccessible-rank interpretation shows exactly how these facts can
coexist: internal classes and the external collection of all such
classes occupy different levels. Respecting those levels removes the
apparent paradox without weakening the embedding and universality
questions into trivialities.

% ---- source 09, lines 1524-1546
\subsection{Source 09's conclusions}
The lexicographic well-ordering problem has three different answers,
depending on its carrier. On a fixed set of surreals of size $\kappa$,
the exact ordinal spectrum stops at $\kappa^+$. Allowing arbitrary
set-length words gives a class-universal dictionary order, but prefix
limits give it local order properties different from those of $\No$.
For set-like global well-orders, first disagreement again defines a
linear comparison. Existence of a global enumeration is equivalent to
global choice over $\GB$ with set choice, and all global enumerations
cannot be collected into an ordinary class of set codes.

The eventual-agreement core resolves the constructive side of the
global problem: it is a genuine class, it is isomorphic to the surreal
line, it realizes every uniformly set-sized cut of global enumerations,
and it is dense in every global interval. Bounded reflection explains
why set-indexed configurations can always be studied in a suitable
bounded permutation stage. The explicit missing branch and binary
diagonal identify two independent boundaries: arbitrary class-sized
cuts need not have solutions, and no single uniform class-indexed
coding covers all global enumerations.

These boundaries are not paradoxes. They are the information needed to
state theorems at the correct logical level.

% ---- source 08, lines 1818-1843
\subsection{Source 08's conclusion}

The global enumeration problem has a particularly clean answer once
the types of objects are separated. Set-like well-orders of $\No$ are
represented by class bijections from $\Ord$, and numerical
first-disagreement gives a rigorous virtual linear order. That order
is large enough to encode every class binary word, yet its set-cut
geometry is already witnessed by a single class of set-supported
perturbations. This code class is another copy of the surreal line.

At a fixed set carrier, the structure is different: its exact ordinal
spectrum stops at the successor of the carrier cardinal, and finite
permutation tails account for every jump. At varying set carriers,
termination introduces new local cofinalities. For non-set-like
well-orders, a further index convention or relation code is necessary.
None of these distinctions is optional bookkeeping: each changes a
mathematical conclusion.

The principal proofs are elementary in their stated foundations.
Their formalization should begin with bounded objects and the
repository's existing sign-sequence cut theorem, and only then pass to
the uniform class formulation. The dense-skeleton theorem provides a
concrete target: it combines first-disagreement combinatorics,
controlled set support, small-cut filling, and universe-aware
back-and-forth without requiring a paradoxical collection of all
classes.

% ---- source 11, lines 2598-2658
\section{Results, assumptions, and verification status}
\label{swo:sec:status}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.54\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Result & Foundation used here\\
\midrule
Fixed-alphabet cardinality and ordinal spectrum &
\(\ZFC\); existing repository theorem\\[3pt]
Explicit set-length word compression &
\(\ZF\), as a definable class construction\\[3pt]
Local word characters and set-cut arguments &
Surreal set cuts; \(\ZFC\) for cardinal-valued characters\\[3pt]
Any class well-order of \(\No\) implies global choice &
\(\GB\) with \(\ZF\) set part\\[3pt]
Raw comparison, canonical initial part, finite-jump criterion &
\(\GB\), with the given class orders\\[3pt]
Bounded-support completion and interpolation &
\(\GBC\), with a fixed global enumeration\\[3pt]
\(\Pbd\cong\No\) and class-order universality &
\(\GBC\), by set-valued back-and-forth\\[3pt]
Binary class coding and no exhaustive uniform evaluator &
\(\GBC\), interpreted with class parameters\\[3pt]
Unrestricted order does not embed into its set-like fragment &
Full inaccessible universe; or \(\KM+\GC\) for definable uniform maps\\[3pt]
All higher-order collections treated as actual objects &
An explicitly chosen higher-order or containing-universe setting\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The proof dependencies are short. Set cuts yield fresh
coefficients after deleting a set. Fresh coefficients and
bounded-support completion yield strong interpolation.
Strong interpolation yields density, local cofinality
statements, the \(\No\)-isomorphism, and cut coding.
Independently, the predecessor comparison yields convex
cones, the canonical initial-part decomposition, and the
finite-residual jump theorem. Pair swaps supply both
positive binary embeddings and the diagonal obstruction.

The bundled Python script checks finite instances of
the two comparison rules and of the sign encoding. It
checks 465 coefficient-code comparisons, 79,800
word-code comparisons, and 266,272 pairs of finite
permutations, with the predecessor rule checked in both
directions and the adjacency criterion compared with the
finite lexicographic order. These checks help detect a
reversed delimiter or an incorrect finite comparison.
They do not verify an ordinal induction, a class
comprehension argument, or a cardinal nonembedding
theorem.

The article contains full mathematical proofs for the
claims it promotes to theorems. It does not claim that
the new results have been checked in Lean, that the
whole repository has been audited, or that every
research question listed here is a previously published
open problem.

\subsection{Source 12: reproducibility and inspection record}

The package contains \texttt{article.tex}, the compiled
\texttt{article.pdf}, a build script, an exact finite-check program,

results. The PDF was compiled and rendered for visual inspection.

well-orders; it was not sufficient to certify historical originality.

\subsection{Source 09: verification and reproducibility}
\label{swo:ec:app:verification}
The accompanying \texttt{code/finite\_checks.py} uses only Python's
standard library. It checks finite versions of the pair-switch
lexicographic embedding, partial-prefix permutation completion, the
forced-prefix separator algorithm, support extension preserving first
disagreements, and the diagonal switch construction. Exact rational
arithmetic is used for the separator tests. A deterministic random seed
makes the sampled checks reproducible; other suites exhaust their
specified finite ranges.

\textbf{These checks do not verify any transfinite theorem.} Their role
is to detect indexing, orientation, and extension mistakes in the
finite combinatorial mechanisms. Proofs of the infinite statements
are the written arguments in the article. The recorded run passed 164,560 assertions, including 1,101 separator-family
cases. The test report is \texttt{data/finite\_checks.json}. The archive also contains a build
script and a theorem-status ledger.

To compile the article, run \texttt{bash build.sh} in the archive's root
directory. It makes three passes with \texttt{pdflatex}, resolves the
internal bibliography and cross-references, and writes build products
into \texttt{build/} before copying the finished PDF to the root.

% ---- source 08, lines 1874-1906
\subsection{Source 08: finite regression checks}\label{swo:sk:app:tests}

The package includes \texttt{verify\_finite.py}, requiring Python 3.10
or later and only its standard library. Running it writes
\texttt{verification\_results.json}. The supplied run passed:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}Y r@{}}
\toprule
Check & Instances \\
\midrule
Consecutive permutation pairs, sizes $1$ through $7$ & 5,906 \\
All increasing pairs of distinct permutations, sizes through $5$ & 7,432 \\
Pair-code order comparisons, bit lengths $0$ through $7$ & 21,845 \\
Sign words of lengths $0$ through $6$ & 127 \\
All sign-code order and prefix-free comparisons & 16,129 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The permutation test checks both directions of the adjacency rule on
all manageable pairs, not just a few positive examples. The sign test
compares the numerical convention $-<0<+$ with the binary blocks
$00<01<11$, including pairs in which one sign word is a proper prefix.
The pair-code test checks that every output is a permutation of the
correct finite alphabet and that comparisons agree with the input
binary order.

These tests can detect a reversed pair orientation, a wrong terminal
block, or a mistaken finite-tail rule. They cannot prove regularity
arguments, transfinite stabilization, set/class separation, or the
existence of a global isomorphism. Those depend on the mathematical
proofs in the text.

\section{The other sources' title pages and abstracts}\label{swo:app:abstracts}

\merge\ The results these summaries announce are printed in the body; the
summaries are kept for the record.

\subsection*{Source 12}
\paragraph{Title-page summary.}

% ---- source 12, lines 86-111
\textbf{Main distinction.} An arbitrary class well-order of $\No$ need not
have order type $\Ord$. Nevertheless, two such well-orders have an
\emph{elementarily definable largest common labelled initial segment},
possibly a proper class. This supplies a lexicographic comparison in GBC
without invoking general class well-order comparability or ETR.

\medskip
\textbf{Principal quantitative result.} Put
$\No_{<\kappa}=\{s:\alpha\to\{-,+\}:\alpha<\kappa\}$ and
$\mu=|\No_{<\kappa}|=2^{<\kappa}$, for an infinite initial cardinal $\kappa$.
The minimal-length permutation order satisfies
\[
\WO_\mu(\No_{<\kappa})\equim
\begin{cases}
\BB_{\kappa\cdot\kappa},&\mu=\kappa\text{ and }\cf\kappa<\kappa,\\
\BB_\mu,&\text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\]
where $\BB_\gamma=(2^\gamma,<_{\lex})$ and $\equim$ means mutual
order-embeddability, not isomorphism.

\medskip
\textbf{Status.} This is an AI-assisted, unrefereed research manuscript.
Proofs are supplied for the stated results; historical priority is not
claimed. The accompanying exact finite tests do not verify the transfinite
arguments. No new Lean formalization is claimed.

% ---- hand chunk abs12_mid

\paragraph{Abstract.}

% ---- source 12, lines 117-143
We study the lexicographic order of well-orderings of the surreal numbers,
carefully distinguishing bounded sets of surreals, set-like global
well-orders, and arbitrary global class well-orders. For the full class
problem, ordinal-indexed enumerations are insufficient. We construct the
largest common labelled initial segment of two well-orders directly from
their relations, prove that comparison of their next elements defines a
linear order, and show that this construction needs only elementary class
comprehension once the well-orders exist. Over GB with set choice, the
existence of any class well-order of the surreals is equivalent to global
choice. The collection of all these well-orders is not itself an ordinary
class of GBC or Kelley--Morse theory; a diagonal theorem rules out any
uniform exhaustive coding by a class of sets.

For set-like global well-orders we prove density, interpolation for
set-indexed cuts, local universality for class linear orders, and mutual
embeddability with the lexicographic comparison of binary classes on
$\Ord$. For unrestricted global well-orders we classify adjacent pairs:
they arise precisely from finite residual permutations after a common,
necessarily proper-class, prefix. At a birthday cutoff $\kappa$, we obtain
an exact regular/singular dichotomy for the binary coding rank of
minimal-length enumerations. We also establish the exact ordinal and
binary-cube embedding spectra for the full set-sized order. Finally, a
full-class model based on an inaccessible rank explains exactly how the
bounded and global theories fit together, and provides a rigorous example
in which the full order and the set-like order have the same cardinality
and ordinal spectrum but different coding ranks. Explicit proof-assistant
interfaces and further research questions are included.

% ---- hand chunk abs09_head

\subsection*{Source 09}
\paragraph{Abstract.}

% ---- source 09, lines 98-124
The phrase ``the lexicographic order of all well-orders of the surreal
numbers'' has several mathematically different interpretations. We develop
three: exhaustive enumerations of a fixed set of surreals; all set-length
injective surreal words; and class bijections from the ordinals onto the
surreal numbers, representing set-like global well-orders. For an infinite
set $X$ of cardinality $\kappa$, we give a self-contained proof that its
lexicographic well-ordering space has cardinality $2^\kappa$, contains every
linear order of size at most $\kappa$, and contains an ordinal precisely when
that ordinal is below $\kappa^+$.

For the global problem, we work in G\"odel--Bernays set theory with global
choice and do not pretend that a collection of proper classes is itself an
ordinary class. Fix a global enumeration $b$. Its eventually-equal
perturbations admit canonical set codes. We prove that their lexicographic
order is isomorphic to the surreal line, meets every set-prefix cylinder,
and interpolates every uniformly set-indexed separated pair of families
of arbitrary global enumerations. Thus every class linear order embeds
into this genuine class core. We also prove bounded reflection for
set-indexed families, construct an explicit unfilled
$(\Ord,\Ord)$-configuration, and diagonalize against every uniform
class-indexed list of all global enumerations.

A strongly inaccessible truncation gives a fully set-sized model of the
size distinction: the core has cardinality $\kappa$, whereas all length
$\kappa$ enumerations have cardinality $2^\kappa$. The article includes
foundational and Lean formalization plans, a repository audit, finite
verification code, and a focused research agenda.

% ---- hand chunk abs09_mid

\paragraph{Title-page status.}

% ---- source 09, lines 127-131
{\small\color{Gray}\noindent
Status: AI-assisted research manuscript; complete written arguments are
provided for the stated results. Not peer-reviewed or Lean-verified.
Classical ingredients and repository results are distinguished from the
constructions developed here. No claim of historical priority is made.}

% ---- hand chunk abs08_head

\subsection*{Source 08}
\paragraph{Abstract.}

% ---- source 08, lines 90-113
There is no single unqualified object called ``the lexicographic order of
all well-orders of the surreal numbers.'' We distinguish well-orderings of
a fixed set of surreals, well-orderings of varying set-sized subsets,
set-like well-orderings of the whole surreal class, and arbitrary class
well-orderings. For an infinite ordered set $X$ of cardinality $\kappa$, the
exhaustive-enumeration order $\W(X)$ has size $2^\kappa$, contains every
linear order of size at most $\kappa$, and contains an ordinal $\beta$
exactly when $\beta<\kappa^+$. Its adjacent pairs arise precisely from
finite permutation tails.

For the whole surreal class, work in G\"odel--Bernays set theory with
Global Choice. The numerical first-disagreement comparison of class
bijections $\Ord\to\No$ is meaningful without treating classes as elements
of classes. Fix one such bijection $b$. We prove that every set-sized
separated family of global enumerations has a strict separator obtained
from $b$ by a permutation of set support. The class of canonical codes for
these perturbations is therefore order-isomorphic to $\No$. It is a dense,
set-cut-realizing skeleton inside the much larger virtual enumeration
order, and every class linear order embeds into it. The full virtual order
is equimorphic with the lexicographic order of class binary sequences, but
admits no class-uniform exhaustive parametrization. We supply proofs,
cutoff versions, counterexamples to tempting identifications, a
universe-sensitive Lean implementation plan tied to inspected ProveIt
sources, and a research agenda.

% ---- hand chunk abs08_mid

\paragraph{Status box.}

% ---- source 08, lines 115-123
\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status and scope.} The results below are mathematical derivations
with explicit proofs, not claims of historical priority. The manuscript
has not been independently refereed, and its new results have not been
checked by a proof assistant. Repository declarations are identified
separately from proposed extensions. The accompanying executable checks
cover finite coding and adjacency examples only. All uses of ``class''
are relative to the stated foundational model.
\end{resultbox}
